# Description of Liturgical Manuscripts Preserved in the Libraries of the Orthodox East (*Описаніе литургическихъ рукописей, хранящихся въ библіотекахъ православнаго Востока*)

### Volume I: Typika (*Τυπικά*)

#### Part One: Monuments of Patriarchal Rubrics and Ktetorik Monastic Typika (*Памятники патріаршихъ уставовъ и ктиторскіе монастырскіе Типиконы*)

By Aleksei Dmitrievsky, Docent of the Kyiv Theological Academy

Kyiv  
Typography of G. T. Korchak-Novytsky, Mykhailivska St., No. 4  
1895

=== LEAF p4 ===

## Author's Preface (*Предисловіе*)

*(Orig. p. I)*

The Church Ordo or Typikon, which in ancient times among us was termed *Tserkovne Oko* (lit. 'Eye of the Church', *«Око церковное»*), occupies the preeminent place within the cycle of liturgical books of the Orthodox Church. Its historical destiny is bound up in a close, indissoluble connection with the history of Orthodox liturgical worship in general, and for this reason it is not in the least surprising that the history of the Church Ordo has from antiquity served as a subject of scholarly research for Western investigators and for our Russian scholars alike. Apart from the well-known dissertation: *De libris et rebus ecclesiasticis Graecorum dissertationes*, Paris 1646, pp. 3–16, by the renowned 16th-century scholar Leo Allatius, who devoted a brief sketch to this book, we also possess in the West a specialized monograph on this subject, belonging to the learned librarian and hieromonk of the Grottaferrata Monastery Theodore Toscani under the title: *Ad Typica Graecorum ac praesertim ad typicum cryptoferratense S. Bartholomaei abbatis animadversiones*, Rome 1864. In our Russian literature, there appeared in the most recent time on the very same question the extensive work of the late talented professor of the Moscow Theological Academy I. D. Mansvetov: *The Church Ordo (Typikon), its Formation and Destiny in the Greek and Russian Church* (*«Церковный уставъ (Типикъ) его образованіе и судьба въ греческой и русской церкви»*), Moscow 1885. In addition, we possess no small number of brief journal articles, notes, and sketches that in one way or another touch upon the historical destiny of our Church Ordo, proceeding from the pen of prominent representatives of our scholarly thought, such as, for instance, Bishop Porphyrius Uspensky, Archimandrite Antonin, Archbishop Sergius of Vladimir (author of *The Menologion of the East* [*«Мѣсяцесловъ востока»*], Moscow 1876), and others; yet of a complete, systematic, and scientifically elaborated history of this liturgical book based on a comprehensive study of primary sources—we do not even now possess.

Leo Allatius intended in his book, which embraces the history of all the liturgical books of the Greek Church and certain other ecclesiastical subjects, to provide only a general sketch, without entering...

=== LEAF p5 ===

*(Orig. p. II)*

...into detailed examinations of all the questions touching upon the destiny of the Greek Church Ordo, for which, the truth must be told, he was scarcely fully prepared. The subsequent Western scholar, Father Toscani, was in comparatively better circumstances; however, as the very title of his work demonstrates, he set as his primary objective to familiarize readers with the Typikon of St. Bartholomew, the founder of the Grottaferrata Monastery, and speaks of the Greek Typika only to the extent that it was necessary for him in order to indicate the connection of the Typikon of St. Bartholomew with them. One cannot in any way demand a detailed history of the Church Ordo from Bishop Porphyrius, Archimandrite Antonin, Archbishop Sergius, and others, who touched upon the given question only incidentally and in passing. Finally, Professor I. D. Mansvetov also failed to provide such a history of the Church Ordo, from whom, judging by the title of his book, one might most of all have expected it. The reason, as the author candidly confesses in his "preface," lies in the fact that he began to write his history with merely "a dozen Greek copies" of the Typikon from the Moscow book depositories, and moreover, it must be added, all these copies belonged to one and the same recension. Professor Mansvetov "remained unaware of the materials for the history of the Greek Ordo in its most ancient epoch—in the transitional period from brief notes to the formation of full written expositions thereof." But in reality, much also remained unknown to the late professor about which he did not even suspect in his time.

This is why we, when departing on a foreign research mission authorized by the supreme authorities in the 1887–88 academic year, and subsequently, during repeated excursions through the East and West on our own initiative, set for ourselves two tasks with respect to our Church Ordo: 1) to describe and study as fully and thoroughly as possible those Greek manuscripts that in one way or another relate to the Church Ordo and elucidate for us the historical past and current composition of this book, with primary attention to those manuscripts that concern its history in the most ancient epoch of the Christian Church, when our Church Ordo was only just being forged into a code of rules regulating the order...

=== LEAF p6 ===

*(Orig. p. III)*

...of Orthodox worship, and when written records of Ordines or Typika in the present-day sense of this word did not yet exist; 2) to verify by personal inspection all those manuscripts and data for the history of the Church Ordo that had been published and brought into God's light from the book depositories of the East and West by previous travelers and scholars, in order to be able to judge fully independently and without prejudice both of the monuments themselves and of their scholarly value for the history of this liturgical book. The results in all respects, we state without self-delusion, surpassed our expectations. There accumulated in our hands, in this manner, much new, highly valuable, and curious material for the history of our Church Ordo, which it is at present utterly impossible to enclose within the bounds of a single book, even an extensive one. In view of this, we resolved to postpone the publication of the extensive materials relating to the historical destiny of the Jerusalem Typikon (linked in its origin with the lavra of St. Sabbas the Sanctified) and the Holy Mountain Athonite Typikon to the second part of this volume, and to dedicate the present first part exclusively to the materials of two divisions from the most ancient history of the Church Typikon: *the monuments of patriarchal rubrics and ktetorik monastic Typika*. The following reasons, in our view entirely valid, served to move us to grant an independent and prominent place in the edition we have undertaken to the materials of these two divisions.

Above we noted that to our predecessors "the materials for the history of the Greek Ordo in its most ancient epoch remained unknown—in the transitional period from brief notes to the formation of full written expositions thereof." The monuments that we now publish in the present first part of our *Description* were sought out and studied by us primarily with the aim of filling this essential deficiency. Certain of these monuments, such as, for instance, the Patmos manuscript of the Typikon of the Great Church, the Athens manuscript of the Evergetis Typikon, and others, contain an integral systematic exposition of the divine services of the Byzantine Church for the entire year, and precisely in its most ancient and most important...

=== LEAF p7 ===

*(Orig. p. IV)*

...epoch of formation. Consequently, the scientific liturgical importance of the given materials is beyond all doubt. This is on the one hand. On the other hand, both the monuments of patriarchal rubrics—or the monuments of the ordo of the Great Church of Constantinople [^1] in particular, whether these be complete manuscripts (such as, for example, the Patmos manuscript No. 266 published by us), or brief extracts from the Gospels, Apostolos, Prophetologia, and other liturgical books of the most ancient epoch (such as, for example, the notes and additions to the Patmos manuscript published by us)—and likewise the ktetorik Byzantine Typika up to the 13th century inclusive, possess, beyond their specialized liturgical significance, an immense scientific interest in general. In monuments of this kind we discover extensive historical material elucidating for us the fortunes of very many of the most ancient and renowned monasteries, temples, and other monumental structures, chiefly of Constantinople and its environs, concerning which either no information whatever existed in historical literature, or else this information was fragmentary and inaccurate. Here, furthermore, we draw diverse data on hagiology, topography, archaeology, and the history of culture and religious life in ancient Byzantium. This is the reason why the majority of the monuments are printed by us almost in their entirety, with very insignificant omissions, which will be noted in their proper place below. The important scientific significance of the present monuments in this respect was recognized long ago, and their publication was undertaken by such scholars of the West, the East, and our own Russian scholars, as *Montfaucon* (the Typikon of Empress Irene of 1114), *Sathas* (the Ordo of 1077 of the Byzantine grandee Michael Attaleiates), Professors *Miklosich and Müller* (*Acta et diplomata monasteriorum et ecclesiarum orientis*, vol. II, Vienna 1887), *Georgius Musaeus* (Γρηγόριος Πακουριανός, μέγας δομέστικος τῆς δύσεως καὶ τὸ ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ Τυπικὸν τῆς μονῆς τῆς Θεοτόκου τῆς Πετριτζονιτίσσης, Leipzig 1888), Hierodeacon *Kyrillos Voines* (the Typikon of the Patmos Monastery of St. John the Theologian...

=== LEAF p8 ===

*(Orig. p. V)*

...given in the 12th century by St. Christodoulos, printed in Ἀκολουθία ἱερὰ τοῦ Χριστοδούλου, Athens 1884), Professor *I. E. Troitsky* (*Autobiography of Emperor Michael Palaeologus and an Extract from the Ordo Given by Him to the Monastery of St. Demetrius*, St. Petersburg 1885), Professor *I. D. Mansvetov* (*The Decrees of the Monastery of Our Holy Father John the Presbyter, Hegumen of Patellaria*; see *Typikon*, Moscow 1885, pp. 442–445), *Daničić* (*Typikon of the Hilandar Monastery of St. Sabbas of Serbia* in *Glasnik*, Book XX), *J. Jireček* (*Typikon of the Studenica Monastery of the Same Sabbas* in *Glasnik*, Book XL), and others. The liturgical monuments now published by us, appearing in print for the first time, not only stand no lower in interest than those already known to scholarship, but in the majority significantly surpass the latter (such as, for instance, the Pantokrator and Evergetis Typika, and others) and, consequently, fully justify our approach to them in the present case.

Let us now communicate concerning each of the monuments that constitute the content of the present first part of our *Description* the bibliographical and other necessary information, in order to present more vividly to our readers the scientific significance of each of them individually.

I. The first division of our *Description* opens with the publication of the *Typikon of the Great Church of Constantinople*, extracted by us from a parchment manuscript of the 10th century that has been preserved in the Patmos Library from a very ancient period [^2], but which became known to us only after the appearance of the book of the late I. Sakkelion, *Patmiake Bibliotheke* (*Πατμιακὴ βιβλιοθήκη*, Athens 1890), who described it very briefly (p. 136). To the present monument we have assigned the first place because hitherto there was no complete Ordo of the Great Church known to scholarship, and all information concerning the peculiarities of this practice we drew partly from the book *De ceremoniis aulae Byzanti-...

=== LEAF p9 ===

*(Orig. p. VI)*

...nae*, attributed to Emperor Constantine Porphyrogenitus, partly from the writings of the 14th-century liturgist Symeon of Thessalonica, and partly, finally, from the *Taktikon* of Nikon of the Black Mountain, an 11th-century writer. Only in the most recent time have our scholars turned for material of this kind to the synaxaria attached to Apostolos and Gospel lectionaries [^3], written in Byzantium in the most ancient era. It is self-evident that the manuscript of the Patmos Library, as the firstborn of its kind, deserves the full attention of those who are interested for whatever reason in the state of liturgical worship in the ancient Byzantine Church. In it we possess a sequential—though brief and in places fragmentary—exposition of the services of the entire church year, that is, according to the Menaia and the Lenten and Flowery Triodia. The scientific interest of this highly precious and curious monument in other respects is presented with sufficient fullness and thoroughness by Professor N. Th. Krasnoseltsev of Novorossiysk University, to whom the contents of the present manuscript became known chiefly through a copy prepared for the present edition, in a solid specialized monograph under the title: *The Typikon of St. Sophia in Constantinople of the 9th Century* (*«Типикъ св. Софіи въ Константинополѣ IX в.»*), Odessa 1892.

The brevity of exposition of the present Typikon accounts for the circumstance that, in editing it, we resolved (we regret that this thought did not occur to us from the very beginning) to supplement its content in the footnotes, and subsequently in the "supplementary" notes, with that information from the practice of the Great Church of Constantinople which we extracted from Eastern manuscripts—namely, Apostolos, Gospels, Prophetologia, and others. Thanks to these "supplementary" data, the practice of the Great Church can be studied with greater thoroughness, and the data themselves are presented to the readers in an unscattered form, given their occurrence in manuscripts of the most diverse...

=== LEAF p10 ===

*(Orig. p. VII)*

...content, yet arranged according to the order of the church year. Certain of such additions have been inserted with an explanatory note directly into the text of the monument, since they successfully supply the extant defects of the given manuscript. As such an addition we regard the insertion in the description of the rite of the Elevation of the Precious Cross in the service of September 14, borrowed by us from a parchment Apostolos of the 10th–11th century, No. 12 of the Lavra of St. Athanasius on Mount Athos (p. 5).

The Patmos manuscript No. 266, containing within itself the Ordo of the Great Church of Constantinople, is written in the very careless hand of an illiterate scribe (*ὑπ’ ἀмаθοῦς γραφέως*), as the late I. Sakkelion expressed it, and is therefore overfilled with a multitude of orthographical errors that severely impede the reading of its text. Hence the methods that we were forced to adopt when copying and publishing the present manuscript become understandable. Wishing to lighten our labor during transcription, we abbreviated the texts of Holy Scripture that are well known at present and stand in the manuscript as prokeimena or alleluia verses, and we did not transcribe the opening words of the apostolic and gospel readings, nor the verses of the communion hymn, if these readings and communion hymns proved identical to those indicated at present in the Typikon currently in force. Since, by reason of the scribe's illiteracy, proper names were distorted most of all—that is, the names of saints, emperors, governors, and the names of cities, localities, rivers, and the like—therefore, in order to make the text of the published manuscript fully intelligible to readers, we resolved to adhere to the spelling of these names found in a monument contemporary with the given manuscript, in the well-known *Menologium Graecorum Basilii Porphyrogeniti imperatoris* (Migne, *Patrologia Graeca*, vol. 117). In certain cases we had recourse for the same purpose to the *Μηноλόγιον τῶν εὐαγγελίων ἑορταστικόν, sive Kalendarium ecclesiae Constantinopolitanae Steph. Anton. Morcelli*, Rome 1788, in two volumes, and sometimes even to the book of Nicodemus the Hagiorite: *Συναξαριστὴς τῶν δώδεκα μηνῶν τοῦ ἐνιαυτοῦ*, publ. in Zakynthos 1868, in three volumes. But even with all these aids, it must be candidly confessed, by no means all the orthographical and paleographical difficulties of the manuscript being described were overcome by us. In the edition of the monument itself one can not infrequently encounter question...

<!-- END COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort1 -->

---

<!-- START COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort2 -->

=== LEAF p11 ===

*(Orig. p. VIII)*

...-marks, and that not only opposite proper names, but even opposite ordinary words of the manuscript's text that either yield no sense whatever, or else whose meaning is not entirely clear and intelligible.

II. Typika of the Jerusalem recension usually begin with an exposition of the daily cycle of services, whereas the sole copy of the Ordo of the Great Church known thus far says nothing of these services, and a detailed exposition of their order according to this ordo was hitherto unknown to scholarship. Desiring to supply the indicated deficiency, we resolved to print, according to a 15th-century manuscript of the library of the Russian St. Andrew Skete on Mount Athos, the order of these services under the following title: "Order observed in the Great Church both at Vespers, at Matins, at the Divine and sacred Liturgy, and at the ordination of a bishop" (*«Τάξις, γινομένη ἐν τῇ μεγάλῃ ἐκκλησίᾳ ἐπί τε τῷ ἑσπερινῷ, τῷ ὄρθρῳ, τῇ θείᾳ καὶ ἱερᾷ λειτουργίᾳ καὶ ἐπὶ τῇ χειροτονίᾳ τοῦ ἐπισκόπου»*) [^4]. The order of the daily services published by us we have supplemented in the footnotes with variant readings borrowed chiefly from the Athos Vatopedi manuscript No. 297 (931) under the title: *Papadike Techne* (*«Παπαδικὴ τέχνη»*).

III. Serving as the conclusion of the first division is the Sinai Kanonarion of the 9th–11th centuries, already well known to scholarship, under the title: "Kanonarion, with God, beginning from Holy Pascha until Holy Saturday, and from the month of September until the month of August" (*«Κανονάριον σὺν Θεῷ, ἀρχόμενον ἀπὸ τὸ ἅγιον Πάσχα μέχρι τοῦ ἁγίου σαββάτου, καὶ ἀπὸ μηνὸς Σεπτεμβρίου μέχρι μηνὸς Αὐγούστου»*). This Kanonarion, according to manuscript No. 150 of the 10th–11th century of the Sinai Library (from a complete Gospel lectionary) and according to fragments of the 9th–10th century found in the rubbish there, was for the first time published in the Russian language by the late Archimandrite Antonin. Although the learned translator stated in the preface that he was rendering this Kanonarion "in an accurate translation into the Russian language" [^5], nevertheless, upon consultation with the originals, it turns out that this Kanonarion not only cannot be regarded as "an as accurate as possible translation" [^6], but not even as a complete exposition of their contents, since the edi-...

=== LEAF p12 ===

*(Orig. p. IX)*

...tor did not carry the exposition of commemorations through the entire two final months—July and August—according to copy B, halting at the 6th day of the month of August, where the Kanonarion of the 9th–10th century according to copy A breaks off. "At the present time," Father Antonin remarks, "I cannot account to myself as to why, in compiling the Kanonarion from two copies or distinct recensions, I halted where Kanonarion A (the most ancient) ended, and did not continue to trace it to the end of the month according to Kanonarion B. I think (?) that the text of the latter ceased even earlier than Kanonarion B, for throughout the entire month of July not once is reference made in my notes to Kanonarion B" [^7]. In actual fact, however, it turns out that Father Antonin for some reason ignored the months of July and August according to copy B, where there exist certain commemorations not noted by him in his composite Kanonarion: such as, for instance, under July 16, SS. Antiochus, Anna, and Cyriacus; under July 26, Symeon the Stylite and the Great Martyr of Jerusalem; and under July 27, Therapon (p. 219); while in the month of August after the 6th day there are further commemorations assigned under the 11th, 14th, 15th, 29th, and 31st days (pp. 220–221).

The Sinai Kanonarion contains not only very abundant hagiological material, but is also filled with exceedingly curious and extensive observations of a liturgical character from the practice of the ancient Byzantine Church, which, although they interested the editor, he proved powerless to explain, and for this reason was compelled to restrict himself to perplexities and questions [^8] concerning them. Archimandrite (now Archbishop of Vladimir) Sergius, who made use of the hagiological material of this Kanonarion in his fundamental work: *The Complete Menologion of the East* (*«Полный мѣсяцесловъ востока»*), Moscow 1875, noticed the "omissions" indicated by us with respect to his copy B in Archimandrite Antonin, and expressed the opinion that, "judging by the abundance of Western saints in it," there is evident "no small Roman influence" upon this Kanonarion [^9]. Professor I. D. Mansvetov, having in view the aforementioned extensive extracts from the Church Ordo, con-...

=== LEAF p13 ===

*(Orig. p. X)*

...cerning that same monument, stated that "the basis of the rite in the Sinai Kanonarion is identical with the Studite record, but in particulars diverges from it, and in certain details of the festive service points to another source and to other liturgical orders. The Sinai record was designated not for monastic use, but for services in secular churches" [^10].

Upon closer acquaintance, however, with the character of the liturgical peculiarities of the Sinai Kanonarion and the commemorations of saints contained in it, and comparing this monument with the Typikon of the Great Church, which is printed by us under No. 1 according to Patmos manuscript No. 266, there remains no doubt whatever that we are dealing here with monuments of the ordo of the Great Church of Constantinople. The peculiarities in the service of the Elevation of the Cross (p. 196, cf. p. 5), on the Eve of the Nativity of Christ (p. 206, cf. p. 35), in the services of Great Lent (pp. 185–190, cf. pp. 110–127), Passion Week (pp. 191–193, cf. pp. 127–135), and Paschal week (pp. 173–177, cf. pp. 135–141), with few insignificant peculiarities, are the very same as those we see in the Typikon of the Great Church, while in places in both monuments there is even observed identity in exposition (p. 196, cf. p. 5; p. 187, cf. p. 118; pp. 191–193, cf. pp. 131–134, etc.). As for what concerns in particular the commemorations of saints, which gave Archimandrite Sergius cause to see in the present monument the influence of a Western calendar [^11], after the indicated comparison it becomes evident that those same commemorations can also be found in the menologia of the Orthodox East. That is why we resolved to place the Sinai Kanonaria in the first division immediately following the Typikon of the Great Church of Constantinople.

In editing the Sinai Kanonarion, we adhered to the following method. As its basis we laid the later copy of the 10th–11th century, found with the Gospel lectionary No. 150, as a complete copy and one well preserved in the manuscript. The other copy of this Kanonarion of the same library, but of a more ancient time, the 9th–...

=== LEAF p14 ===

*(Orig. p. XI)*

...10th century, we have printed throughout in the footnotes to the first, noting in the most meticulous manner all the extant variant readings, so that those interested in monuments of this kind might possess accurate and clear conceptions concerning what is contained in both copies.

The second division of the first part of our *Description* we dedicate to the so-called "ktetorik Typika" (*Τυπικὰ κτητορικά*), which in the period of time from the 9th to the 13th century were, one may say, almost the sole regulators of liturgical worship and the internal order of life in Byzantine monasteries, built in vast numbers by emperors, empresses, patriarchs, and noble grandees. Inasmuch as the "ktetorik Typika" developed out of those spiritual testaments (*διαθήκη*, *ὑποτύπωσις*, *διατύπωσις*, etc.) that their founders or ktetors bequeathed to monasteries, and which in frequent cases took the form of a genuine rule, intended, after the ktetor's death, to guide the brethren not only in daily monastic life, but also in the celebration of church services in the monastery, we therefore begin this division of monuments with the *testaments* of hegumens, and then proceed directly to "ktetorik Typika" in the proper sense.

IV. The most important and most ancient of the testaments must be considered the "rules" (*τὰς παραδόσεις*) that by St. Sabbas the Sanctified, the founder near Jerusalem of the "lavra bearing his name," were "introduced into the monasteries" founded by him, and prior to his death "delivered in writing" (*δοὺς ταύτας ἐγγράφως*) to his disciple and successor in the hegumenate, Melitas [^12]. These rules, termed at times also the "ancient law" (*παλαιὸς νόμος*) [^13], were regarded in scholarship as lost until recent times, and concerning their contents scholars made various kinds of suppositions and conjectures. Fortu-...

=== LEAF p15 ===

*(Orig. p. XII)*

...nate chance helped us discover these rules in the Greek original in two manuscripts of the Sinai Library, No. 1096 of the 12th–13th century and No. 531 of the 15th century, according to which we now print them in our edition. All bibliographical details concerning this precious monument and the proofs regarding their authenticity and attribution to St. Sabbas the Sanctified are set forth by us in a specialized article: "The Cenobitic Rules of St. Sabbas the Sanctified, Delivered by Him before His Demise to His Successor, Hegumen Melitas," which is placed as the second appendix to the report on our foreign scholarly mission in the 1887–88 academic year under the title: *A Journey through the East and its Scientific Results* (*«Путешествіе по востоку и его научные результаты»*), Kyiv 1890, pp. 171–193. All the information set forth by us in that article, albeit briefly, and the text of the rules in its entirety—with certain corrections, however, which we now fully share and note at the end of the present book among the errata additions and corrections—were reprinted by E. Kurtz in the journal *Byzantinische Zeitschrift*, 1894, Vol. III, Issue I, pp. 167–170.

V. The Studite Monastery, founded in the 6th century by the patrician Studios (*μοναστήριον Στουδίου*), held in Constantinople precisely the same significance as the monastery of St. Sabbas the Sanctified in Palestine. At present, two expositions of the liturgical orders and strict disciplinary rules of the monastic life of this monastery are known to scholarship: in the *Hypotyposis* (*Ὑποτύπωσις*) and in the Typikon of Patriarch Alexius (1025–1043), and both of them connect the orders they describe with the personality and authority of the renowned champion of Orthodoxy, church poet, and preacher, Theodore the Studite († 826), hegumen of this monastery. "Many and varied traditions (*παραδόσεων*) have taken root in venerable monasteries from ancient times," says the anonymous author of the *Hypotyposis*, "and some of them are governed and led into the Heavenly Kingdom by one set of institutes (*ὑποτυπώσει*), others by another. One among all (the traditions) is the tradition preserved by us, *which we received from our great father and confessor Theodore; and not we alone, but also many of the best monasteries have chosen (and preserve) it as the most per-...*

=== LEAF p16 ===

*(Orig. p. XIII)*

...fect, removing both excesses and defects. Therefore we also now, submitting to paternal commands, have inclined ourselves unto obedience, *so as to transmit it in writing* (*ταύτην ἐν χάρταις εἰς ἄληστον μνήμην ταῖς μετέπειτα γενεαῖς καταλιπεῖν*) *unto everlasting memory for generations to come*" [^14]. Patriarch Alexius of Constantinople makes this connection understood by the very title of his exposition: "Typikon examining both the food and the drink of monastics, and every other order, and the stays both in church and everywhere, established indeed not in writing in the Studite monastery by our venerable father and confessor Theodore, who was hegumen therein, but delivered in writing by Alexius, holy and ecumenical patriarch, in the monastery established by him in the name of the Divine Mother" (*«Оуставникъ расмотрѧѧй, о брашьнѣ же, и о питии мнихо̑. о вьсѧцѣмь иномь чино̑. и о прѣбываниихъ и въ цр҃кви и вьсьде, оуставленъ оубо не по писанию въ манастыри стѹдиестцѣмь прпⷣбныимь оц҃мь нашимь исповѣдьникъмь Ѳеѡдоръмь, бывъшимь въ немь игоуменъмь, прѣданъ же писаниемь, ѿ Алѯиа с҃тго и вселеныѧ патриіарха, въ поставленѣмъ имъ монастыри, въ имѧ бж҃твыныа мт҃ре»*) [^15].

The important scholarly significance of both records just named has already been appreciated at its true worth by many scholarly researchers and apparently is beyond dispute. We desire at present merely to point out that to these two records currently known to scholarship must be added a third, which constitutes a highly important variant of the first Greek record, occurring in many manuscripts of Eastern book depositories, and hitherto for some reason entirely bypassed by previous investigators of the Church Ordo, although it was known to certain of them [^16]. This third record of the liturgical and monastic rules of the Studite Monastery we place immediately following the rules of St. Sabbas the Sanctified according to the Vatopedi manuscript of the 13th–14th century, No. 322 (956), with an indication of the variant readings according to the first record already known to scholarship. We possess sufficient substantial reasons for doing so.

=== LEAF p17 ===

*(Orig. p. XIV)*

The first record, bearing the title: "Hypotyposis of the constitution of the monastery of Studios" (*«Ὑποτύπωσις καταστάσεως τῆς μονῆς τῶν Στουδίου»*), became known to scholarship through the edition of the learned Cardinal Angelo Mai in his *Novae patrum bibliothecae* (vol. V, 1849, pp. 111–125), whence it was subsequently reprinted by Migne in the *Patrologiae Cursus Completus* (vol. 99, cols. 1704 ff.). This record, consisting of 38 small chapters, with a subdivision into seven sections or paragraphs bearing separate headings, attracted the attention of scholarly researchers solely by the titles of these paragraphs, which, in their opinion, "do not correspond to their contents and for the most part relate to the single nearest paragraph, while the following ones have their own distinct content. Thus, for example, after the introduction follows this title: 'In what manner we conduct ourselves in the assemblies of the holy, glorious, and third-day Resurrection of our Savior. Concerning the wood (that is, concerning the klepalo)'. Yet to this title only the second paragraph corresponds, while in the third and subsequent ones the discussion concerns the service of Pentecost, the Apostles' Fast, and diverse liturgical observations. The following rubric concerning Holy Great Lent even less justifies its title and contains more than half of its articles with unrelated contents," etc. Therefore, Professor Mansvetov made the supposition that these headings were superscribed by "a later compiler on the basis of the more prominent articles of the ordo, for greater convenience in use and for distinguishing the more important articles" [^17]. But the noted defect in the *Hypotyposis* according to Cardinal Mai's edition is, unfortunately, not the only one. Upon a careful reading of it, we encounter other peculiarities whose presence cannot be explained by anything other than textual corruption. At the end of the paragraph: "Concerning proportion in ministries" (*«περὶ τῆς ἐν διακονίαις συμμετρίας»*), we find, for example, utterly unrelated to the given title, the following very strange exposition of the rite of the Great Blessing of Waters on the Eve of the Theophany. "It should be known," we read in the *Hypotyposis*, "that on the Eve of the Lights (that is, Theophany), after the dismissal of the Divine Liturgy, we receive the antidoron; the communicants rinse their mouths, but we do not taste of the antidoron. When the priest puts away the vessels, he goes into the holy...

=== LEAF p18 ===

*(Orig. p. XV)*

...doors, and having made the opening exclamation (*καὶ ποιήσας εὐχήν*), goes out with the brethren to the water-blessing basin (*εἰς τὸν λουτῆρα*), during the singing of: **"When Thou, O Lord, wast baptized in the Jordan"** (*«Во Іордани крещающуся Тебѣ, Господи»*). During the singing of this, the deacon intones the Great Litany, after which the priest begins the prayer of sanctification. Upon the completion of the blessing of waters and the aspersion of the brethren with holy water, there is sung the troparion in Tone 8: **"The voice of the Lord cries upon the waters, saying"** (*«Гласъ Господень на водахъ вопіетъ глаголя»*), Verse 1: **"The sea saw it and fled"** (*«Море видѣ и побѣже»*), Verse 2: **"What ailed thee, O sea, that thou fleddest?"** (*«Что ти есть море, яко побѣже»*). After singing this troparion thrice, returning into the church, we sing the troparion in Tone 4: **"Thou Who clothest Thyself with light divinely"** (*«Одѣяйся свѣтомъ божественнѣ»*). After singing this troparion thrice, there is a prayer by the priest, and this divine doxology is concluded" [^18]. Although we find an exactly identical exposition of the blessing of waters on the Eve of the Theophany also in an excellent parchment codex of the year 1086 of the Sinai Library, No. 401, we nevertheless have no doubt that the present exposition suffers from irregularities and departs from the Byzantine practice that existed in the Church. Indeed, all the numerous copies of the rite of the Great Blessing of Waters known to us, not excluding those printed in our edition according to the Ordo of the Great Church (p. 41) and according to the Typikon of the Evergetis Monastery in Constantinople (cols. 380–381), in the exposition of its order, upon going out for the blessing of waters, indicate singing **"The voice of the Lord upon the waters"**, and upon the immersion of the cross into the water—the troparion **"When Thou, O Lord, wast baptized in the Jordan"**. Nowhere hitherto in the rites of the Great Blessing of Waters known to us have we seen indications of the troparion **"Thou Who clothest Thyself with light divinely"**, appointed to be sung upon returning into the church after the blessing of waters, in place of which in the majority of cases stands the troparion: **"Today the Trinity in the unity of the Godhead"** (*«Днесь Троица въ единицѣ Божества»*).

With still greater incongruities does the exposition of the article on fasting (chapter 30) on Great and Holy Saturday astonish us. "On Holy Saturday," we read in the *Hypotyposis* according to the edition of A. Mai, "at the eleventh hour begins Vespers (*τὸ λυχνικόν*), and when the dismissal is accomplished, we eat cheese and fish and eggs (*ἐσθίομεν τυρὸν καὶ ἰχθὺς καὶ...

=== LEAF p19 ===

*(Orig. p. XVI)*

...*ὠά*) and we drink three (krasobolia of wine)" [^19]. This remark is in direct contradiction with the practice of the ancient Christian Church, founded upon the exact instruction concerning the present fast in the book of the *Apostolic Constitutions* [^20], and with the entire subsequent practice of the Orthodox Church recorded in the numerous and most varied copies of the Church Ordo or Typikon [^21]. For us it is highly important to note here also the following...

=== LEAF p20 ===

*(Orig. p. XVII)*

...two curious facts known to us: first, that in the best copies of the *Hypotyposis*, such as is undoubtedly the aforementioned copy of the Sinai manuscript of the year 1086 under No. 401, the phrase that troubles us: *"ἐσθίομεν δὲ τυρὸν καὶ ἰχθῦς καὶ ὠά· καὶ πίνομεν ἀνὰ γʹ"* ["and we eat cheese and fish and eggs, and we drink three (krasobolia) each"] is entirely *absent*; and second, that in the *Diatyposis* (*Διατύπωσις*) of St. Athanasius of Athos, formed on the basis of the *Hypotyposis* attributed to Theodore the Studite, the present phrase is not only not reflected, but, on the contrary, we see an enactment concerning the fast of Great Saturday that is completely the opposite, set forth in the spirit of the practice generally accepted in the Orthodox Church regarding this matter, and even with a sufficiently well-founded rationale. "On Holy Saturday," writes St. Athanasius of Athos, "at the half of the twelfth hour we begin Vespers. After the dismissal *the refectory is not opened* (*ἡ δὲ τράπεζα οὐκ ἀνοίγει*), *because the Liturgy finishes late, and lest by satiety we burden the stomach and the mind*. We content ourselves with a single blessed bread, drinking two krasobolia of wine in the narthex" (p. 253).

These two examples noted by us seem to us sufficient for us to have the right to regard the *Hypotyposis* known to scholarship according to the edition of Angelo Mai with skepticism; yet we can also point to other grounds for this. Thus, for example, if one compares the exposition of the services on Thomas Sunday and on Monday of that same week according to Mai's *Hypotyposis*...

<!-- END COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort2 -->

---

<!-- START COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort3 -->

=== LEAF p21 ===

*(Orig. p. XVIII)*

...with the same services in the *Diatyposis* (*Διατύπωσις*) of St. Athanasius of Athos, then it immediately becomes evident that in the first exposition there is no clarity, no completeness; a certain confusion is noticeable, leading the reader into perplexity, especially if we continue the comparisons with other monuments known to us that have the peculiarities of the Studite practice. Between homogeneous monuments there is even observed a contradiction: according to Mai's *Hypotyposis*, as at present, on Thomas Sunday the Sunday service is sung and the first Sunday Gospel is read (p. 228, note 4), whereas in the *Diatyposis* of Athanasius of Athos on Thomas Sunday the ordinary service is celebrated, and the entire Sunday service is transferred to Monday (p. 247). Continuing the comparisons of the monuments named by me still further, one cannot fail to notice that in the section: "Concerning Holy Great Lent" (*«περὶ τῆς ἁγίας τεσσαρακοστῆς»*) in the *Hypotyposis* there are not a few superfluities and repetitions compared with the *Diatyposis*. (Cf. p. 231, note 7 and pp. 250–251).

Finally, if we direct our attention to the multitude of orthographic and paleographic errors in the currently known *Hypotyposis*, which hinder the understanding of its meaning even for persons of profound education and well acquainted with the language of the present monument, our mistrust toward it will become fully understandable. The least satisfactory in this respect in the *Hypotyposis* must be considered chapters 28 and 30. The end of chapter 28 reads as follows: *Σημεῖον δὲ τέλους τῆς ἀναγνώσεως ταύτης ἐστὶν ὁ ἦχος τῶν κλιερίων ἐν τῇ ὑστάτῃ μαγειρίᾳ, πάντων ὁμοῦ ἐπὶ τῶν παραδίψων ἐῤῥιπτομένων* ["And the sign of the end of this reading is the sound of the spoons in the final cooked dish, all being thrown down together upon the side dishes"], with an indication directly from the manuscript whence Mai took the text of the *Hypotyposis*, in this form: "Ita cod." [^22]. In the Sinai codex of the year 1086, No. 401, that same place reads more correctly: *Σημεῖον δὲ τοῦ τέλους τῆς ἀναγνώσεως ταύτης ἐστὶν ὁ ἦχος (κο)χλιαρίων ἐν τῇ ὑποστάτῃ μαγειρίᾳ, πάντων ὁμοῦ ἐπὶ τῶν παροψίδων ἐπιῤῥιπτομένων* ["And the sign of the end of this reading is the sound of the spoons in the underlying dish, all being thrown down together upon the side dishes"]. As for chapter 30, we shall dwell here on the non-existent words: *ἀπίεφθα* and *ἔνκρασιν*, which plunge into utter perplexity anyone desiring to comprehend the text of the *Hypotyposis*. Prof. E. E. Golubinsky, having appended...

=== LEAF p22 ===

*(Orig. p. XIX)*

...-ing to his *History of the Russian Church* a translation of this *Hypotyposis*, left the first word untranslated with a Russian transcription: "apieftas" [*«апіефты»*], and with a reservation in a footnote that of such a "word we find no trace either in Passow or in Byzantios." Only with the aid of the Latin translation (*pira cocta* [stewed pears]) did the esteemed professor guess the meaning of the given word [^23], whereas in other copies this word, or rather, two words, are read separately: *ἄππια* and *ἑφθά*, and their meaning is completely clear: stewed pears. The second word Prof. Golubinsky translates: "eukraton" and considers it "corrupt" in the Greek text [^24], whereas this word also consists, like the first, of two words: *ἓν* and *κρασίν*, or rather, *κρασίον*, and their meaning is likewise clear: one krasobolion of wine each.

All these and similar deficiencies prompted us to direct serious attention to this exceedingly important liturgical monument, which occurs in manuscripts in two recensions utterly different in composition and exposition. Examining both recensions of the *Hypotyposis* more closely, we noticed that one of them is well known to scholarship through the edition of A. Mai and occurs in manuscripts rather frequently, while the other, occurring more rarely in manuscripts, is completely unknown, although in the interest of its textual peculiarities it stands not a whit lower than the first. In this latter recension we found both a more systematic arrangement of the materials included in the composition of the *Hypotyposis*, and more correspondence between the titles and the contents of the rubrics that they encompass; there are no unnecessary repetitions here, such as abound in the first recension; the article on the Theophany blessing of waters stands as an addition, as it should, at the end of the *Hypotyposis*, while the order itself of the blessing of waters is set forth in accordance with the customary practice of the Byzantine Church; the article on food on Holy Saturday does not contain the incongruities noted above (*Τῷ δὲ ἁγίῳ σαββάτῳ ὥρας ιαʹ. ἄρχεται λυχνικόν, καὶ ὅπου σώσει ἡ ἀπόλυσις, ἐσθίομεν δὲ ἄρτον καὶ ὀπώρας, πίνομεν καὶ οἶνον, ποτήρια δύο* ["And on Holy Saturday at the eleventh hour Vespers begins, and wherever the dismissal falls, we eat bread and fruits, and we drink also wine, two cups"]); in its...

=== LEAF p23 ===

*(Orig. p. XX)*

...place and in proper order stands the service of Monday of Thomas Week; finally, in the text of this recension there is far more correctness compared with the first. But the second recension of the *Hypotyposis*, whose text we publish according to the most ancient Athos Vatopedi manuscript of the 13th–14th century, No. 322 (956), has yet another important advantage: in its text we find very many substantial additions of which the first recension knows nothing, and which acquire special value in our eyes because these additions are at times literally identical with similar articles in the 12th-century Typikon of Patriarch Alexius of Constantinople, who, as we have said, took the orders of the Studite Monastery as the basis of his record. Let us make, by way of example, several comparisons between both records, that is, according to the *Hypotyposis* of the Athos Vatopedi manuscript No. 322 (956) and according to the manuscript of the Moscow Synodal Library No. 380/330.

The first peculiarity of the recension of the *Hypotyposis* published by us consists in a detailed description of the rite of the kiss of the monks at Matins on the first day of Pascha. This description is completely omitted in the first recension of A. Mai.

| Athos Vatopedi MS No. 322 (956) | Moscow Synodal Library MS No. 380/330 |

| *(p. 227)* One must know that after the completion of Matins of the bright-bearing Sunday the kiss takes place thus: one of the Levites [deacons], putting off his priestly vestment and taking the Holy Gospel, stands before the doors of the holy sanctuary. The hegumen then, entering and kissing the Holy Gospel, then also the deacon, stands near him. Then thus the priests and all the brethren, each according to his own order, with tapers kiss one another, and proclaiming **"Christ is risen"**, those being kissed reply in turn... | *(fol. 37v)* And the order of kissing takes place thus: the deacon stands before the screen of the altar on the right side, holding the Holy Gospel; then [^25] the hegumen passes first, and having kissed the Holy Gospel, he too [^26] stands on the left side near the deacon; after this all [^27] the brethren come with candles, first [^28] kissing the Gospel, after this the hegumen, and the other... [^29] |

*(Orig. p. XXI)*

| ...**"Truly He is risen."** The taxiarchs maintaining this good order and all standing in rank. When they reach as far as the royal doors, they run back as far as the same royal doors; and thus finally they fill up also the other part of the temple, and all saying with a loud voice **"Christ is risen"**, they subjoin also **"Let the heavens rejoice and let the earth be glad"**. Then thus the kanonarch, if he happens to be present, or another of the brethren, ascends the ambo and reads the discourse of our holy father John Chrysostom, whose incipit is: **"If anyone is devout and a lover of God."** And after the completion of the reading, all having assembled in a flock, and having given thanks unto the Lord, and having made the three prostrations, a prayer is said by the hegumen and the complete dismissal of Matins is made. | ...brethren. And they hold candles before the beginning of the kanon and until the conclusion [^30]. And after the kiss, all the brethren standing, the word of Chrysostom on Holy Pascha is read from the hegumen, *(fol. 38)* whose incipit is: **"Whosoever is devout and a lover of God."** [^31] And after the reading there is a litany, in which the hegumen and the brethren make commemoration, saying [^32] [^33]: **"Lord, have mercy,"** first thrice, after this twelve times; after this [^34] the dismissal. |

The following two observations of our *Hypotyposis*—concerning the Liturgy on the first day of Pascha and concerning the procession on Monday—likewise find corresponding parallels in the Typikon of Patriarch Alexius of Constantinople according to the manuscript of the Moscow Synodal Library No. 380/330.

*(Orig. p. XXII)*

| ...Likewise also on every other master-feast [despotic feast] we do thus. And the deacon says the prokeimenon and the Apostle and the Alleluiarion from within the bema, likewise also the protopriest [protopresbyter] reads the Holy Gospel. | ...the Gospel, which is read by the hegumen, standing near the altar table, two deacons standing on either side and holding the Gospel. The antiphons they sing outside the altar, from the people, which are these: (that is, of Pascha). *(fol. 39)* And this too is to be known: that after the passing of the Great Week, no one any longer says the prokeimenon, and the Apostle, and the Alleluia, and the Gospel within the altar, but outside the altar, for the one who maintains the ordo. |
| *(p. 228)* And on the morrow, at the second hour of the day, the kanonarch striking the three beats, we assemble in the temple of the great Forerunner, and the priests vesting in the sacred phelonia [diploides], and all the brethren lifting the precious crosses and the venerable and holy icons, we encircle the vineyard situated near the monastery, all saying with a loud voice **"Christ is risen"**. Then thus we depart unto the seashore, and a fervent litany [ektene] having taken place, we depart unto the temple of the Most Holy Theotokos, and there making a fervent litany, we return unto the temple of the holy Forerunner. And before the entrance of the litany [procession] the kanonarch strikes, and the beginning takes place, and the priests making the entrance, the Divine Liturgy is celebrated. | *(fol. 40)* On that day (MS of the Typography Library No. 285: *on Monday*), at the second hour of the day, striking the klepalo thrice (there also: *three times*), all the brethren gather at the church, and having vested in hierarchical (there also: *hierarchs'*) vestments, the beginning takes place, that is, after **"Christ is risen"**, and after this going with the crosses, they make a circuit around the monastery, singing **"Christ is risen"**, and **"Though into the tomb"**, and with **"Lord, have mercy."** And while these things take place, they strike the klepalo; and having returned with the cross, they begin *(fol. 41)* the Liturgy, and they celebrate the service in the church, just as on the Great Sunday. |

In the recension of the *Hypotyposis* published by us we read (p. 228) an observation unknown to Cardinal A. Mai: "One must know that...

=== LEAF p26 ===

*(Orig. p. XXIII)*

...on the third day of Renewal Week we bestow [the schema] upon the brethren appointed to receive the holy and great schema." In the Typikon of Patriarch Alexius not only is a similar custom mentioned, but a special service is appointed for this occasion. Behold with what features it is described in the named monument: *(fol. 41)* "That same thing one must observe (in MS No. 285 of the Typography Library it reads more clearly: *in the synaxarion it is fitting*) likewise: that if certain of the brethren desire to be adorned with the great schema on the second day of the festive week (there also: *the feast*), they sing on Monday evening after the stichera of Pascha, three stichera of the schema in Tone 2, podoben: **'When from the tree'** (*«Егда от древа»*), likewise also the kanon of the divine schema at Matins in Tone 2 by Theophanes, after the kanon of Pascha, **'Day of Resurrection'** (*«Въскрьсения день»*), and the stichera after **'Praise the Lord'** (*«Хвалите Господа»*), **'Pascha sacred'** (*«Пасха священая»*), and the rest; thus they sing also on the Great Sunday."

Above we already had occasion to note that the copy of the *Hypotyposis* published by us, unlike the one published by Cardinal Mai, contains, among other things, indications also concerning the fact that on Monday of Thomas Week the Sunday service was to be celebrated, while on the eve, on the Sunday itself, the ordinary service. In our *Hypotyposis* we read: "On Sunday evening we begin: **'Blessed is the man'** (*Μακάριος ἀνήρ*) and of the resurrectional first tone [^35]..." and further: "And on the morrow at Matins after the Six Psalms, we sing **'God is the Lord'** (*Θεὸς Κύριος*) in Tone 1 (*ἦχος πρῶτος*) and the troparion: **'The stone having been sealed'** (*Τοῦ λίθου σφραγισθέντος*), and one kathisma of psalms, and we read thrice. And after the completion of the reading, immediately the anavathmoi in Tone 1, the resurrectional prokeimenon, **'Let every breath'** and the Gospel, then **'In the nights'** and **'Having seen the Resurrection of Christ'**, Psalm 50 [LXX 50], and immediately the kanon" (pp. 228–229). If we compare this observation of the *Hypotyposis* with the complete exposition of the service for Monday of Thomas Week in the Typikon of Patriarch Alexius, we shall see that although in the exposition there are differences between both monuments, nevertheless the peculiarities of the service itself in both are one and the same. Behold how the present service is described in the manuscript of the Moscow Synodal Library No. 380/330, fol. 45v: "On Monday of Antipascha at Matins after the psalmody...

=== LEAF p27 ===

*(Orig. p. XXIV)*

...they sing **'God is the Lord'** in Tone 1: **'The stone having been sealed'** (*«Камени знаменану»*), at *Glory... and now...*: **'While Gabriel was uttering'** (*«Гаврилу провѣщавшу»*). After this they sing the second kathisma: **'I will confess Thee, O Lord'** (*«Исповѣмь тебѣ, Господи»*), and they sing the sessional hymn of the resurrection in Tone 1, and at *Glory... and now...* the second, and then *(fol. 46)* reading begins from the beginning: the commentary of Chrysostom on the Holy Gospel of John... And after the reading they sing the anavathmoi in Tone 1. After this they sing the prokeimenon: **'Now will I arise, saith the Lord'** (*«Нынѣ воскресну, глаголетъ Господь»*); **'Let every breath'**, and the number of the Gospel: the first resurrection Gospel, that of the eleven disciples. After this they say: **'Having seen the Resurrection of Christ'** (*«Воскресение Христово видѣвше»*), and Psalm 50 [LXX 50]. After this they sing two kanons: one resurrectional in Tone 1, then that of Antipascha in the same tone, the composition of Andrew: **'With a pillar of fire'** (*«Столпомъ огненымъ»*) (in MS No. 285 of the Typography Library: *then the resurrectional kanon*)."

The observation of our *Hypotyposis* that in the service of Holy Pentecost nothing is sung from the Octoechos and not even the Sunday Matins Gospel is read, but everything appointed is festive (p. 230), finds a place for itself also upon the pages of the Typikon of Patriarch Alexius (MS of the Moscow Synodal Library No. 380/330, fols. 60v, 61).

Noting, in this manner, the peculiarities of the copy of the *Hypotyposis* published by us and comparing them with similar peculiarities in the well-known Typikon of Patriarch Alexius of Constantinople, we not only indicate the proper place for this copy of the *Hypotyposis* alongside the named Typikon of Patriarch...

| A. Mai, *Bibliotheca Patrum*, Vol. V, pp. 112–113 | Ph. Meyer, *Die Haupturkunden*, pp. 130–131; cf. p. 246 |
| :--- | :--- |
| One must know that after the second watch of the night has passed, that is, the sixth [^36] hour, at the beginning of the seventh, the signal of the water-clock falls, and at its sounding the awakener arises, and goes round the bedchambers with a lantern, ex-... | One must know that after the third watch of the night has passed, that is, the ninth hour, at the beginning of the tenth, the signal of the water-clock falls, and at its sounding they arise immediately and sound the wooden klepala, and all the brethren assembling in the nar-... |

=== LEAF p28 ===

*(Orig. p. XXV)*

...Alexius, whose scholarly reputation is already sufficiently firmly established, but by that very fact we clear for ourselves also a possible path, in our opinion, toward a more or less probable determination of the time of origin of both records or recensions of the given monument.

The *Diatyposis* (*Διατύπωσις*) of St. Athanasius of Athos, as we have said, was formed on the basis of the *Hypotyposis* attributed to St. Theodore the Studite. Upon comparing both monuments, this dependence becomes beyond all doubt and can be proved by numerous examples, which we consider superfluous to cite here [^37]. For us in the present case it is important merely to state two indubitable facts: (a) that the *Diatyposis* of Athanasius of Athos, in its exposition and even in its text, stands much closer to the *Hypotyposis* in the recension of Cardinal Mai than to the recension published by us; and (b) that the *Hypotyposis* in our recension, as we have already partly indicated above, stands much closer to the other record of the orders of the Studite Monastery in the exposition of Patriarch Alexius of Constantinople, which in its turn diverges significantly from the *Hypotyposis* of Cardinal Mai. To be convinced of this, we consider it sufficient to make here a comparison of the texts of the Paschal service according to all four records of Studite liturgical orders currently known.

| ...and consubstantial and life-creating Trinity, always, now [and forever, and unto ages of ages]. And the priest, running back through the southern part and censing, enters into the sanctuary. And having deposited the censer, the brethren having already said **"Christ is risen"** for the third time, the priest stands before the mercy-seat [hilasterion], saying the verse: **"This is the day which the Lord hath made; form a feast with thick branches even unto the horns of the altar"**, and he glorifies, the brethren singing at each [verse] **"Christ is risen."** And after the completion of this, begins: **"Let us sing unto the Lord"**, and immediately **"Day of Resurrection"**, inasmuch as during this week we do not sing the Six Psalms. And there is one reading from the Theologian [Gregory of Nazianzus]: **"Day of Resurrection"**, and the beginning is on the right [side]; and at the sixth ode, the kontakion, **"Having seen the Resurrection of Christ"**, and the fiftieth [Psalm 50], and the order of Matins dismisses. | ...-ern side of the church, entering the church [^45] doors, while the brethren behind him enter into the church.
