=== LEAF p33 ===

*(Orig. p. XXX)*

...-ness of the Resurrection." After this is read the Word [^51] of Gregory the Theologian: **"Day of Resurrection."** After the 6th ode they sing the kontakion in Tone 8: **"Though into the tomb Thou didst descend"**, the brethren standing until the completion of the first ikos. After this: **"Having seen the Resurrection of Christ"**, thrice; the entire **"Have mercy upon me, O God"** [Psalm 50] being recited [^52]. Exapostilarion [svitilen] in Tone 4: **"Having fallen asleep in the flesh"**, which they sing during the whole week. After this, before **"Praise the Lord"**, they sing the stichera in Tone 1, idiomela, two by two [^53]. **"Today salvation to the world"**, then **"The day of Resurrection, bring forth fruit"**, then **"Of the bright and glorious"**; and at "Glory..." and "Now...", the first sticheron. After this they sing the morning song quietly: **"Glory to God in the highest."** After it there are the diaconal petitions from the deacon, and from the priest the prayer. After this they sing the stichera in Tone 5: **"Pascha sacred."**

If we now recall the above-indicated examples of similarity between the *Hypotyposis* published by us and the Typikon of Patriarch Alexius according to the 12th-century manuscript of the Moscow Synodal Library No. 330/380, which very sharply distinguish both recensions of the *Hypotyposis* known to us from one another, both in completeness of composition and even in exposition; if, finally, we direct our attention to the fact that certain articles of the *Hypotyposis* printed by Cardinal Mai are completely omitted in the recension published by us (as, for example, chapters 14 and 15), and in their peculiarities and exposition stand in a rather close relation to the *Diatyposis* of the Venerable Athanasius of Athos (cf. p. 231, note 7 and pp. 249–250), then the connection of this latter with the *Hypotyposis* of Cardinal Mai becomes beyond all doubt for us. And since the *Diatyposis* of the Venerable Athanasius of Athos appeared not long before the demise of this father, which, as Meyer supposes, ensued between the years 997 and 1011 [^54], it is natural to conclude from this that the copy of the *Hypotyposis* that was laid at its foundation appeared much earlier than this time. In other words, we consequently consider the *Hypotyposis* in the recension of Cardinal Mai to be older than the *Hypotyposis* printed by us, which, as we think, in its turn came into the world not later than *the first half of the 11th century*, because all...

=== LEAF p34 ===

*(Orig. p. XXXI)*

...the peculiarities of this recension were reflected, as we already know, in the Typikon of Patriarch Alexius, written for the monastery founded by him in Constantinople, in all probability around the year 1034, when the main catholicon Church of the Dormition was consecrated.

Having thus indicated the scholarly significance of both recensions of the *Hypotyposis*, attributed by tradition to the Venerable Theodore the Studite, which are encountered very frequently in manuscripts, it remains for us merely to express regret that during our stay on Sinai we did not make use of the excellent manuscript of the year 1086, No. 401, for the correction or even for a new edition of the *Hypotyposis* in the recension of Cardinal Mai, but limited ourselves only to noting from it some of the most important and most curious variants [^55]. We cherish the firm conviction that this gap will be filled in the near future by other researchers and editors of the works of the Venerable Theodore the Studite.

VI. From what has been stated above concerning the relation of the *Hypotyposis* of Theodore the Studite to the *Diatyposis* [^56] of the Venerable Athanasius of Athos, it becomes obvious of itself what important significance this monument possesses in scholarly terms. The necessity of publishing it...

=== LEAF p35 ===

*(Orig. p. XXXII)*

...to the world is, consequently, beyond all doubt. But one can also point out other reasonable motives for the same. The *Diatyposis* of Athanasius of Athos until the very latest time was known to scholarship only in the *Russian translation* [^57] of the late father Archimandrite Antonin, and that with large *omissions* in the text, which depended on the defectiveness of the original from which it had been made. We noted several omissions in the translation at the beginning of the *Diatyposis*, but there are far more of them in that part which describes the divine services of the lavra of the Venerable Athanasius of Athos. For example, here the service on the Saturday before Pentecost is not finished; the service of Vespers on the feast of Pentecost is completely omitted, as are the daily cycle services during the fast of the holy Apostles (pp. 248–249), and so forth. It is not surprising, therefore, that in our time we could not agree [^58] with many of those differences between the *Diatyposis* of Athanasius of Athos and the *Hypotyposis* of the Venerable Theodore the Studite that were pointed out by Prof. Mansvetov in his dissertation: *The Church Ordo*, pp. 66–68.

Having printed in the present *Description* the *Diatyposis* of the Venerable Athanasius of Athos according to a 16th-century Iveron manuscript, as the only one accessible to us, with the addition of variants for certain parts of it taken from manuscripts of the Cairo Patriarchal Library and the library of the Russian Panteleimon Monastery on Mount Athos, when there appeared recently Meyer's book under the title: *Die Haupturkunden für die Geschichte der Athosklöster*, Leipzig 1894, in which its author succeeded in publishing entirely the testament of the Venerable Athanasius of Athos according to codices of the skeuophylakion of the Athos Lavra hitherto inaccessible [^59], we collated our copy with this latter and found very substantial gaps in our own. In view of the serious critical methods that were applied by Mr. Meyer in publishing these most important monuments of 10th-century liturgical literature, and the authority...

=== LEAF p36 ===

*(Orig. p. XXXIII)*

...of the originals, we resolved in our *Description*, at the end of the book, "in the proof-reading errata and additions," to print corrections to the testament of the Venerable Athanasius of Athos, borrowing them from Meyer's new book.

VII. The division of "ktetorik Typika" in the proper sense begins with manuscript No. 788 of the Athens National Library with the title: "Synaxarion, with God, that is, the Typikon of the ecclesiastical service of the venerable monastery of the Most Holy Theotokos the Benefactress [Evergetis], beginning from the month of September, containing the entire service until the completion of August; likewise...

=== LEAF p37 ===

*(Orig. p. XXXIV)*

...and of the Holy Great Lent, from the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee until All Saints." As the cited title shows, this manuscript contains within itself a complete Church Ordo with a detailed exposition of the services for every day of the year, that is, according to the Synaxarion and the Lenten and Flowery [Festal] Triodia [Pentecostarion]. In scholarship, such a ktetorik Typikon was known hitherto only in Church Slavonic translation according to the 12th-century manuscript of the Moscow Synodal Library No. 330/380, given by Patriarch Alexius of Constantinople (around 1034) to the monastery founded by him in Constantinople; but in the Orthodox East, the Evergetis Typikon, so far as we know, is for the time being the sole representative of this class of monuments. In Eastern manuscript repositories, to be sure, not a few copies and originals of ktetorik Typika are encountered, but all of them consist of a single part, the so-called prologue, treating of the time of foundation of this or that monastery, of the ktetors and benefactors, of property holdings, of the internal monastic organization of life in them, and so forth. In the Evergetis Typikon being described, this part of the Typikon bears the title: "The Prologue of the Typikon" (*«Ὁ πρόλογος τοῦ Τυπικοῦ»*), or more specifically, "Exposition and rule of life of the monks in the monastery of the Most Holy Theotokos the Benefactress" (*«Ἔκθεσις καὶ ὑποτύπωσις τοῦ βίου τῶν ἐν τῇ μονῇ τῆς ὑπεραγías Θεοτόκου τῆς Εὐεργέτιδος μοναχῶν»*), and occupies a position at its end, and not at the beginning as the inscription says—evidently through a misunderstanding of the binder of the manuscript, who placed this part of the Typikon not in its proper place. As regards the exposition of the peculiarities of church services, and that for an entire year, in the majority of monuments of this class we do not find it; the whole matter in them is for the most part limited to a brief indication to be guided in liturgical practice either by the generally accepted Typika—the Studite and the Jerusalem—or else by the Typika of some well-known monasteries or churches. Thus, for example, in the Typikon of the year 1162 of the Monastery of the Mother of God *tōn Hēliou Bōmōn*, there is indicated "the Studite Synaxarion currently sung" (*«τὸ νῦν ψαλλόμενον Στουδιτικὸν Συναξάριον»*) (p. 728 et al.); in the Typikon of the year 1210 of Nilus, hegumen of the Monastery of the Mother of God of Machairas—to the Typikon of the monastery of the Venerable Sabbas the Sanctified according to Palestine (*κατὰ Παλαιστίνην*) [^60]; in the Typikon of the Auxentios Monastery—to the Jerusalem...

=== LEAF p38 ===

*(Orig. p. XXXV)*

...Typikon (*τὸ ἱεροσολυμικὸν Τυπικόν*) (p. 785); in the Typikon of the Monastery of St. Mamas—to the Typikon of the Evergetis Monastery (p. 728, note 1; p. 758, note 5; p. 760, note 3); in the Typikon of the Pantokrator Monastery for the Church of the Mother of God the Merciful [Eleousa]—to the Typikon of the Great Palace Church or the *Ekklesiastes* (p. 678), and so forth. But the Evergetis and Alexian Typika were not exceptional phenomena in the series of monuments of this kind. Among the ktetorik Typika of ancient Byzantium there are known at present also such in which we find clear indications of the existence of separate, independent expositions of church-liturgical orders practiced in a specific monastery, written by its ktetor specially for it alone. "And all doxologies, both nocturnal and diurnal," writes the dignitary Michael Attaleiates in his Typikon, "I will to take place according to my Typikon, which I composed privately" (*«Τὰς δὲ δοξολογίας πάσας, τάς τε νυκτερινὰς καὶ ἡμερινάς, θέλω γινέσθαι κατὰ τὸ Τυπικόν μου, ὅπερ ἰδίως συνεγραψάμην»*) [^61]. "We will, therefore, that the service with the divine hymns of this venerable monastery proceed," says Emperor John Komnenos in the Typikon of the Pantokrator Monastery, "according to the ecclesiastical Typikon enacted by us" (*«κατὰ τὸ γεγονὸς παρ’ ἡμῶν ἐκκλησιαστικὸν Τυπικόν»*) (p. 657). There is reason to believe that similar Typika or Synaxaria existed in the monastery of Empress Irene [^62], in the Hilandar Athonite Monastery [^63], and elsewhere. Confirmation that there were many such ktetorik liturgical Typika in ancient times in Byzantium is found in the *Taktikon* of Nikon of the Black Mountain, an 11th-century writer, who, when compiling his comparative table of the peculiarities of various Typika, undoubtedly made use of such Typika. Unfortunately, they have not been preserved down to our time and are not encountered in the known manuscript repositories of the Orthodox East. It is completely natural, therefore, that the Evergetis Typikon in the Greek original and the Alexian Typikon in the Slavonic translation, as representatives of this class of monuments of an entire epoch, acquire in our eyes a special value and exceptional interest.

=== LEAF p39 ===

*(Orig. p. XXXVI)*

In our literature concerning the time of foundation of the Monastery of the Mother of God Evergetis and the personality of its ktetor, there exist two opposing opinions. Some (Prof. N. P. Kondakov) call its ktetor Basil the Protasekretis [^64] and assign the time of the monastery's construction to the 10th or the beginning of the 11th century, while others (P. Savvaitov) more correctly consider it to be the hegumen of this monastery, Paul [^65], but do not know either the place where the monastery was situated, nor the time when it was built. Both of these opinions are based on a single source—on the book: *Constantinopolitana Christiana* by Du Cange [^66], who himself does not specify precisely either the time of construction of the Evergetis Monastery, nor the name of its ktetor, and cites extracts from manuscripts confirming both the one and the other opinion. In the complete copy of the Evergetis Typikon now being published to the world according to the manuscript of the Athens University Library, we draw the most authentic information concerning everything that relates to the initial fate of the given monastery. The second chapter of its prologue directly and categorically names as ktetor of the monastery the ever-memorable and most blessed Paul of Evergetis, who, having left fatherland, parents, relatives, and friends, arrived in Constantinople (*«εἰς βασιλίδα ταύτην μεγαλόπολιν»*) and here, in a suburban (*προάστειον*), deserted (*τὸ ἀγρίδιον*) little place that had fallen to him by inheritance (*«ἐκ γονικῆς αὐτῷ κληρονομίας»*), founded a monastery at his own expense in the month of June 6556 = 1048, fourth indiction (pp. 615–616). An indication of the suburban location of the Evergetis Monastery is also found in the second journey of St. Sabbas, Archbishop of Serbia, to Jerusalem (between 1233 and 1237). Upon returning from this journey to Constantinople, the Venerable Sabbas, as is related here, "first having come, bowed before the most bright Benefactress, the Most Pure Theotokos Evergetis, and thence went into the midst of the city to the holy Apostle Andrew" [^67]. Our 12th-century pilgrim,...

=== LEAF p40 ===

*(Orig. p. XXXVII)*

...Archbishop Antony of Novgorod, likewise places this monastery outside the city, not far from the renowned Monastery of the Peribleptos, which was located in a district called "Triantaphyllitsa" (*Τριακονταφύλλου*), in the neighborhood of the Monastery of St. Mokios, situated in the 12th Region of Constantinople, near the Golden Gate: "And thence (that is, from the monastery where lay the relics of St. Stephen the New) the metochion of the Holy Theotokos of Evergetis... and in this monastery lived Sabbas, the Serbian prince, when he came forth from the Holy Mountain" [^68].

The Venerable Paul governed the small brotherhood (*ὀλίγους τινάς*) of his monastery for five years, dying on April 16 in the year 6562 = 1054. Dying, the Venerable Paul transferred his little (*μικρόν*) monastery, by testament (*ἐνδιαθήκως*), to his disciple Timothy, who had come to the monastery founded by him only two months after its opening, that is, in the month of September in 6557 = 1048. To the lot of this second hegumen of the present monastery fell the task of building in it the main catholicon church (*κυριακὸς ναός, μεγάλη ἐκκλησία*) in the name of the Mother of God Evergetis (the Benefactress), the renewal [consecration] of which was celebrated in the monastery on December 29 (p. 369); of expanding and improving the monastic cells; of supplying this church with ecclesiastical appointments; of painting it with frescoes; of seeing to everything that concerned both its internal and external arrangement and prosperity (p. 616). Besides the catholicon church, under the same hegumen there were built a small chapel (*τὸ εὐκτήριον τοῦ Σωτῆρος*) in honor of the Savior (pp. 481–571), a similar chapel in honor of the Apostles (*τὸ εὐκτήριον* or *ναὸς τῶν ἁγίων ἀποστόλων*) (pp. 366, 533), and a church in the brethren's sepulchre (p. 447). It is not in the least surprising, therefore, that Hegumen Timothy, the compiler of the present Typikon, directly declares concerning himself that this little (*μικρόν*) monastery, which had come to him from his renowned predecessor, he brought from obscurity and incompleteness (*«ἀπὸ τοῦ ἀφανοῦς καὶ ἀτελοῦς»*) into a magnificent appearance and made it famous (*«ἐπὶ τὸ μεγαλοπρεπέστατον καὶ διαφανές»*) (p. 616).

<!-- END COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort4 -->

---

<!-- START COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort5 -->

=== LEAF p41 ===

*(Orig. p. XXXVIII)*

In the same views of the good ordering of the present monastery and the establishment of a strict order regarding all aspects of its life, one must suppose, Hegumen Timothy also compiled an independent Typikon for his monastery. The compiler everywhere speaks of himself in the first person (*"I came... having left the monastery to me"*, etc.) (p. 616) and commands the solemn celebration of the memory of his predecessor, the ktetor of the monastery, Hegumen Paul (pp. 646, 381). But the manuscript of the Athens University Library is not the original copy of the Evergetis Typikon that Hegumen Timothy compiled in the second half of the 11th century, but one somewhat later in origin, reworked and supplemented already after his death. This is evident from the inscription of the prologue to the present Typikon: "Exposition and rule of life of the monks in the monastery of the Most Holy Theotokos the Benefactress, handed down by Timothy, monk, priest, and hegumen, who came after the ktetor of the same monastery" (*«Ἔκθεσις καὶ ὑποτύπωσις τοῦ βίου τῶν ἐν τῇ μονῇ τῆς ὑπεραγίας Θεοτόκου τῆς Εὐεργέτιδος, παραδοθεῖσα παρὰ Τιμοθέου μοναχοῦ, ἱερέως καὶ καθηγουμένου, γεγονότος μετὰ τὸν κτήτορα τῆς αὐτῆς μονῆς»*) (p. 615). Here Hegumen Timothy is already called the "former hegumen." At the end of the prologue, after its last chapter: "Concerning the reading of the Typikon at the beginning of each month" (*«Περὶ τοῦ ἀναγινώσκεσθαι τὸ Τυπικὸν καθ’ ἑκάστου μηνὸς ἀρχήν»*), and the concluding words of the compiler of the Typikon: **"Glory to Thee, our God, glory to Thee"** (*«Δόξα σοι, ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν, δόξα σοι»*) (p. 654), in our copy there stands a new supplementary chapter prescribing the celebration of the memory of the second hegumen of the monastery, Timothy, together with the first, the Venerable Paul, on the same day, that is, April 16, since the demise of the second hegumen also coincided with the day of the demise of the first (*ibid.*). This supplementary chapter concludes with the same prayer as the entire prologue: **"And may the God of peace..."** (*«Ὁ δὲ Θεὸς τῆς εἰρήνης κ. τ. λ.»*) (p. 655 cf. 654). In virtue of this chapter, in the Synaxarion itself under April 16 we already read the following: "On the same day we perform together the commemorations of our venerable fathers and ktetors, both the monk Lord Paul and the monk Lord Timothy" (*«Τῇ αὐτῇ ἡμέρᾳ τελοῦμεν ἡνώμενος τὰ μνημόσυνα τῶν ὁσίων πατέρων ἡμῶν καὶ κτητόρων τοῦ τε μοναχοῦ κυροῦ Παύλου καὶ τοῦ μοναχοῦ κυроῦ Τιμοθέου»*), and we find a detailed exposition of their memorial service (pp. 446–447). Further: as a tradition proceeding from the person of Hegumen Timothy ("We received from our superior, the holy father, Lord Timothy" [*«Παρελάβομεν παρὰ τοῦ προεστῶτος ἡμῶν ἁγίου πατρὸς κυροῦ Τιμοθέου»*]), who moreover is called a holy father, there is set forth in the Synaxarion an article on food and drink on the Saturday of the first week [of Great Lent] (p. 519) and on the eve of that day, while in the prologue there is a prohibition against the consumption of wine...

=== LEAF p42 ===

*(Orig. p. XXXIX)*

...for the monks during the entire first week and on Wednesdays and Fridays throughout all of Great Lent (p. 654).

To whom, then, does the final redaction of the Typikon of the Evergetis Monastery belong, and to what time can the definitive editing of the copy published by us be assigned? On the basis of the supplementary chapter in the prologue, we can conclude with certainty that the final redaction of the Evergetis Typikon known to us and its indicated additions appeared here under the immediate and nearest successor of the second hegumen, the Venerable Timothy. In setting forth the rule concerning the joint celebration of the memory of both ktetors, its compiler—who is also the final editor of the Typikon (p. 654)—says that Hegumen Timothy, while still alive, asked and bequeathed to him to honor the memory of his demise (*"while still living, he both foretold and enjoined upon us"* [«ἔτι ζῶν καὶ προεῖπε καὶ ἐπέσκεψε ἡμῖν»]). Thus, if the Evergetis Typikon had preserved for us the name of the third hegumen of the monastery and the year of the demise of his predecessor, we would very easily determine the time when the present Typikon received its current form in the copy of the Athens National Library. But since neither the one nor the other is known to us, we must turn to collateral, secondary data that in one way or another aid in a satisfactory resolution of the question posed, as close to the truth as possible. Of such data we have an adequate amount.

In the copy of the Evergetis Typikon published by us we find (p. 614), among other things, a reference to the Typikon of a monastery unknown to us of a certain Manuel ("the Typikon of the most venerable monastery of Manuel forbids" [*«τὸ τῆς εὐαγεστάτης μονῆς τοῦ Μανουὴλ Τυπικὸν ἀπαγορεύει»*]), in whom, as we suppose, one may see the renowned Byzantine Emperor Manuel (1143–1179) Komnenos [^69], son of Emperor John Komnenos. In another Typikon now known to us, namely...

=== LEAF p43 ===

*(Orig. p. XL)*

...that of the Monastery of the Holy Great Martyr Mamas of the year 1158, it is repeatedly recommended to conduct divine services according to the Evergetis Typikon. "The service ought to be sung," it is said here, "according to the Evergetis Synaxarion, according to the model and custom that have been followed" (*«Ἡ ἀκολουθία ὀφείλει ψάλλεσθαι, κατὰ τὸ Εὐεργετινὸν Συναξάριον, κατὰ τὸν παρακολουθήσαντα τύπον καὶ συνήθειαν»*) (p. 728, note 1), or, as in other places of the same Typikon: "according to the Evergetis Synaxarion currently in force among us" (*«κατὰ τὸ σήμερον παρ’ ἡμῖν ἐνεργοῦν Εὐεργετινὸν Συναξάριον»*) (p. 758, note 5; p. 760, note 4). Thus, the time of the editing of the Evergetis Typikon in its present composition falls in the years between 1143 and 1158, that is, from the beginning of the reign of Emperor Manuel Komnenos to the composition of the Typikon of the Monastery of St. Mamas. It is self-evident that in order to gain wide popularity even beyond the walls of its own monastery—such as the Evergetis Typikon, as we shall see below, actually enjoyed—not a little time was required; this could not have happened in two or three years. That is why, while assigning the composition of the Evergetis Typikon to the second half of the 11th century, we do not extend its final editing and exposition in the form in which it has come down to us in the manuscript of the library of the Athens National University beyond the limits of the first half of the 12th century. The paleographical data and the ornamentation of this very elegant and carefully written manuscript do not stand in contradiction with the indicated time [^70].

The entire Typikon of the Evergetis Monastery of the Mother of God, as we have said, consists of two parts: (a) the church Typikon in the proper sense of the word, and (b) the prologue, placed by the binder at the end of the manuscript. The prologue contains 43 chapters, an additional chapter on the commemoration of the second hegumen, Timothy, and an injunction...

=== LEAF p44 ===

*(Orig. p. XLI)*

...of the latter regarding the consumption of wine during Great Lent. In the 43 chapters, besides a brief history of the foundation of the Evergetis Monastery by Paul of Evergetis [^71] and its good ordering under the second hegumen, Timothy, rules are set forth concerning the daily cycle of divine services, food during fasts and on ordinary days, the conduct of monks at table, the celebration of feasts of the Lord and of the Theotokos, the officials in the monastery, their election, rights, and duties, the conduct of monks outside the church, rules regarding their number, clothing and footwear for them, baths for the monks, the commemoration of the ktetor and deceased brethren, the order of monastic tonsure, the distribution of alms at the gates, the exclusion of women from the monastery, the infirmary and the sick, and the reading of the Typikon at the beginning of each month. In a word, in these chapters are set forth all those rules that were necessary for every well-ordered monastery, and therefore it is completely natural that in this part the present Typikon is similar to the analogous prologues of the Typika known to us of Michael Attaleiates of 1077 and Empress Irene of 1114, with the similarity between these monuments being verbatim in places. As regards the exposition, all advantages are on the side of the Evergetis Typikon, since in it we find greater systematicity and consistency than in the others.

The Synaxarion or Typikon, as the above-cited title of this part of the Evergetis Typikon also shows, embraces the complete circle of the annual church divine services, with a detailed exposition of the order of services for every day of the year. This Typikon was compiled specifically for monastic use and is contrasted with the Typikon or liturgical...

=== LEAF p45 ===

*(Orig. p. XLII)*

...orders practiced in secular churches. Concerning the Matins Gospel on Monday of Holy Week, for example, it is noted: "at Matins the Gospel is not said, as is the custom for those in the world" (*«εἰς τὸν ὄρθρον εὐαγγέλιον οὐ λέγεται, καθὼς ἔθος τοῖς ἐν κόσμῳ»*) (p. 544). Hence in the Synaxarion of the Evergetis Monastery, after the exposition, for example, of the ordinary rite of the washing of feet on Great Thursday, there are also appointed "the prayers of the sacred washing [niptēr], which take place in monasteries" (*«αἱ εὐχαὶ τοῦ ἁγίου νιπτῆρος, αἱ γινομέναι ἐν τοῖς μοναστηρίοις»*) (p. 549). But above all, the compiler of the Typikon of the Evergetis Monastery, as he himself frankly admits (p. 442), followed in his exposition of church-liturgical orders the Typikon or Synaxarion of the Great Church ("following the model of the Great Church" [*«τῷ τύπῳ τῆς μεγάλης ἐκκλησίας ἀκολουθοῦντες»*], or "we found in the Synaxarion of the Great Church" [*«εὕρομεν ἐν τῷ Συναξαρίῳ τῆς μεγάλης ἐκκλησίας»*]). And indeed, collating the services of our Typikon with the same services according to the Typikon of the Great Church in the Patmos manuscript published by us, we note an immense similarity between both expositions. One must therefore recognize as an established fact that the compiler of the Evergetis Typikon created it on the basis of the patriarchal Constantinopolitan Typikon of the Great Church. In the manuscript of the Athens University Library now published by us, we consequently obtain a new source for our acquaintance with the liturgical practice of the Great Church in Constantinople, a source standing, as it were, on the boundary between patriarchal Typika and ktetorik Typika, thanks to which it acquires in our eyes an even greater significance and a greater scholarly value.

Besides the Typikon of the Great Church, the compiler of the Evergetis Typikon made use at times of the Typika of other monasteries—for example, the Typikon of the monastery built by Emperor Manuel Komnenos (p. 614), and the Typika of Mount Olympus (p. 443), which were held in great esteem in the East for the sanctity of life of their monastic dwellers and the strictness of their church and monastic rules; in certain cases, for the resolution of perplexities concerning church-liturgical practice, the compiler addressed questions to a certain hierarch Antony (p. 442) ("having also learned accurately through inquiry of the most venerable Antony, who served as bishop thereof" [*«ἔτι μὴν καὶ δι’ ἐρωτήσεως τοῦ ταύτης ἀρχιερατεύσαντος Ἀντωνίου τοῦ ὁσιωτάτου ἀκριβῶς ἀναмаθόντες»*]), who was a contemporary of the compiler of the Typikon, and whom the successor of Hegumen Timothy in the supplementary...

=== LEAF p46 ===

*(Orig. p. XLIII)*

...chapter of the prologue honors thus: "The all-revered sebastos and great doux, the monk Lord Antony" (*«Ὁ πανσέβαστος σεβαστὸς καὶ μέγας δοῦκας, ὁ μοναχὸς κῦρις Ἀντώνιος»*) (p. 654). The compiler of the Evergetis Synaxarion-Typikon was also well acquainted with the Typikon of the renowned Studite Monastery (*«τὸ Τυπικὸν τῆς περιωνύμου μονῆς τῶν Στουδίου»*), which was followed in liturgical practice by many other Byzantine monasteries (p. 603), and he in places explicitly [^72] and implicitly makes use of this Typikon (pp. 355, 512, 606, et al.) for his own Typikon, but at times enters into direct contradiction with it and makes enactments that run contrary to its practice. "And it is necessary to know," this Typikon notes, "that the remaining monasteries, as many as observe the Typikon of the renowned monastery of Studius, do not sing the Typika at the Liturgies, but after the dismissal of the Ninth Hour, then they sing the Typika to receive the antidoron. But we," remarks this Typikon, "because almost everyone daily partakes of the divine Mysteries, have judged it necessary to sing the Typika in their cells and for the brethren to rinse their mouths [diaklyzesthai] after the completion of the divine hierurgy, but briefly, and then to sing the Ninth Hour" (*«Δεῖ δὲ γινώσκειν, ὅτι τὰ λοιπὰ μοναστήρια, ὅσα τὸ Τυπικὸν κρατοῦσι τῆς περιωνύμου μονῆς τῶν Στουδίου, οὐ ψάλλουσι τὰ τυπικὰ ἐν ταῖς λειτουργίαις, ἀλλὰ μετὰ τὴν ἀπόλυσιν τῆς θ΄. ὥρας, τότε ψάλλουσι τὰ τυπικὰ λαμβάνειν τὸ ἀντίδωρον. Ἡμεῖς δέ... διὰ τὸ καθεκάστην σχεδὸν πάντας μεταλαμβάνειν τῶν θείων μυστηρίων, δέον ἐκρίναμεν ψάλλειν τὰ τυπικὰ ἐν τοῖς κελλίοις αὐτῶν καὶ διακλύεσθαι τοὺς ἀδελфоὺς μετὰ τὴν τῆς θείας ἱερουργίας συμπλήρωσιν, πλὴν συντόμως καὶ ἔπειτα ψάλλειν τὴν θ΄. ὥραν»*) (p. 603). Regarding food on the eve of the Nativity of Christ, if it falls on a Saturday, it is said: "and after the morning Liturgy we do not depart to the table according to the rule of the Studite, eating boiled pulse without oil and fruit, but in the narthex, after eating the broken bread [kataklaston] and rinsing our mouths after the holy gift, the cellarer distributes to all one piece of bread, and likewise one flask of wine, as the superior shall determine, and nothing more" (*«μετὰ δὲ τὴν τῆς πρωΐας λειτουργίαν οὐκ ἀπερχόμεθα ἐν τῇ τραπέζῃ, κατὰ τὸν τύπον τοῦ Στουδίτου, ἐσθίοντες ὄσπριον ἑξεστὸν ἄνευ ἐلاίου καὶ ὀпώρας, ἀλλὰ ἐν τῷ νάρθηκι, μετὰ τὸ φαγεῖν τὸ κατακλαστὸν καὶ διακλύσασθαι ἀπὸ τῆς ἁγίας δωρεᾶς, διανέμει ὁ κελλαρίτης πᾶσι πρὸς ἓν τεμάχιον ψωμοῦ, ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ πρὸς ἓν βαυκάλιον οἴνου, ὡς ἂν ὁρίσῃ ὁ προεστώς, καὶ πλέον οὐδέν»*) (p. 355).

By the fact noted by us of the independence and autonomy of the Typikon of the Evergetis Monastery, even in the presence of the universally recognized authority in Constantinople—the Studite Monastery—it is given us to understand that the Evergetis Monastery occupied by no means the lowest place in the rank of other Byzantine monasteries of its time. Natural, therefore, is also another no less...

=== LEAF p47 ===

*(Orig. p. XLIV)*

...curious phenomenon: that the Typikon of this monastery, after the example of other famous monasteries, claimed to extend the sphere of its influence beyond the monastery enclosure and to regulate the church-liturgical practice of other Constantinopolitan monasteries. Above we already noted that the Typikon of the Monastery of St. Mamas of 1158 directly prescribes that the monks guide themselves in liturgical practice by the Typikon or Synaxarion of the Evergetis Monastery. But the significance of the latter undoubtedly extended even further. If we leave aside the Synaxarion and direct our attention to its prologue, collating this latter with the Typikon of the Monastery of St. Mamas given by Theokharistos the Cappadocian, one cannot fail to notice the great similarity between both monuments, which in places and across entire chapters extends to positive identity. Undoubtedly, the compiler of the Typikon of the Monastery of St. Mamas, in recommending for liturgical practice the Synaxarion of the Evergetis Monastery, did not wish to diverge from his model in the organization of internal monastic coenobitic life either, and therefore borrowed these rules in their entirety from the Typikon of that same Evergetis Monastery. The similarity between both Typika is hence completely natural. The present curious fact acquires an important significance in our eyes also because the recognition of authority for the Evergetis Monastery took place, so to speak, before the eyes of representatives of the most important Constantinopolitan monasteries. Under the Typikon of the Monastery of St. Mamas there signed the monks of the Studite Monastery: Archimandrite and Hegumen Theophylact, Oikonomos Theodosius, Priest Akakios, Priest Barnabas, and monks Philaret, Michael, and Cyril; from the Monastery of the Peribleptos: Protopope Paul and monk Theodoulos; and Hegumen Athanasius of the Monastery of Philanthropos (p. 710).

Below we shall have occasion to speak of the almost complete identity—with the exception of the introduction (*πρόλογος*), Chapter 40, and the signatures—of the Typika of the Monastery of St. Mamas and of the Asia Minor Monastery of the Mother of God *tōn Hēliou Bōmōn, ētoi tōn Elegmōn*, which, in our opinion, confirms to a certain degree our thought concerning the dependence of this latter Typikon also on the Evergetis Typikon. But if in the fact indicated by us a depend-...

=== LEAF p48 ===

*(Orig. p. XLV)*

...-ence on the Evergetis Typikon can be demonstrated only with greater or lesser probability, the influence of the latter on the well-known "Rule, narrative of common life in the monastery of the Most Holy Theotokos the Directress [Hodēgētria], here written and handed down to you, my brethren and beloved children in the Lord, by me, the sinful and humble monk Sava" (*«Оуставь, сказание житиа собщаго вь манастыри прѣсветые Богородице наставнице, здѣ написаное и прѣданное вамъ братие и чеда моꙗ възлюбленаꙗ о Господѣ, мною грѣшьнымь и смиреннымь мніхомь Савою»*), published twice by Daničić and by the late archimandrite and namestnik of the Moscow Trinity-Sergius Lavra in the Serbian journal *Glasnik* [^73], is no longer subject to the slightest doubt. The similarity between original and copy here is astonishing and extends to the point that the order and number of chapters are preserved. For the rest, the Venerable Sava of Serbia in his Typikon expounds certain chapters in an abridged manner compared to the Evergetis Typikon, some he completely omits—for example, the chapter unnecessary for Athos: "Concerning the monastery being inaccessible to women, without distinction of the eminent and high-born" (*«περὶ τοῦ ἄβατον εἶναι τὴν μονὴν γυναιξὶν ἄνευ τῶν περιφανῶν τε καὶ ὑψηλῶν»*), etc. (p. 650)—while some, according to the needs of the given place, he compiles anew—for example, Chapter 42: "Concerning the cell of silence (of the skete-dweller Sava), which is in Karyes" (*«о мльчалницѣ (скетителиа Саввы), ꙗже вь Кареи»*) (*Typikon*, p. 54). The similarity between the Evergetis Typikon and the Typikon of the Venerable Sava of Serbia is especially striking to us in those chapters that contain historical information about the monastery itself and which, seemingly, ought to be the most original and independent. For an example, let us collate Chapter 3 of both Typika.

| Greek (Evergetis Typikon, Ch. 3) | Church Slavonic (Hilandar Typikon, Ch. 3) |
| :--- | :--- |
| Concerning the time of the demise of our holy father, the first ktetor, and that by testament he left the monastery in a small state to our venerable father, Lord Timothy, the second ktetor, | Concerning the time of the death of the venerable father Symeon and of me... |

=== LEAF p49 ===

*(Orig. p. XLVI)*

| Greek (Evergetis Typikon, Ch. 3) | Church Slavonic (Hilandar Typikon, Ch. 3) |
| :--- | :--- |
| ...and he brought it to the present magnificent state, and concerning the things acquired for it. Here then, as we have said, the blessed one, having gone forth and consecrated the place for a monastery, having built small cells, and having tonsured a few men along with me, and having accomplished those great and supernatural struggles, and having survived only five years, was translated to eternal blessedness, on the sixteenth of the month of April, in the year six thousand five hundred sixty-two [April 16, 1054], leaving me the monastery by testament in that small and humble state to which he himself had brought it; which indeed, having been left to me thus small and most humble, what a state it has reached instead through the intercessions of my Benefactress [Evergetis] and through his holy prayers, and how from obscurity and incompleteness it has been raised to this most magnificent and illustrious state; yea, moreover, what a church was erected from the very foundations to the Mother of God and our Lady the Benefactress, and cells that were built far better than the former ones, and books what kind and how many, likewise... | ...Where having stayed a short time with me, only eight months, yet having passed through great and supernatural ascetic struggles, he was translated to eternal blessedness, on the 13th day of the month of February, in the year 6708 (more correctly, 1200), having by testament left this monastery to me. And I received great sorrow and fear, partly from desolation, that is, from the small number of people in it; and partly from godless robbers, for I remained with a small number of brethren. In addition to that, the venerable man Hegumen Methodius departed, being the tenth himself. But how, by the good favor of the Mother of God our Directress, and by the prayers of our father Symeon, from obscure and small it grew magnificently into equality with the rest! For this monastery was built by us from the foundations entirely; and the church we only renewed and painted; and we provided books; likewise also sacred vessels, divine icons, and vestments; and curtains and all adornment; and further also for the bodily needs of the brethren as far as was possible... |

=== LEAF p50 ===

*(Orig. p. XLVII)*

| Greek (Evergetis Typikon, Ch. 3) | Church Slavonic (Hilandar Typikon, Ch. 3) |
| :--- | :--- |
| ...both sacred vessels and divine icons, but indeed also holy veils and furnishings, and simply all other adornment of the all-sacred church, and moreover also immovable possessions were acquired for the constitution and endurance of the monastery, as you know, and the truth shows, concerning which it is not necessary for us to speak; but it is permitted to him who wishes to learn, looking into the brebion, to be taught more clearly, while we, holding fast to what lies before us, must turn our discourse to the liturgical order. But, O God and Lord of all, and O all-hymned Mother of our Lord and God and Savior Jesus Christ, may these things become active and effective for us and for those after us, unto the end of the present age, through the honorable prayers of Thy servant, and our father and ktetor. Attention must therefore now be given to what is said; since we wish to begin whence it is better, and it is better from the hour that spreads light upon us, which is the first of the day. And I deem it completely necessary for those concerning works... | ...as far as was possible; and concerning these things, to you who wish to be informed with certainty, it will be possible from the chrysobull. But we incline ourselves to the following proposition of the discourse. But Thou, O God and Lord of all things, and O all-praised Mother of our Lord Jesus Christ, may these things be effective and operative; for us and those who come after us unto the end of this age, through the honorable prayers of Thy servant, and our father and ktetor Symeon, attending to what is spoken, for we have greater honors [^74]; and greater honors extend to us from the light of the first hour of the day. And it is fitting, it seems to me, to know concerning the work of light; on this account, from the light I begin to make the following explanation [^75]. |

<!-- END COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort5 -->

---

<!-- START COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort6 -->

=== LEAF p51 ===

*(Orig. p. XLVIII)*

...making the explanation of works of light, naturally to begin from light.

It is self-evident that in the chapters concerning the order of daily services, the election of officials, food during fasts, and the like, the textual resemblance in both Typika is even closer, and sometimes directly identical [^76].

=== LEAF p52 ===

*(Orig. p. XLIX)*

The fact noted by us of the dependence of the Typikon of the Athonite Hilandar Monastery upon that of Evergetis will become completely understandable to us if we recall in what relations to the Evergetis Monastery stood the ktetor of the Hilandar Monastery, Archbishop Sava, son of Stefan Nemanja, Grand Župan of Serbia. In the Life of this saint, "written by Domentijan, hieromonk of Hilandar on the Holy Mount Athos, disciple of Saint Sava", the Evergetis Monastery is twice termed "his own" [^77]; in this monastery the Venerable Sava stopped each time he came on business to Constantinople [^78]; from the God-given gold pointed out to him by "a certain woman of goodly countenance and devout", Saint Sava "gave one part in Tsargrad to the monastery of the Most Holy Theotokos Evergetis" [^79]; hither, to this monastery, Archbishop Sava "first having come made obeisance" [^80] upon his second return from his journey to Palestine, and so forth. All this speaks to us of the fact that the Venerable Sava of Serbia had special and vivid ties with the Constantinopolitan Evergetis Monastery, which render completely natural the fact of the dependence of the Hilandar Typikon upon that of Evergetis.

If, however, the Hilandar Typikon represents a translation of that of Evergetis, then we have full opportunity from this now-established fact to draw several new and scientifically very important conclusions, which introduce very...

=== LEAF p53 ===

*(Orig. p. L)*

...substantial corrections into the history of the translation of the Jerusalem Typikon into the Serbian language. "The first positive reports concerning Jerusalem orders of service in the Slavic world," says I. D. Mansvetov, "come from the South Slavic lands and are associated with the name of Sava of Serbia. For the Hilandar Monastery established by him, he provided an Ordo which may be considered the first Slavic monument of the Jerusalem Ordo" [^81]. As the chief foundation for this assertion, Prof. Mansvetov took the circumstance that in the Ordo of Sava of Serbia "mention is made of vigils", which "shall be held on Sundays, and Dominical feasts, and on the commemorations of other great saints, concerning which the Synaxarion shall instruct" [^82]. But now this foundation turns out to be untrustworthy and irrelevant. To Chapter 8 "On Vigils" of the Hilandar Typikon corresponds Chapter 8 "On vigils, when they ought to take place" (*περὶ τῶν ἀγρυπνιῶν, πότε ὀφείλουσι γίνεσθαι*) of the Evergetis Typikon, which literally reads thus: "And vigils also according to the due pattern ought to take place both on every Sunday feast and on commemorations of the other saints, which we shall learn from the Synaxarion" (*«Καὶ τὰς ἀγρυπνίας δὲ κατὰ τὸν τύπον ὀφειλόμενον γίνεσθαι ἔν τε πάσῃ κυριακῇ ἑορτῇ καὶ ἐν λοιπῶν ἁγίων μνήμαις, ἃς ἐκ τοῦ Συναξαρίου μαθησόμεθα»*) (p. 624). Taking into account the reference of the Evergetis Typikon to the Synaxarion or Typikon of the church service, we turn to this latter and find here the article "On the vigil of Sundays" (*«περὶ τῆς ἀγρυπνίας τῶν κυριακῶν»*) (p. 607), from which we become convinced that in the given article the discussion concerns not the all-night vigil in the present meaning of this word, but an *agrypnia*, which stood in no connection whatever with the ordinary orders of the Vespers and Matins divine services, and constituted an inalienable component of early Christian ecclesiastical-liturgical practice throughout the entire Orthodox East [^83] in the period of time prior to the introduction here of the Jerusalem Ordo, connected by its origin with the monastery of the Venerable Sabbas...

=== LEAF p54 ===

*(Orig. p. LI)*

...the Sanctified. Echoes of this *agrypnia* in contemporary divine service we can see in our paraklesis or moleben, as well as in the service celebrated nowadays before the beginning of Paschal Matins and in colloquial speech called the "Midnight Office".

Thus, we not only do not find in the Hilandar Typikon of the Venerable Sava of Serbia any "positive reports concerning Jerusalem orders", but not even faint traces of acquaintance on the part of its compiler with the Ordo of the monastery of the Venerable Sabbas the Sanctified. On the contrary, from the cited chapter "On Vigils" and from other places of the same Typikon (chapters 35, 36 on pp. 44–47, cf. Everg. Typ. ch. 35 and 36 on pp. 646–648, 446–448, etc.), we gain firm conviction that concerning these orders, at the publication of his Typikon (which was undoubtedly prior to his journey to Jerusalem), the Venerable Sava had not heard, and that the original liturgical order in the Hilandar monastery was very probably the same that was practiced in other Constantinopolitan monasteries of that time, or, even closer to the truth, that very one which the Venerable Sava of Serbia had studied and brought out from "his own" Evergetis monastery. References in the Hilandar Typikon to the "Synaxarion" (*Συναξάριον, ἤτοι Τυπικὸν τῆς ἐκκλησιαστικῆς ἀκολουθίας*) in such expressions as: "and concerning the evening singing the Synaxarion clearly shows" (*καὶ τὸν ἑσπερινὸν δὲ ὕμνον ἡ τοῦ Συναξαρίου διάταξις... ἀριδήλως παρίστησιν*) (*Typikon of St. Sava*, p. 13, cf. p. 620), or "as the Synaxarion declares" (*καθὼς τὸ Συναξάριον διέξεισιν*) (*ibid.*, p. 15, cf. p. 621), or "and that concerning the holy fasts, examine in the Synaxarion with scrutiny, and as it shows, so also preserve" (*περὶ δὲ τῆς τῶν ἁγίων νηστειῶν ἀκολουθίας τὸ Συναξάριον ὑμῖν ἀκριβέστατα ὑποθήσει, καθὸ καὶ ποιητέον ἐν αὐταῖς πάντοτε*) (*ibid.*, p. 18, cf. p. 624), etc., with their almost complete resemblance in phrasing in both Typika, provide foundations for such a supposition. There will therefore be nothing surprising if someone among subsequent scholars manages somewhere to discover a Slavic, or, more correctly, Slavo-Serbian translation also of the Synaxarion or Typikon of the church services of the Evergetis Monastery, of course with the corresponding and necessary additions. We know that this Typikon was accepted as the regulator of divine services...

=== LEAF p55 ===

*(Orig. p. LII)*

...in the monastery of St. Mamas; it is still more natural, given the above-indicated constant ties of the two monasteries, to see it in the same role also in the Serbian Hilandar monastery.

An indirect, but at the same time sufficiently weighty foundation for the proposition that the Hilandar monastery until the 14th century was completely unacquainted with the orders of the Jerusalem divine service and in its liturgical practice was guided by its own special Typikon, can be found in the manuscript Nikodim Typikon of the Belgrade Library. From the extensive preface to it, written in the year 1319, we learn that the Serbian Archbishop Nikodim, who prior to that was hegumen of the Hilandar monastery, for the first time became acquainted with the Jerusalem Ordo during his stay in Constantinople during the patriarchate of Niphon I (1313–1315), through the Patriarchs of Jerusalem Athanasius and of Antioch, "holding the Typikon according to the custom of the holy city of Jerusalem, according to the Ordo of Saint Sabbas the Desert-Dweller". Upon his accession to the archiepiscopal throne of the Serbian Church, Archbishop Nikodim conceived the plan to make a translation of this Ordo into the Serbian language and introduce it into the liturgical practice of his Church. "I sent to the imperial city to the monastery of the holy Forerunner and Baptist John," says Archbishop Nikodim, "and there was brought to me this Jerusalem Typikon, and I translated it into our tongue from the letters of the Greek language" [^84]. If at Hilandar they had known of the Jerusalem orders already during the life of the Venerable Sava, then there would have been no need anew to translate the Jerusalem Ordo, and moreover by a person who was well acquainted with the orders of the Serbian Athonite Hilandar Monastery. In its turn, there would have been no need afterwards, in the year 1331, for Gervasius, hegumen of the Hilandar Monastery, to give an order to a certain Roman [^85] to copy the Nikodim manuscript of the Typikon for use in the liturgical practice of this monastery.

The Typikon of the Evergetis Monastery is published by us almost entirely, and with the observance even of peculiarities in the ortho-...

=== LEAF p56 ===

*(Orig. p. LIII)*

...graphy of certain words (e.g., *διακινήσιμος* instead of *διακαινήσιμος*, etc.). We allowed ourselves to abridge only the troparia, prokeimena, and antiphons, which in the text present nothing special compared to those accepted in the present-day Typikon. The prayers in the rite of the washing of feet (p. 549) we collated with similar prayers according to the *Euchologion* of Goar, edit. Venet. 1730, and likewise, in view of their identity, omitted them in our edition.

VIII. The renowned "imperial" (*βασιλικὴ μονή*) Pantokrator Monastery, despite the grandeur and magnificence of its structures, which astonished and struck pilgrims of antiquity, and despite the significance which it possessed in the rank of other famous Constantinopolitan monasteries, does not possess to the present time a detailed historical sketch. In the very latest works on the study of "Byzantine churches and monuments of Constantinople", the old opinion is still repeated [^86] that the founder of this monastery was the spouse of Emperor John Komnenos...

=== LEAF p57 ===

*(Orig. p. LIV)*

...Irene, in monasticism Xene, who reposed in the year 1124, while concerning the monastery there are communicated, despite every effort of the researcher thereof, reports far from complete. On the foundation of the Typi-...

=== LEAF p58 ===

*(Orig. p. LV)*

...kon of the Pantokrator Monastery of 1136, we now establish the fact that the ktetor of this monastery was Emperor John Komnenos, who wished by the establishment in it of magnificent churches and model charitable institutions: 1) to express gratitude to God and the Most Holy Mother of God for those manifold mercies which he received from Them, whether in the form of brilliant victories over the Persians, Scythians, Dalmatians, Dacians, and other peoples, or in the form of unexpected and fortunate discoveries of insidious plots and dangers on the part of his internal and external enemies, friends, and even close relatives (p. 656); 2) to acquire constant intercessors in prayer for his sins and for the sins of the other numerous members of his house (p. 657), among those living and departed (pp. 662–663) (among the latter the emperor names his father, grandmother, mother, and spouse [*τῆς μακαριωτάτης δεσποίνης καὶ συζύγου μου*] [pp. 662, 679, 680, 681]); and 3) during his lifetime to create for himself a memorial and designate a place of burial for himself, his wife, his son Alexios, and members of his family (667, 680, 681, 663). In view of such elevated goals of the given construction, John Komnenos spared not the wealth of the imperial treasury and established in all respects a model and even brilliant monastery.

On the basis of narratives in the Synaxarion, brief and fragmentary notes on the Pantokrator Monastery by our ancient pilgrim eyewitnesses, and descriptions in the Typikon itself of the litanies with the icon of the Mother of God Hodegetria and the all-night vigil at the tombs of the imperial family and in the church of the Mother of God the Merciful [*Eleousa*] (p. 677), celebrated weekly on Friday and in the night toward Saturday, the picture of the layout of the present monastery is drawn in the following form. The monastery was built not far from the Church of the Holy Apostles, on the elevated fourth hill, in the 10th densely populated (*τὸ συγκεχυμένον τῶν οἰκήσεων*) (p. 695) quarter of the city, with a beautiful view over the surroun-...

=== LEAF p59 ===

*(Orig. p. LVI)*

...ding area [^87] and occupied a rather considerable area, encircled by high walls with two gates, of which the "rear" [^88] or "second" [^89] led into the Pantokrator Monastery (*πύλη τῆς μονῆς*) and were accessible only to men (p. 671), while the "front" or "first", being near the door to the Church of the Mother of God the Merciful (*ἀπὸ τῆς πύλης τοῦ ναοῦ τῆς Ἐλεούσης*), were opened according to need sometimes also for women, when, for instance, they came to the monastery for the burial of monk-relatives, or for their commemoration (p. 671). As the façade of the monastery served the façade of the Church of the Mother of God the Merciful. "Above the gates" of its narthex was also "the Savior fashioned in mosaic, great in form and lofty" (*τὴν εἰκόνα τοῦ Χριστοῦ, τὴν πρὸ τῆς πύλης τοῦ νάρθηκος*). On the open courtyard before the church stood a phiale (*φιάλη*) with water for quenching the thirst of travelers and pilgrims (pp. 677, 679).

The principal church in the monastery was dedicated to the All-Powerful Wisdom of God (*τῇ παντοδυνάμῳ σοφίᾳ σου*) (pp. 656, 702) or the Almighty [Pantokrator] and had, besides the sanctuary apse with a niche (*μύαξ*), also two lateral apses (*δεξιὰν ἁψίδα καὶ ἀρι-...*

=== LEAF p60 ===

*(Orig. p. LVII)*

...-στεράν*), which were called diakonika (*ἐν τοῖς διακονικοῖς*). On the west the church was bounded by an inner narthex (*νάρθηξ*), having several entrance doors, of which the Typikon mentions only the Beautiful Gates (*ὡραῖαι πύλαι*), and an exonarthex (*ἐξωνάρθηξ*), and above was covered by a dome (*τροῦλλον*) with four small cupolas (*ἐπιθόλια*) at the sides. The sanctuary was separated from the church by a barrier (*κιγκλίδες*) (p. 658) and an iconostasis (*τέμπλον*), at the sides of which were two small iconostases (*μικρὸν τέμπλον*), separating from the church the lateral apses (*τὸ δεξιὸν τέμπλον*). In the church were several icons, namely: in the sanctuary apse, icons of the Pantokrator, the Resurrection, and the Crucifixion; in the right apse—of the Mystical Supper (*ὁ Δεῖπνος*); in the left—of the Washing of Feet (*ὁ Νιπτήρ*); above the Beautiful Gates—of the Dormition of the Mother of God; and several icons in the dome. In addition, icons were in the large and small iconostases and on two proskynetaria (*δύο προσκυνήσεις*) at the sides of the church (pp. 660–661). The walls were lined with stasidia (pp. 659–660), which, according to the direction of the ekklesiarches, were occupied by the brethren according to seniority. In the church for its illumination there was a large choros (*ὁ χορός*) or metal circle with 16 vessels (*κρατῆρες*) for oil, which on great feasts were replaced by polycandela (*πολυκάνδηλα*) [^90]. Before the icons hung lamps (*λαμπάδες*), stood candlesticks for a single candle (*μανουάλια* and *κηроπήγια*), for three candles (*τρικάνδηλον*), and on great feasts before the icon of the Savior for 12 candles (*δωδεκαφώτια*) (pp. 660–661).

The second church in the name of the Mother of God the Merciful [^91] was similar in its arrangement to the first, but had on the west side only one narthex, in which was a mosaic icon...
