<!-- START COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort7 -->

=== LEAF p61 ===

*(Orig. p. LVIII)*

...of the Mother of God (*ἐν τῇ διὰ μουσείου εἰκόνι τῆς Ἐλεούσης* [in the mosaic icon of the Eleousa]); opposite her, immediately above the door of the narthex (*ἄνωθεν τῆς πύλης τοῦ νάρθηκος*) leading into the church, another icon of the Forerunner and a phiale (*ἑτέρα φιάλη*) with running water for the people present at the divine service. Into the church from various sides seven doors led, above which seven icons hung (*ἕτερα κηρία ἑπτά, καθ’ ἑκάστην δηλονότι εἰκόνα, τὴν ἱσταμένην ἐπάνω τῶν ἐν τῷ ναῷ διαφόρων πυλῶν, κηρίον ἕν* [seven other tapers, namely one taper according to each icon standing above the various gates in the church]). From the narthex through a passage (*ἡ εἰσοδοέξοδος*), closed by a door, one exited into open porticos (*εἰς τοὺς ἐμβόλους*), adjoining directly the municipal portico [^92] (*τοὺς παρακειμένους τῷ δημοσίῳ ἐμβόλῳ*). Side porticos (*ἐν τῷ ἐξωτερικῷ τοῦ ναοῦ πλαγίῳ*) were also arranged by the church. In the square before the church stood a phiale with water for those weary from the journey [^93]. The adornments of the church and furnishings were the very same as in the church of the Pantokrator (pp. 677–678).

=== LEAF p62 ===

*(Orig. p. LIX)*

Between the two named churches (*μεταξὺ τοῦ τοιούτου ναοῦ* (that is, of the Mother of God) *καὶ τῆς μονῆς*) was built in the manner of a Heroon (*ἐν σχήματι Ἡρῴου* [in the form of a hero's shrine/mausoleum]) a small church (*εὐκτήριον* [oratory]) in the name of the Archistrategos Michael, intended to serve as the imperial burial vault (p. 677). This church was round with three apses: in two of them (*εἰς τὰς δύο τοῦ Ἡρῴου ἁψῖδας*) were...

=== LEAF p63 ===

*(Orig. p. LX)*

...icons of the Crucifixion and Resurrection, and in the third (*εἰς τὴν ἑτέραν ἁψῖδα*) the Holy Sepulcher (*τὸν ἅγιον τάφον*), with an icon above it of Christ appearing to the Marys, and it had a dome covering (p. 681). A door led into the church to the imperial tombs (*εἰς τὴν εἰσοδοέξοδον*), above which stood an icon of Christ. Around the church were hung a multitude of vessels with oil or polykandila (pp. 678, 681).

=== LEAF p64 ===

*(Orig. p. LXI)*

In the monastery, according to the Typikon, there are to be no more than 80 monks, of whom 50 constantly (*ἀδιαπάστως*) abide in the church at prayer, while the remainder, distinguished by high qualities of mind and character (*τὴν φρόνησιν πολιοί, σεμνοὶ τὸ ἦθος* [venerable in wisdom, grave in manner]), adorned with virtues, zealous in the fulfillment of their duties, are appointed to various monastic offices. From these persons are chosen the oikonomoi, the ekklisiarches, the skeuophylakes, the chartophylakes, those in charge of the hospital (*νοσοκόμος*) and the hostel (*ξενοδόχος* [xenon-master]), as well as the docheiarioi and granary-keepers (*δοχειάριοι καὶ ὡρειάριοι*). The number of church servitors was comparatively small: two domestikoi, 6 priests, 6 deacons, two canonarchs, two paradomestikoi, and one timekeeper (*ὁ ὡроλόγος*). Moreover, the number of deacons and priests, according to the abbot's wish, could also be increased. Laymen were by no means admitted into the staff of monastic servants. According to the Typikon, "monks alone had the right to inhabit their own paradise (*αὐτοὶ μόνοι τὸ οἰκεῖον παράδεισον κατοικήσουσι*), to cultivate and till it." Counted among the staff of servants were assistants to the ekklisiarchai (*παραεκκλησιάρχαι*), under-oikonomoi (*παροικονόμοι*), bakers, gardeners, cooks, etc. Four persons from the servant staff (*ἀπὸ τῆς τῶν δουλευτῶν μοίρας*) were appointed to the most menial tasks (*πρὸς τὰς ἐσχάτας διακονίας*), or, as the compiler of the Typikon corrects himself, "to the first and godlike" (*τὰς πρώτας καὶ ἰσοθέους*), namely: to wash the linen and outer garments for the brethren, to prepare baths (*τὰ σώματα ῥύψουσι τῷ λουτρῷ*), to minister unto the sick, and to wash rolling pins, dishes, and jars (*ξέστας καὶ πίνακας καὶ χύτρας*) (p. 671; cf. p. 696).

In a different manner and on entirely different principles, reminiscent, according to the description of Archbishop Antony [^94], of the organization of the clergy...

=== LEAF p65 ===

*(Orig. p. LXII)*

...at the Church of Hagia Sophia, the clergy rolls at the Church of the Mother of God the Merciful [*Eleousa*] were established. Here a total of 50 clerics was appointed in the following composition: 8 priests, of whom two presided weekly over the remaining priests (*πρωτεύσει τῶν λοιπῶν*) and were considered the senior ones; 10 deacons; 2 domestikoi; two laosynaktai; 16 chanters; four canonarchs; orphans or kandilaptai (*ὀрфаνούς* [^95] *ἤτοι κανδηλάπτας*) 8, and a further 4 supernumerary orphans (*περισσούς*) in the event that any of the regular kandilaptai (*τῶν ἐμβάθμων ὀρφανῶν*) should vacate his post (p. 678). These supernumerary orphans temporarily performed the role of scribes and rendered various kinds of service in the church to the numerous pilgrims, were present at divine services on solemn feast days, taking part in various kinds of ceremonies, and oversaw together with the women the church and the buildings adjoining it. Over and above the regular roll of clerics, there were appointed four women, venerable in age and upright of conduct (*γυναῖκας σεμνὰς καὶ κοσμίας καὶ τῇ ἡλικίᾳ καὶ τῷ ἤθει πεπιλιωμένας*), whose duty was to serve at the church and care for its splendor, as well as for the porticos and outbuildings situated around it (*καὶ τὰ περὶ αὐτόν*). These four women rotated among themselves in such a manner: two served in the church for one week, and the other two the following week; but on Friday evening, when a great concourse of people was expected on the occasion of the vigil before...

=== LEAF p66 ===

*(Orig. p. LXIII)*

...the icon of the Mother of God Hodegetria, all four were obliged to be present in the church in person for various kinds of service to the pilgrims (p. 678). These very women, as we think [^96], Archbishop Antony calls "myrrh-bearers" [^97], by analogy of those duties that they performed both in the church of Jerusalem at the Sepulcher of the Lord, and in Constantinople at Hagia Sophia and in the other most important churches of this city. All clerics were likewise divided into two choirs: one half fulfilled their duties for one week, and the other half the next (p. 678).

Demanding that the clergy of the Church of the Holy Mother of God the Merciful be "chosen" (*κλῆρον ἐκλελεγμένον, μερίδα τιμίαν, ἀφωσίωμα κάλλιστον* [a chosen clergy, an honorable portion, a most noble offering]) (p. 657), and the priests and deacons, in particular, worthy of every respect (*ἀξιολογώτεροι*) (p. 678), the emperor sought in every way to elevate and improve their material circumstances. According to the emperor's testament, the clergy received remuneration in stipend [*roga*] and bread (*ῤόγας καὶ ἀννόνας*) [^98] in such amount: the first two priests, 15 hyperpyra coins each (*ὑπέрπυρα*) [^99] and 25 maritime modioi of bread each; six ordinary priests, 14 hyperpyra each and 25 maritime modioi of bread each; 10 deacons, 13 such coins each and 24 maritime modioi of bread each; as much was received by the two dome-...

=== LEAF p67 ===

*(Orig. p. LXIV)*

...stikoi and the two laosynaktai; 16 chanters—12 hyperpyra each and 20 maritime modioi of bread each; 4 canonarchs—6 hyperpyra each and 15 such modioi of bread each; as much was received by each of the seven kandilaptai or orphans; and four scribes received 4 hyperpyra each and 12 maritime modioi of bread each (p. 679). Salaries over the course of time could also be increased. To the lower servitors at the church payment was made weekly (*ἑβδομαδικόν*) at one hyperpyron, and on Friday for the vigil as much again. In addition to the indicated salaries, bonuses were appointed for the entire clergy on the feasts of the Annunciation and the Meeting of the Lord: 2 hyperpyra on each feast; on the days of commemoration of the emperor's father, the emperor himself, and his spouse: three hyperpyra; and on the days of commemoration of the emperor's mother and grandmother: two hyperpyra. In the event that the emperor's son, Tsar [Emperor] Alexios, should die and be buried, as promised, in the monastery, then at his commemoration the clergy shall receive an honorarium likewise of 3 hyperpyra (pp. 679–680).

At the Church in honor of the Archangel Michael there was no special clergy. Services were celebrated chiefly by the monks of the monastery, with the participation in certain cases also of the clerics of the Church of the Mother of God (p. 679). This church was administered by the monastery's ekklisiarches, and all care for it was laid by the ktetor upon the monastery (p. 681).

The two distinct churches of one and the same monastery not only had separate rolls of clerics, but also completely different church typika (*ἐκκλησιαστικὸν Τυπικόν*). For the monastery, according to the custom of other ktetors, the emperor composed (*κατὰ τὸ γεγονὸς παρ’ ἡμῶν ἐκκλησιαστικὸν Τυπικόν* [according to the church Typikon produced by us]) his own Typikon (pp. 657, 664), the detailed exposition of which remains unknown to us, and enacted that it be followed in liturgical practice; whereas upon the clergy of the Church of the Mother of God the Merciful he enjoins the obligation, when celebrating divine services, to adhere, after the model of the great church in the palace, to the Order of the Hagiopolite. "The Order of the Hagiopolite ought to be chanted," writes the emperor in the Typikon, "according to the model of the great church in the palace" (*«Ὀφείλει ψάλλεσθαι ἡ ἀκολουθία τοῦ Ἁγιοπολίτου, κατὰ τὸν τύπον τοῦ ἐν τῷ παλατίῳ μεγάλου ναοῦ»*) (p. 678). This last remark is for us in the highest degree precious. It provides to a certain degree the key to resolving the question as to whence Emperor John Komnenos takes for...

=== LEAF p68 ===

*(Orig. p. LXV)*

...the clergy of the Church of the Mother of God the Merciful founded by him the model. This on the one hand. On the other hand, from this we now obtain a clear and distinct notion of what the *"Akolouthia tou Hagiopolitou"* [^100] (*Ἀκολουθία τοῦ Ἁγιοπολίτου* [Order of the Hagiopolite]), or *"Akolouthia kata ten paradosin tou Hagiopolitou"* (*Ἀκολουθία κατὰ τὴν παράδοσιν τοῦ Ἁγιοπολίτου* [Order According to the Tradition of the Hagiopolite]), or, finally, simply *"Ho Hagiopolites"* (*Ὁ Ἁγιοπολίτης* [The Hagiopolite]) is, excerpts from which we frequently encounter in various liturgical books of ancient Byzantium. Several such excerpts for an example we cited in the footnotes to the Typikon of the Great Church, published by us from the Patmos manuscript (p. 58, note 1; p. 59, note 1, etc.). Now it is beyond doubt that by this name was designated the court Church Typikon, practiced also in certain imperial monasteries, as for example in the Pantokrator and, in all probability, in others.

The liturgical details of the Pantokrator Typikon are not devoid of interest, but, unfortunately, they chiefly concern the daily cycle of divine services. Remarks concerning the annual festal cycle in it are quite meager.

The monastic day in ordinary times began at midnight, when the brethren awakened from sleep for the morning service [Matins], but on feast days the service began even earlier, in the fifth hour of the night. The awakener (*ὁ ἀφυπνιστής*) with the exclamation: **"Bless and pray for me, father,"** asked the blessing of the abbot and then struck the great assembly semantron (*μέγα συνακτήριον*). The brethren at the sound gathered into the narthex of the church for the Midnight Office, during which the **"Blameless"** [Ps. 118] were sung, divided into three staseis, wherein, after each stasis, they pronounced the Trisagion and two midnight troparia with a theotokion (p. 658).

Upon the conclusion of the Midnight Office, from the narthex the brethren proceeded into the church, where each, having prayed before the Royal Doors, took his appointed place assigned to him by the ekklisiarches. To converse in church, or to begin chanting or reading...

=== LEAF p69 ===

*(Orig. p. LXVI)*

...before the leader on the kliros, or to instruct him, even if one knew the order better, was strictly forbidden. In extreme cases questions were permitted only during the kathismata, but with observance of due silence (p. 660).

The priest on duty, stopping before the sanctuary, receives the censer from the ekklisiarches and, having censed thrice before the chancel barrier (*ἔμπροσθεν τῶν κιγκλίδων*), withdraws into the sanctuary and censes thrice each side of the holy table [altar]. Upon exiting from the sanctuary, the priest, preceded by a reader with bared head and with a lamp in hand, proceeded into the Church of the Archistrategos Michael unto the imperial tombs, then censed the icon of the Pantokrator, the other icons and holy places in the church, and all the brethren standing in the stasidia. During this censing, during the lifetime of the emperor, the monks were obliged to chant for him the Trisagion and Psalms 19 and 100, as also now, and the troparia: **"Save, O Lord"** [Troparion of the Cross], **"Thou Who wast lifted up upon the Cross"**, and the theotokion: **"O speedy protection, anticipate us before we are enslaved"**, and 15 times **"Lord, have mercy"**. After the death of the emperor, the reading of the 19th Psalm alone and the indicated troparia was retained for the living emperor, while for the deceased the monks were obliged to chant another Trisagion, Psalm 6, the troparion: **"With the saints give rest"** with the theotokion: **"Thee, a wall and a haven"**, and the prayer: **"Remember, O Lord, our deceased Orthodox sovereigns and ktetors"**. After the conclusion of the censing of the church, the priest took his stand before the Royal Doors, censed them, pronouncing the exclamation: **"Glory to the holy [and consubstantial, and life-creating, and undivided Trinity]"**, and the Six Psalms [Hexapsalmos] were read in the midst of the church. The brethren without noise quietly repeated the words of the psalms being read. After the Six Psalms they chanted either **"God is the Lord"** or **"Alleluia"**, and then the kathismata were read (pp. 658–659). The hypakoe, as well as the other hymns, the chanters sang always before the Royal Doors (p. 660). The First Hour was joined to Matins.

The Third and Sixth Hours preceded the Liturgy, while the Ninth was celebrated after it (p. 664).

For the Liturgy [^101] prosphoras of pure flour (*σεμιδάλεως* [fine flour / semidalis]) of 4 exagia weight were prepared for each day: two larger ones—in...

=== LEAF p70 ===

*(Orig. p. LXVII)*

...honor of the Lord and the Mother of God, a third in honor of the saint of the day, and four smaller ones for the commemoration of the emperor's father, grandmother, mother, and spouse. From this same flour bread was baked also for the abbot. The prosphoras, cut into portions [antidoron], were distributed to the monks. On Saturdays, in addition to this, there were prepared from wheat flour (*ἐξ ἀλεύρου*) also other prosphoras for the commemoration of the chief members of the imperial house, both living and deceased, and a separate prosphora for the grandson Alexios, son of the Sebastokrator Isaac (pp. 662–663). For the same purpose on Saturdays three dishes of kolyvo were prepared. The prosphoras and kolyvo for the commemoration of the persons of the imperial family constituted an indispensable accompaniment of the liturgies also on the days of the annual commemorations: on Meatfare Saturday, on Cheesefare Saturday, and on Saturday of Pentecost (p. 663). For the commemoration of all the persons of the imperial house named in the Typikon at the proskomedia and liturgy—both living and deceased—diptychs were kept in the monastery (*ibid.*).

The day of monastic life concluded with Vespers and Compline, between which, according to the Typikon of the monastery, supper was appointed (pp. 667–668).

It goes without saying that for us of far greater interest in scholarly terms must be those remarks of the present Typikon which in one way or another concern the annual festal cycle of divine services, and in particular the divine services celebrated in the churches of the Mother of God and of the Holy Archangel Michael according to the typikon of the main church in the imperial palace. Such remarks, to our deep regret, are very few, and in their exposition they are distinguished by brevity and even fragmentariness. But this prompts us to value all the higher the worth and significance of these data for the history of Byzantine church ritual. And so, what then does the Pantokrator Typikon provide us in this regard?

<!-- END COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort7 -->

---

<!-- START COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort8 -->

=== LEAF p71 ===

*(Orig. p. LXVIII)*

Here in the first place must be placed the solemn all-night vigil with pannychis (*ἀγρυπνία μετὰ παννυχίδος* [vigil with pannychis]) instituted by the emperor from Friday evening throughout the entire night into Saturday of each week. This pannychis was celebrated for the health of the emperor and the living members of his house, as well as for the deceased. The vigil was preceded by a solemn litany or procession of the cross, in which were carried out the church standards (*σίγνον* [*signon*]), icons, and preeminently the icon of the *Mother of God, called Hodegetria* [^102],...

=== LEAF p72 ===

*(Orig. p. LXIX)*

...venerated by the emperor and preserved in the palace [^103]. In the celebration of the litany, the clergy and a multitude of people of both sexes took part. Coming out from the Church of the Mother of God to meet them was a solemn...

=== LEAF p73 ===

*(Orig. p. LXX)*

...procession of its clergy, and with profound reverence and due honors (*μετ' εὐλαβείας ἁπάσης καὶ τῆς προσηκούσης τιμῆς* [with all reverence and fitting honor]) greeted the procession of the cross and the venerated icon. After this reception, all entered into the church, where the people and the standard-bearers (*τὰ σίγνα καὶ ὁ λαός* [the standards and the people]) pronounced an earnest petition "for the forgiveness and remission of the transgressions of the ktetors," and **"Lord, have mercy"** was chanted 15 times for each standard (*παρ' ἑκάστου σίγνου*). From the Church of the Mother of God the litany proceeded into the imperial burial vault unto the holy tomb (*ἐπὶ τὰ ἔμπροσθεν πρὸς τὴν ἁγίαν σορόν* [forward toward the holy tomb]) (p. 679), where were celebrated the memorial Trisagion (*Τρισάγιον*) or a brief litiya and the supplication of the people for the sins of the ktetors with the chanting of **"Lord, have mercy"** 30 times.

=== LEAF p74 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXI)*

The icon of the Mother of God Hodegetria was brought inside the Church of the Archangel Michael and placed near the imperial tombs (*πλησίον τῶν τάφων ἡμῶν* [near our tombs]) (p. 681), while the standards and other icons either remained in the Church of the Mother of God, or even in its porticos and galleries, illuminated all night by lamps in lanterns and candles (p. 678). The monastic clergy of the Pantokrator Monastery and the clergy of the Church of the Mother of God jointly, in the presence of numerous people, celebrated a memorial agrypnia [vigil] throughout the entire night, and in the morning served a memorial Liturgy (p. 681). The full staff of clerics, not excluding the kandilaptai or orphans fulfilling the duties of scribes, and the women myrrh-bearers, was present at this divine service (p. 678). Not exempt from this vigil were those serving in the philanthropic institutions of the Pantokrator Monastery, the sick and the aged capable of movement who were in its almshouse. The latter spend the night in the Church of the Mother of God and together with the rest of the people sing a litiya consisting of the troparia: **"Remember Thy servants, O Lord, forasmuch as Thou art good"**, **"With the saints give rest"**, and **"Fervent protectress"**, of an earnest litany with the chanting of **"Lord, have mercy"** 40 times, and the conclusion: **"May God grant beatitude to the ktetors"** (p. 685). The churches of the Mother of God, with the porticos and galleries adjoining it, and of the Archistrategos Michael were abundantly illuminated, and around the latter all night burned vessels (*κρατῆρες* [*krateres*]) with oil or polykandila (p. 678). All those present at the divine service held in their hands lighted large torches (*μεγάλα φατλία* [*megala phatlia*]) or tapers (p. 685). After the Liturgy, to the worshippers, for the reinforcement of their strength and as a memorial of the deceased, were given kolyvo, prosphoras, and wine (*ibid.*), and to the participants in the procession—clerics, monks, those serving in the institutions, the sick, and the aged—monetary rewards (pp. 681–682, 685, et al.). Then, after the pronunciation of an earnest litany for the emperor and the chanting of **"Lord, have mercy"**, the procession of the cross with the icon of the Mother of God Hodegetria, accompanied likewise by numerous people, returned from the monastery back (p. 681).

Similar litanies and memorial vigils were to be celebrated also on the annual commemorations of the emperor's spouse Irene, of himself, and of his heir, Tsar [Emperor] Alexios, naturally after the death...

=== LEAF p75 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXII)*

...of these latter two. Herewith the emperor noted that regarding his own commemoration he composed a special secret document (p. 681).

Apart from these solemn memorial services at the tombs of the imperial family, the clerics of the Church of the Mother of God were obliged here daily in the morning to celebrate the Trisagion, and in the evening pannychides for the ktetors of the monastery (p. 681).

Besides memorial days, patronal feasts and certain other days of the church year were celebrated solemnly. In the Church of the Mother of God the Merciful [*Eleousa*], with equal solemnity were celebrated the Marian feasts of the Dormition of the Mother of God, the Entry into the Temple, and the Nativity of the Mother of God (p. 680). In the Pantokrator Monastery the patronal feast was considered the feast of the Transfiguration [^104], but, apart from it, according to the Typikon, it was prescribed to celebrate Bright Week [the Paschal week], the Nativity of Christ, Theophany, Palm Sunday, Pentecost, the Annunciation, the three above-mentioned Marian feasts, the Meeting of the Lord, the commemoration of the Three Hierarchs, the Holy Apostles, the Holy Apostle Philip, St. Basil the Great, Gregory the Theologian, and Great and Holy Thursday (pp. 661–662), when the abbot of the monastery performed the Washing of Feet for all the aged and the sick residing in the almshouse and in the hospital of the monastery (pp. 689, 695).

The vigil (*ἀγρυπνία*) on these feasts likewise began from the evening and continued throughout the entire night. It was celebrated ordinarily by the full staff of clergy and with exceptional solemnity. Evading attendance at divine services on these feasts...

=== LEAF p76 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXIII)*

...was punished by a fine or forfeiture of supplementary stipend (p. 680). The solemnity of the divine service, moreover, was expressed in the abundant illumination (*φωταψία* [*photapsia*]) of the churches, narthexes, and galleries by means of polykandila with large candles (pp. 677–678, 661), in the burning of incense and aloeswood (*ξυλαλόη* [*xylaloe*]) [^105], in the use of rose-water (*ῥοδοστάγματα* [*rhostagmata*]), with which they sprinkled the hands and faces of the worshippers (p. 661), and in the invitation for chanting (*χάριν ψαλμῳδίας* [for the sake of psalmody]) of vocal chanters from outside (p. 680), so that they, by their excellent execution of church melodies, might impart aesthetic enjoyment to the worshippers assembling in multitude for the spiritual celebration in the churches of the monastery. The emperor, however, strictly forbade the monks of the Pantokrator Monastery from visiting other monasteries and secular churches for a similar purpose, and terms such visits harmful and shameful (p. 672). On feasts artoklasia was celebrated, wine and kolyvo were distributed to the worshippers (p. 685), and at the gates of the monastery bread and money to the poor (pp. 661–662). The clerics of the Church of the Mother of God and those invited to take part in the divine service received supplementary money and bread allowances over and above those appointed by the establishment (pp. 679–680); nor were those serving and residing in the monastery's philanthropic institutions deprived of rewards on these days (p. 685).

The order of organization of internal monastic life in the Pantokrator Monastery was established by the emperor upon the principles of strict cenobitism (*κοινοβία* [*koinobia*]). Having recognized the monastery as completely autonomous and independent of the authorities, ecclesiastical and...

=== LEAF p77 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXIV)*

...civil (*μὴ ὑπὸ ἐκκλησιαστικὴν ἀρχήν, μὴ ὑπὸ ἀνακτορικὴν ἐξουσίαν, μὴ ὑφ' ἑτέραν ἀρχοντικὴν ἐξουσίαν τασσομένη, μόνη τῶν ἑαυτῆς ἀντιποιουμένη* [not subject to ecclesiastical authority, not subject to imperial authority, not placed under any other magistral power, but managing her own affairs alone]), in disposing of everything belonging to the monastery, corporeal and incorporeal (*δίκαια σωματικὰ ἢ ἀσώματα* [corporeal or incorporeal rights]), the emperor placed at its head an abbot (pp. 700, 696, 672), who was elected from the entire existing membership of the brotherhood not only of the ruling [*kyriarchic*] Pantokrator Monastery, but even from the midst of the brotherhood of the monasteries subordinate to it and of its numerous metochia. In view of disagreements that might arise among the brotherhood concerning a worthy candidate, according to the emperor's will, the living abbot of the monastery selected three candidates whose names, in the presence of all the monks, were written upon slips of paper, which were then sealed and placed in the *skeuophylakion* [sacristy]. The names of the chosen candidates were known to none of the brethren. Upon the death of the abbot, all the brethren assemble in one place and before them the sealed papers with the names of the candidates are opened. If all the brethren settle their choice upon one person from among these three candidates, then that person is appointed as abbot; but if disagreement arises concerning the identity of the most worthy for the orphaned throne, then all the brethren gather in the main monastery for earnest and tearful prayer to God that He might indicate the most worthy to govern the monastery. In this case the names of the candidates are written upon three slips of paper, which are placed in the sanctuary upon the holy table [altar] beneath its sacred coverings. All the brethren over the course of three days celebrate pannychides or vigils, Vespers, and Liturgies. On the third day, after the Liturgy, an illiterate [^106] monk (*γράμματα μὴ εἰδώς* [knowing no letters]) is sent into the sanctuary, who takes from the holy table one of the three slips of paper, and the name of the candidate read aloud to the entire brotherhood from the paper resolves the brotherhood's dispute. This candidate is considered the chosen one of God. It may happen, however, that from among the brotherhood there is found a man who, although he did not enter the number of the three candidates for the abbatial throne, is in actual fact more worthy than the others...

=== LEAF p78 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXV)*

...of this dignity, and for whom two thirds (*τὸ διπλάσιον μέρος* [the double portion / two-thirds part]) of the entire existing membership of the brotherhood cast their vote, then such a one they elevate to the abbacy without any election. After the act of election, the cheirotonia [elevation/ordination] to abbot is immediately performed at the Liturgy, which is celebrated, by permission of the patriarch (*διὰ πιττακίου πατριαρχικοῦ* [by patriarchal letter]), by one of the invited hierarchs residing in Constantinople. The newly appointed abbot, as a sign of his authority, received from the icon of the Pantokrator the abbatial staff (pp. 673–675).

The abbot was the head of the monastery, sovereign master in its administration, and at the same time the spiritual father to whom were accessible all the inner thoughts of his subordinate brethren. Every monk was obliged to reveal all his thoughts and inner movements of the heart to the abbot and to receive from him necessary spiritual healing. It was not forbidden, however, for the brethren to have a spiritual father besides the abbot, but from the midst of their own brotherhood and by all means a venerable elder, experienced in spiritual life and having received the right to confess the brethren through a hierarchal charter (*διὰ τῆς ἀρχιερατικῆς σφραγῖδος* [by hierarchal seal]) (pp. 672–673). But notwithstanding the extensive powers with which the abbot of the Pantokrator Monastery was endowed by the emperor, in matters concerning the monastery he, however, undertook nothing without counsel with the representative elders (p. 696). The council of elders under the abbot consisted of the oikonomos, the ekklisiarches, the chartophylax (pp. 700, 671), the nosokomos (superintendent of the hospital), the xenodochos (head of the hostel/guest house), the docheiarios (superintendent of the vestiary/storehouse), and the horiarios (head of the granaries) (pp. 696, 671), as well as the oikonomoi of all monasteries subordinate to the Pantokrator Monastery in other parts of the city and outside it (p. 696). In their turn, these representative elders likewise had no right to do anything without the will and counsel of the abbot of the monastery (*ibid.*). In exceptional cases that demanded special attention, according to the emperor's rule, the Grand Droungarios Nikephoros and the Protonotarios of Nicomedia Constantine were admitted to this council, and it was brought to the notice of the patriarch through a special notification (p. 700).

The abbot enjoys full respect and veneration, which the brethren were obliged to render him at all times and in every place (p. 670). As one bearing upon himself complex and mani-...

=== LEAF p79 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXVI)*

...fold duties, the abbot at the common table of the brethren ate special bread baked for him (*ἡμερέσιον ἀφράτον* [daily leavened white bread]), drank better wine, and on days when the brethren partook of fish, had dessert (*ὀψώνιον* [*opsonion*]) (p. 675). In case of the absence or death of the abbot, his place was taken by the oikonomos.

Since the monks' way of life in the monastery was strictly cenobitic, the brethren received full maintenance from the monastery. To each monk it was appointed to distribute each year two shirts, two pairs of shoes (*καλίγια ζυγὰς δύο* [two pairs of caligae / shoes]), and one cotton himation (*ἱμάτιον βαμβακηρόν*), containing four litras of cotton. Besides this, they received every two years one mantle and one breast-piece (*ἐπιστήθιον* [*epistethion*]) (p. 673). Concerning food provisions in the rule the most circumstantial instructions are given, from which it is evident that the monks' meal consisted for the most part of three courses. On feast days and non-fasting days they were served fish, eggs, and cheese, and on fast days—shellfish [*ostracoderma*], various kinds of vegetables and grains with oil. On feasts they drank wine, and on fast days an infusion with cumin (*εὔκρατον διὰ κυμίνου* [spiced cumin beverage]) (pp. 668–670). On great feasts dessert was appointed also for the brethren (p. 661).

The midday meal [trapeza] in the monastery took place immediately after the Liturgy. Having received antidoron (*εὐλογία* [blessing / antidoron]), after the threefold strike upon the wooden semantron, the brethren proceeded into the refectory with the chanting of the Psalm: **"I will exalt Thee, my God"** [Ps. 144 / LXX 144]. The priest who had celebrated the Liturgy walked ahead of all the monks and, having taken a place above the rest on the right side of the table, recited secretly the prayer: **"O Lord my God, heavenly and life-creating Bread, true food of the whole world, Who guidest our present life and hast promised us communion of the future, do Thou Thyself bless our food and drink and make us worthy without condemnation to partake thereof, who glorify and thank the Giver of all good gifts,"** after which, making the sign of the cross over the table, concluded with the exclamation: **"For blessed and glorified is Thy most honorable name."** The brethren answer: **"Amen."** After the abbot sits in his place at the head of the table, the brethren also sit at table. The priest, standing, exclaims: **"Blessed is God,"** and the reader at the ambo begins the reading. When the first course is set upon the table, the refectorian (*ὁ τῆς τραπέζης διακονητής* [minister of the table]) pronounces: **"Lord, have mercy; pray."** The priest...

=== LEAF p80 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXVII)*

...blesses the food brought. The abbot begins to partake first, and after him the other brethren. During the first course, wine or warm beverage is poured into glasses. After all have finished the first dish, the abbot strikes the end of the table three times with a small wooden mallet (*ξυλίνῃ σφύρᾳ μικρᾷ*). At the first strike, the brethren stand, ask a general blessing of the abbot, and sit down again in their places, after which each already blesses his own drink with the sign of the cross alone. In the absence of the abbot, the oikonomos takes his place, and when the latter is absent, the priest on duty does likewise. The priest's blessing was required also for the second course (*ὁ δεύτερος μίνσος* [*mensa* / course]) and the third, if it was appointed. With the abbot's permission, on feast days, when there was an abundance of food (*παρακλήσεως οὔσης* [when there is consolation / feast fare]), it was customary to drink several more krasobolia [*красовули* / cups of wine]. At the conclusion of the meal, the refectorian brings two baskets: one for fragments [leftover morsels] and the other for spoons. The abbot and brethren, with the verse: **"Blessed is God Who feedeth us,"** throw pieces of bread into the basket and recite the prayer: **"O Lord our God, multiply the abundance of Thy servants and have mercy on us, for Thou art holy, now and forever, and unto ages of ages."** The refectorian, at a sign from the abbot, traces a cross with the basket containing the fragments and pronounces: **"The great name."** The brethren answer: **"Of the Holy, Consubstantial, and Life-creating Trinity."** Then the abbot blesses all, and the refectorian together with the brethren sing: **"The Lord is gracious and merciful"**, after which Psalms 121 and 83 [LXX 121, 83] are read, a thanksgiving prayer is pronounced by the priest, and all depart to their cells.

After the first meal followed a second for those who had waited at table and for those who were at monastic obediences. None of the monks had the right to take food or anything edible into his cell (pp. 665–666).

Supper followed immediately after Vespers. The monks entered the refectory, after a threefold strike upon the wooden semantron, chanting the verse: **"The poor shall eat and be filled"** [Ps. 21:26 / LXX 21:27], and, upon the priest's blessing of the meal, sat down at table. Supper consisted of bread, cabbage, fruits, according to the season of the year, and wine. The order during supper was observed to be the very same that was described for the midday meal. After supper the basket for fragments was brought in with the words: **"Glory..."**

<!-- END COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort8 -->

---

<!-- START COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort9 -->

=== LEAF p81 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXVIII)*

**"...to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit,"** to which the brethren with one voice (*συνημμένως*) said thrice: **"Lord, have mercy,"** and concluded with the words: **"Lord, bless."** The abbot recited the prayer: **"O Christ God, multiply the fragments of Thy servants,"** and blessed the fragments. The refectorian lifted the basket and, tracing with it a cross at the abbot's place (*πρὸς τῷ ἄκρῳ τῆς τραπέζης*) at the table, pronounced in a loud voice: **"Most holy Theotokos, help us,"** and then read the verse: **"Thou hast made us glad, O Lord, in Thy creation"** [Ps. 91:4 / LXX 91:5] [^107]. The abbot said the concluding verse and gave the blessing to the ekklisiarches to strike for Compline (p. 667).

The table for sick monks was distinguished by other qualities (*ἄρτῳ καθαρῷ, οἴνῳ καλλίστῳ* [with pure bread, with finest wine]), and in it were permitted, at the discretion of the abbot, departures from the regimen adopted in the monastery. In his own cell the sick monk had careful nursing on the part of the healthy brethren. In case of severe illness, the patient was placed in a special hospital ward (*τῷ τρικλίνῳ τῶν νοσοκομουμένων*) and made use of the services of a physician invited from the xenon [infirmary/hostel], and of the medications of its pharmacy. According to need, the physician even spent the night together with the patient, and therefore in the ward (*τρικλινάριον* [*triklinarion*]), besides six beds with complete furnishings for the patients, there stood also a special bed for the physician. Upon the demand of the physicians, at the service of the patients at all times was a monastery bath (*λουτρόν* [*loutron*]), well equipped with everything necessary, in which they could bathe at one time up to the number of six men, whereas healthy [^108] monks were deprived of this pleasure during the course of the entire Great Fast (pp. 666–667, 670).

Organizing his monastery with a strictly cenobitic way of life, the emperor demands of its monks their unremitting attendance at prayers (*ἐν ταῖς προσευχαῖς προσεδρίαν ἀπερίσπα-...*

=== LEAF p82 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXIX)*

...*στον* [undistracted perseverance in prayers]), and therefore recommends to them constantly to abide within the monastery, to go out no otherwise than by necessity and with the abbot's blessing on each occasion, by evening without fail to return to the monastery, not to attend solemn divine services outside the monastery, not to choose spiritual fathers from outside the monastery, etc. (pp. 670, 672–673). In order to remove temptations from the monks, the emperor forbids the entrance into the monastery to women, even if the latter should be of noble lineage and blameless life, with the exception, however, of those cases when the latter came to the monastery for the memorial services or funerals of their monastic relatives (p. 671); he demands that none of the laity be accepted for services within the monastery, not permitting at the same time the dispatch of monks for work in the metochia outside the monastery (pp. 672, 696). It is not surprising, therefore, that from those entering into the number of the monks of the Pantokrator Monastery there was required a strict (*μετὰ ἀκριβοῦς ἐξετάσεως* [with precise examination]) testing or probation and an abundance (*περιούσιον τῆς ἀρετῆς* [superabundance of virtue]) of all virtues. From another monastery, without a testimonial from its abbot, no one was received (p. 670).

Emperor John Komnenos, however, well recognized that a strictly cenobitic monastic regimen is not suitable for every monk, and therefore in the monasteries subordinate to the Pantokrator Monastery, situated in the city and outside it, he permitted for the monks a different way of life, arranging the establishments otherwise than in the main monastery. As such subordinate monasteries to the Pantokrator Monastery were counted: the Monastery of Nossiai (*Νοσσιῶν* [*Nossion*]), Monokastanon (*τῆς Μονοκαστάνου*), Anthemion (*τῶν Ἀνθεμίου*), and across on the other side, opposite the city, the Monastery of Medikarion (*τοῦ Μηδικαρίου*), Galakrenai (*τῶν Γαλακρηνῶν*), and Satyros (*τοῦ Σατύρου*). Of these monasteries only the Monastery of Nossiai had a cenobitic organization after the model of the Pantokrator Monastery, while the rest had a kellion [idiorrhythmic/cell-based] organization, and their brethren were divided into those living inside the monasteries and outside them (*ἐσωμονῖται καὶ ἐξωμονῖται* [*esomonitai kai exomonitai*]). The Monastery of Satyros alone was subject to the jurisdiction of the patriarch. In all these monasteries the emperor instituted establishments according to which in the Monastery of Nossiai were appointed 12 monks and 6 servants; in the Monastery of Monokastanon, 16 kellion monks, but without fail living inside the monastery, according to the rule already worked out by them previously; the mon-...

=== LEAF p83 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXX)*

...ks living as kelliotes under the same rights numbered 12 in the Monastery of Anthemion, 6 in the Monastery of Medikarion, 6 in the Monastery of Galakrenai, and 18 in the Monastery of Satyros. All these monasteries were obliged to be subordinate to the abbot of the main monastery, but were administered by special oikonomoi. The surplus revenues these monasteries delivered to the main monastery, but in case of deficiency in anything they had recourse to the latter for assistance and found in it complete satisfaction (pp. 675–676).

Yet perhaps the most curious pages for the historian in the Typikon under analysis must be acknowledged to be those in which are very circumstantially described the xenon (*ξενών*) [infirmary/hostel] with hospital, the home of care for the aged [gerokomeion], and the psychiatric clinic established by the emperor at the monastery. These pages depict for us in the most attractive traits the philanthropic significance of ancient Byzantine monasteries [^109].

The xenon (*ξενών*) with hospital was organized for 50 beds for patients permanently residing in it (*ἀνακεκλιμένους ἀῤῥώστους* [bedridden patients]), of which 10 beds were designated for those suffering from wounds and having sustained injuries, 8 beds for those suffering in the eyes, stomach, and certain other acute illnesses, 12 for sick women, and the remainder were provided for those suffering from ordinary illnesses. If the number of patients with wounds, or eyes, or other acute illnesses does not exceed the norm, and places remain free, then they are occupied by ordinary patients. Each bed must have a small carpet (*τζόλιον* [*tzolion*]), a felt mattress (*πιλωτόν* [*piloton*]) with a pillow and a blanket (*ἐφάπλωμα* [*ephaploma*]), and for winter two wool blankets (*δύο λοονίκια* [*dyo loonikia*]). All these beds are distributed among five divisions, wherein in each division there was appointed also...

=== LEAF p84 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXXI)*

...one supernumerary bed (*παρακράββατον* [*parakrabbaton*]) in case all places in the hospital should be occupied and there arose the necessity to admit a patient on account of the urgency of his illness (*διὰ τὸ καтеπεῖγον τῆς νόσου*). Over and above the mentioned number of beds, at the hospital existed a division of six beds with mattresses having perforations in the center, for patients unable to move either by reason of the acuteness and severity of the illness, or, more frequently, by reason of severe wounds. For patients in general and those suffering from severe illnesses in particular, there were kept at the hospital constantly up to 15 or 20 shirts and outer garments (*ἱμάτια* [*himatia*]), into which the patients are changed upon their admission to the hospital, while their own undergarments and clothing are washed and returned upon recovery, when leaving the hospital. Unusable bedding and remaining garments are replaced annually; the mattresses and pillows are repaired, the wool (*μαλλίον* [*mallion*]) is carded, and torn bedsheets are turned and sewn anew for further use. Replaced clothing and bed linens, if fit for use, are kept by the director of the hospital for the patients, while unusable items are distributed to the poor (p. 682).

To each of the five divisions in the hospital were appointed two physicians, three staff medical assistants (*ὑπουργοὶ ἔμβαθμοι* [*hypourgoi embathmoi*]), two supernumerary assistants (*περισσοὶ ὑπουργοί* [*perissoi hypourgoi*]), and two servants (*ὑπηρέται* [*hyperetai*]). The women's division was directed by two physicians, one female physician/midwife (*ἰάτραινα* [*iatraina*]), four staff female medical assistants (*ὑπούργισσαι ἔμβαθμοι* [*hypourgissai embathmoi*]), two supernumerary female assistants, and two female attendants. At night four medical assistants and one female assistant were on duty with the patients, that is, one person in each division. These night-duty attendants were called excubitors (*ἐξκουβήτορες* [*exkoubitores*]). Of the physicians assigned by the establishment in the five divisions, the first two are called protomenitai (*πρωτομηνῖται* [chiefs of the month]), the two following them primikerioi (*πριμμικήριοι*), two their assistants (*ὀπτίονες* [*optiones*]), and one a teacher of the medical art. For outpatient sick (*λόγῳ τῶν ἔξωθεν ἐρχομένων ἀῤῥώστων* [for the sake of patients coming from outside]) four supernumerary physicians were appointed, of whom two were for internal diseases (*διαιτητικοί* [*diaitetikoi*]) and two surgeons (*τραυματικοί* [*traumatikoi*]). The surgeons were invited also into the women's ward (*εἰς τὸ γυναικεῖον*) whenever any of the women required their assistance. For the four physicians for outpatients it was appointed to have four staff medical assistants,...

=== LEAF p85 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXXII)*

...and four supernumerary assistants, of whom two took turns being on duty for a month in the monastery hospital.

All the physicians were divided into two shifts: one half of them discharged their duties for one month, and the other half the following month. In like manner the two protomenitai and the two primikerioi fulfilled their office. The protomenitai visit the hospital from the beginning of May until the feast of the Exaltation [of the Holy Cross] without fail every day, after the morning psalmody, wherein with diligent attention they inform themselves concerning the illness of each, prescribe the necessary medicines, and make the necessary arrangements regarding all the patients. In his turn each of the primikerioi likewise makes the rounds daily of all the beds, inquires with attention and care of each patient concerning how he is being treated, whether the persons appointed for that purpose attend upon him with fitting diligence and zeal, and endeavors to correct what is amiss, gives reprimands to the negligent, and immediately removes everything that is done contrary to duty. He also watches over the bread for the patients and over everything that is appointed to be distributed to them daily. In view of this the senior physicians, according to the Typikon, ought not to have divisions directly under their charge, since this oversight alone is sufficient for them in place of any other work (p. 683).

If any of the outpatient sick should prove to be seriously ill, the physician appointed to receive such announces him to the primikerios, by whose order another physician, more experienced than the rest (*ὁ τῶν λοιπῶν ἐμπειρότερος*), is invited to the patient. The latter, after a careful diagnosis, prescribes the necessary medicine. From the number of the staff physicians were chosen the director of the hospital (*νοσοκόμος* [*nosokomos*]) and the senior warden (*ὁ μειζότερος* [*ho meizoteros*]). They were charged with the duty of having everything necessary for the hospital in sufficient quantity and of expending it generously not only for those who are in the hospital, but also for outpatient sick.

In view of those cares and arduous duties that were laid by the emperor upon the physicians of the hospital and those serving at it, it was deemed necessary to supplement the staffs (*τάγματα τῶν ἰατρῶν* [corps/ranks of physicians]) of physicians, medical assistants, and other ministers with the following persons in such numbers: the chief of the pharmacy (*ἐπιστήκων τοῦ ποιμέντου* [*epistekon tou poimentou* / prefect of the pharmacy]) one; staff pharmacists (*πημεντάριοι* [*pementarioi*]) three; super-...

=== LEAF p86 ===

*(Orig. p. LXXXIII)*

...numerary two; one porter (*ὀστιάριος* [*ostiarios*]); five laundresses (*σαπωνίστριαι* [*saponistriai*]); one boiler-tender/coppersmith (*λεβητάριος* [*lebetarios*]); two cooks (*μάγειροι* [*mageiroi*]); one groom (*στράτωρ* [*strator*]), looking after the mill beasts and the horses of the physicians during their visits; a gatekeeper (*πυλωρός* [*pyloros*]); a second bread-provisioner (*ὀψωνιάτωρ* [*opsoniator*]); two priests at the hospital church, of whom one, by virtue of an episcopal charter, had the right to confess the patients; two readers; two female bakers (*μαγκίπησσαι* [*mankipissai*]); four gravediggers (*νεκροθάπται* [*nekrothaptai*]); one priest (*ἱερεὺς τῆς κηδείας* [*hiereus tes kedeias*]) for celebrating funerals; one channel-inspector/drain-cleaner (*καναλοπλύτης* [*kanaloplytes*]); and one millstone-dresser/miller (*μυлоχαράκτης* [*mylocharaktes*]) (p. 684).

Over and above the number of physicians indicated above, the Typikon prescribes having also two special supernumerary physicians (*δύο ἰατροὺς περισσούς*) for the hospital inside the monastery, in which only sick monks were treated. These physicians were chosen from the supernumeraries designated according to the rule (*ἀπὸ τῶν ἐμβαθμιωτέρων περισσῶν* [from the higher-ranking supernumeraries]), and performed their duties in the monastery, alternating monthly. Service in the monastery hospital was for them a stepping stone toward obtaining a higher staff post and a larger salary establishment at the hospital of the xenon. At first the physicians of the women's division performed these duties, and thereafter the supernumerary physicians of the other divisions in order and seniority (*καὶ ἐν τοῖς ἑξῆς κατὰ προкоπήν*). Medicines, plasters, various kinds of auxiliary supplies, and other articles necessary for the patients were delivered to the monastery hospital from the hospital of the xenon. In view of this, in order never to encounter refusal, the emperor commanded giving annually to the director of the hospital, over and above the sums appointed for the hospital, an additional 20 hyperpyra coins (p. 686).

As regards the annual maintenance of the hospital in general, it was defined in the Typikon in the most precise manner down to the minutest details. ...nual modioi; the tinsmith/brazier (*ὁ χαλκωματᾶς*) and the cooper (*ὁ καδδαρᾶς*) receives 2 similar hyperpyra and of bread 20 modioi [^114] (pp. 689–692).

The physicians of the hospital and the male medical assistants with the female medical assistants, besides their annual salary establishments, received also fixed monthly allowances. To all of them monthly were issued five trikephalon (*τρικέφαλα*) nomismata each. To the physician-protomenites for the assistance rendered by him to outpatient sick was issued one such same trikephalon coin, and to the assistant excubitors—36 nomismata tracheae. Over and above all this, on the patronal monastery feast days, on other solemn days of the year, and on the days of the imperial commemorations, there was additional award money for the entire staff of persons serving in the hospital. On the patronal feast of the Transfiguration of the Lord in the monastery, to the physicians and attendants were issued 50 nomismata tracheae and 920 tetartera (*νομίσματα τραχέα πεντήκοντα καὶ τεταρτηρὰ ἐννεακόσια εἴκοσιν*); on the feast of the Meeting [of the Lord]—309 nomismata tetartera; on the 1st day of the month of November, when the memory of the patrons of medicine, the holy unmercenary physicians Cosmas and Damian, is celebrated—15 coins of tracheae; on Great and Holy Thursday, when the abbot washes the feet, to the physicians and all the attendants were issued 42 tracheae (p. 592 [sic, 689]), and for candles, over and above that, three hyperpyra (p. 689); on every Saturday when an imperial commemoration was celebrated, the physicians and hospital attendants received four nomismata hyperpyra; at the beginning of each month for kolyvo and prosphoras 6 modioi of bread each, which for the year amounted to 72 monastery modioi; on the three memorial Saturdays, that is, on Meatfare, Cheesefare, and Pentecost [Saturday], for the sake of the commemoration of the brethren who had reposed in this hospital, the physicians and attendants of the hospital every Saturday received of bread 15 monastery modioi each and in money 250 folles each (p. 685).

Taking into consideration all these receipts and supplements to their salary, as well as the fact that the physicians serving at the hospital enjoyed free quarters, illumination (p. 687), and even monastery horses (pp. 684–691), it is not surprising...

=== LEAF p93 ===

*(Orig. p. XC)*

...at all that the emperor entirely prohibited them from private practice outside the city, and especially visits to persons of high station, officials, and those close to the emperor, with the exception, however, of those cases when an order of the sovereign himself should follow. This was done by the emperor, on the one hand, in view of having the physicians remain always at their post at the hospital and not entrust the sick to any of their subordinates (*διὰ σουπέρων τινὰ τῶν ἰατρῶν* [through some of the supernumerary physicians]), and on the other hand, so that these physicians should not misuse the hospital medications, the dispensation of which outside was strictly prohibited (p. 693).

The emperor extended his solicitous care for the physicians to such a degree that he took upon himself concern even for their children. With a view to training them in the medical art, he opened an unofficial school at the hospital (*οὐ γὰρ ὡς ὀφφικίον τυποῦται παρ᾽ ἡμῶν τὸ διδασκαλεῖον* [for the school is not instituted by us as an official dignitary-post]) and appointed to it from among the experienced physicians of the hospital a teacher, who in salary was equalized in everything with the director of the hospital, so as thereby to induce him zealously and diligently to fulfill the duties laid upon him. The emperor recommends rewarding a worthy and zealous teacher, and punishing one negligent in the performance of his duty with deprivation of salary and even entirely removing him from the post, replacing him with another more diligent (p. 693).

At the hospital in the men's and women's divisions churches were arranged [^115], in which for the patients liturgies were celebrated on Wednesdays, Fridays, Saturdays, and Sundays, as well as feast days (p. 689); there were two mills at which three horses worked, a bakery (*μαγκιπεῖον* [*mankipeion*]) (p. 686), as well as other establishments useful in a large economy, as for example a locksmith shop, a smithy, a cooperage (p. 692), a bath well equipped with everything necessary (p. 670), etc. The bath for the sick, upon the demand of the physicians, was always ready for their service. They visited it accompanied by medical assistants and...

=== LEAF p94 ===

*(Orig. p. XCI)*

...a sufficient number of attendants. Usually the patients washed in the bath twice a week (p. 686).

The second philanthropic institution at the Pantokrator Monastery was the home for the aged [gerokomeion] (*γηροκομεῖον*) for maimed, lame, shelterless, and uncared-for old men to the number of 24 men. Here, according to the will of the emperor, healthy old men, strong men, monks of the monastery, people of noble lords, or those belonging to the church, or slaves—in a word, persons capable of labor and able to earn their own sustenance—could not be admitted. To each of the old men cared for, it was appointed to issue annually 20 marine modioi of bread, 18 such same metra of wine, 2 modioi of beans, 50 litras of cheese, one marine metron of olive oil, three marine pieces [pisses] of firewood, and as roga and for clothing—2 nomismata hyperpyra. On the days of commemorations of the imperial family and on feasts they received an addition equally with those who had distributions at the gates of the monastery, and on Great and Holy Thursday, after the washing of the feet of the old men, the abbot distributed one hyperpyron for eight men, that is, three hyperpyra for the entire home for the aged.

As chief supervisor in the home for the aged was the most pious (*ὁ τῶν πολλῶν εὐλαβέστερος* [more pious than the many]) monk of the monastery, who was obligated with all diligence to watch over those cared for. At the disposal of the director of the home for the aged were six servants, who for their labor received, as roga—2 hyperpyra each, of bread 20 marine modioi each, of wine 16 such same metra each, for cooked relish 2 marine modioi of beans each, and 50 litras of cheese.

At the home for the aged was a church and its own special priest. For the maintenance of the church (*τοῦ εὐκτηρίου* [*tou eukteriou*]) was appointed 2 marine metra of olive oil, of wine 6 metra, for prosphoras of bread 6 marine modioi, of wax 24 litras, and of incense [thymiama] 6 litras. The priest of the home for the aged with cooked relish received 6 hyperpyra, of bread 24 marine modioi, of wine 18 metra, and the reader half of the priestly maintenance.

Those cared for washed twice a month in the bath of the hospital. In case of illness of any of the old men, upon the declaration of the priest of the home for the aged, a physician or a medical assistant was invited from the hospital, and measures were taken for the relief of the sufferer. In case of grave illness, the old man was transferred to the hospital, where he re-...

=== LEAF p95 ===

*(Orig. p. XCII)*

...mained until complete recovery, whereupon he again returned to the home for the aged (pp. 694–695).

Those who died in the hospital and the home for the aged were buried either in the common burial vault (*ἐν τῇ κοινῇ ἀποθέσει τῶν σωμάτων*) at the church of the holy Apostle Luke [^116], or in other burial vaults of the city and on the other side of it (*καὶ ἐν τοῖς πέраθεν*); therefore Emperor John Komnenos ordered to construct in the Monastery of Medikarion, situated opposite the Pantokrator Monastery (*ἐν τῇ πέραθεν ἡμετέρᾳ μονῇ τοῦ Μηδικαρίου*), on its land in size of 2 modioi, a burial vault with a church (*εὐκτήριον*) and to surround it on all sides with a clay wall covered with lead, with only one door for entrance into the cemetery. In this church-burial vault one of the monks of the Monastery of Medikarion on Friday is to celebrate Vespers with a Pannikhida, and on Saturday a funeral Liturgy for all the departed brethren. For his labor he received no special remuneration, apart from that appointed by the establishment, with the exception only of those cases when a special disposition of those being buried in the given cemetery should follow. For the maintenance of the church from the Pantokrator Monastery was issued annually 12 litras of wax, one and a half annona-metra of olive oil, 6 litras of frankincense, for prosphoras 6 monastery modioi of bread and the same amount of metra of wine (pp. 693–694).

For brethren suffering mentally (*τῶν κατεχομένων τῇ ἱερᾷ νόσῳ* [those afflicted with the sacred disease]), the emperor arranged a psychiatric clinic—the third charitable institution. Since the part of the city where the Pantokrator Monastery was situated was densely populated with inhabitants, the place for its construction was chosen by the emperor far from the city. This was done, on the one hand, to afford the patients necessary rest and quiet (*εἶναι αὐτοῖς ἀνενόχλητον* [that it might be undisturbing to them]), and on the other hand, to re-...

=== LEAF p96 ===

*(Orig. p. XCIII)*

...move from the eyes of neighbors, the inhabitants of the given locality, such patients as by their behavior might cause disturbance and serve as the cause of scandalous scenes. Concern for this clinic and for the provision of the sick in it the emperor laid upon the monastery (p. 695).

In view of the special conditions of the monastic life of the described monastery and those broad philanthropic goals which, according to the thought of the imperial ktetor, it was to pursue and fulfill, Emperor John Komnenos, his deceased spouse Irene, and relatives of the imperial family took care to secure it with rich endowments, the assignment to the monastery of lands and estates yielding good revenues. The Typikon of Emperor John Komnenos, given to the monastery in the year 1136, enumerates in detail the numerous property possessions of the Pantokrator Monastery and its revenues. These possessions consist of suburban lands (*τὸ προάστειον* [*to proasteion*]), country estates (*ἡ ἐπίσκεψις* [*he episkepsis*]), hostels/inns (*ξενοδοχεῖον* [*xenodocheion*]), properties (*ὑπόστασις* [*hypostasis*]), houses with apartments (*οἶκος μετὰ καὶ τῶν ἐνοικικῶν αὐτοῦ*), fields (*χωράφιον* [*choraphion*]), vineyards (*ἀμπελώνων* [*ampelonon*]), small towns or villages allocated for military billeting (*χωρίον σὺν τοῖς προσκαθημένοις ἐκεῖσε ἐστρατευμένοις*), salt pans (*ἁληκαί* [*alikai*]), commercial markets (*ἐμπορεῖον* [*emporeion*]), tithes from taverns (*ἡ δεκατεία τῶν οἰναρίων*), baths (*τὸ λουτρόν* [*to loutron*]), villages with peasants (*τὸ χωρίον σὺν τοῖς ἐκεῖσε προσκαθημένοις παροίκοις*), mills (*μυλώνων* [*mylonon*]), entire well-arranged estates (*κτήματα* [*ktemata*]), revenues from monasteries subordinate to the Pantokrator Monastery, tribute (*τὰ τελέσματα* [*ta telesmata*]) of the Metropolia of Patras, the Bishopric of Methone, the Monastery of Molebotos (*τῆς μονῆς Μολεβωτοῦ*), etc. (pp. 697–699). We, unfortunately, cannot point out precisely where all the possessions of the Pantokrator Monastery were located and determine their property value, but from the Typikon it is evident that they were suburban, urban, but for the most part non-local, as for example in Thessalonica, Athens, Patras, and other cities, and on numerous islands of the Mediterranean Sea. In the course of time, under the successors of Emperor John Komnenos, the metochia of the monastery significantly increased, and, consequently, its material means constantly grew.

=== LEAF p97 ===

*(Orig. p. XCIV)*

The Typikon of the Pantokrator Monastery is published according to the known and sole copy accessible to us, located in manuscript No. 85 of the library of the Chalki Theological School in Constantinople. Thither this copy was made in the year 1749 from the parchment autograph preserved in the rich library of Prince-Voivode Nicholas Mavrocordatos, as the following colophon states, found at the end of the named copy: **"It was transcribed without variation from a manuscript book on parchment, the very prototype Typikon itself, preserved in the most complete library of the most wise and renowned ruler Nicholas Voivode Mavrocordatos in Constantinople, in the month of October of the thirteenth indiction, in the year one thousand seven hundred and forty-nine from the incarnate Dispensation"** (*«᾿Αντεγράφη ἀπαραλλάκτως ἀπὸ χειρογράφου ἐν μεμβράναις βιβλίου, αὐτοῦ φημι τοῦ πρωτοτύπου Τυπικοῦ, σωζομένου ἐν τῇ πληρεστάτῃ βιβλιοθήκῃ τοῦ σοφωτάτου καὶ ἀοιδίμου αὐθέντου Νικολάου βοεβόδα Μαυροκορδάτου τῇ ἐν Κωνσταντινουπόλει, κατὰ μῆνα ὀκτώβриον ἰνδικτιῶνος δεκάτης τρίτης, ἔτους χιλιοστοῦ ἑпτακοσιοστοῦ τεσσαρακοστοῦ ἐννάτου ἀπὸ τῆς ἐνσάρκου οἰκονομίας»*) (fol. 122). Where this autograph is preserved at the present time, we do not know. All the omissions in the copy, marked in our edition by dots and stipulated by special notes, must be thought to belong to the autograph from which the Chalki copy of the year 1749 was made.

IX. The Monastery of St. Mamas, the Typikon of which we include in our *Description* in incomplete form, according to the indications of Byzantine sources [^117] was located "in the Xerokerkos" (*«ἐν τῷ Ξηροκέρκῳ»*) (p. 2), or "at the gate of the Xerokerkos" (*«ἐν τῷ Ξηροκέρκῳ πόρτης»*) (p. 98), or, as archaeologists determine more precisely, "outside the city walls behind the Xylokerkos, at the mouth of the Golden Horn, or at the Sweet Waters, on the sea shore" [^118]. Professor of the Novorossiysk University F. I. Uspensky (presently director of the Archaeological Institute in Constantinople), who published several chapters from the present Typikon and circumstantially acquainted us with the system, so called in ancient Byzantium, of the charistikariate administration of monasteries, that is, the giving of them over to the administration of secular persons, made an attempt more precisely to determine the location of the given monastery. By a series of very in-...

=== LEAF p98 ===

*(Orig. p. XCV)*

...genious juxtapositions and rapprochements of various kinds of historical testimonies regarding the present monastery, he attempts to establish that the Monastery of St. Mamas "cannot be sought on that side of the Golden Horn where the Blachernae Palace was, but on the opposite side" [^119], and that it "lay not on the Golden Horn, but in the direction toward the Bosporus" [^120]. But all these considerations of the esteemed Byzantinist still require reinforcement by facts and testimonies of a more positive character.

From the Typikon of the Monastery of St. Mamas we publish the prologue and the signatures, as material for the history of the monastery, with a note of those omissions which we noticed in the brochure of F. I. Uspensky: *The Typikon of the Monastery of St. Mamas in Constantinople*. With the same views we printed in full also chapter 40 of the given Typikon, entirely omitted in the named brochure. As regards the remaining chapters, in view of their identity with similar chapters in the Typikon of the Anatolian Theotokos Monastery "of the Altars of the Sun, that is, of the Elegmoi" (*«τῶν ῾Ηλίου Βωμῶν, ἤτοι τῶν Ἐλεγμῶν»*), which according to the original Patmos manuscript we publish in full immediately after this, we made juxtapositions of the one and the other and noted all the peculiarities observed by us in the annotations, as variants to the latter Typikon.

The text for the full chapters and prologue of the Mamas Typikon published by us and for the footnote annotations to the Typikon of the Monastery *«τῶν ῾Ηλίου Βωμῶν»* we take from the copy in manuscript No. 85 of the Chalki Library, whence Prof. F. I. Uspensky also borrowed the text of this rule for his brochure named above.

X. The Typikon of the Anatolian Theotokos Monastery "of the Altars of the Sun, that is, of the Elegmoi" (*«τῶν ῾Ηλίου Βωμῶν, ἤτοι τῶν Ἐлеγμῶν»*), published by us according to a parchment manuscript of the year 1162, preserved under No. 265 in the Patmos Library, was compiled in the reign of the Byzantine Emperor Manuel Komnenos by a certain noble dignitary Nikephoros Mystikos, who, by rights of a ktetor, gave it to the mentioned...

=== LEAF p99 ===

*(Orig. p. XCVI)*

...monastery for guidance. The monastery, named "of the Altars of the Sun" (*«τῶν ῾Ηλίου Βωμῶν»*) or "of the Elegmoi" (*«τῶν Ἐλεγμῶν»*) [^121], was located in Anatolia in the Opsikion theme [^122], in the province (*ἐνορία*) of Katabola, and was subject to the Great Church of Constantinople. It once enjoyed renown and was considered famous (*τὴν πάλαι ἐπιφανῆ καὶ ἐπίσημον*), but in the course of time, thanks to various kinds of unfavorable circumstances that befell it, became so impoverished that it came to be called simply a dwelling of monastic men (*οἰκητήριον ἀνδρῶν μοναστῶν*) (p. 716). Moved by the mournful condition of the once glorious monastery, Nikephoros Mystikos resolved to renew it, and with this aim first, with the consent of Patriarch Constantine Chliarinos of Constantinople (*Κωνσταντίνου τοῦ Χλιαρινοῦ*), freed it from subordination to the Great Church, and then obtained for it from Emperor Ma-...

=== LEAF p100 ===

*(Orig. p. XCVII)*

...nuel Komnenos a special chrysobull for complete independent self-governance, having persuaded the latter to donate to the monastery also the customary annual alms (*σολεμνίαν δόσιν ἐτήσιον* [*solemnian dosin etesion*]) of one hundred trikephalon coins (p. 717). Nikephoros Mystikos renewed what had fallen into decrepitude and decay, repaired what was spoiled, built much anew, and, in this manner, adorned and brought into beauteous appearance the neglected and pitifully deteriorated monastery. With the aim of increasing the revenue of the monastery, the solicitous ktetor endeavored to bring into good condition and supply with everything necessary also the metochion of this monastery with the church of St. Bassian (p. 724), located in Constantinople [^123]. With a view, moreover, to a firmer internal organization of the monastery, desiring more precisely to regulate every detail of its life and the relations of the monks living in it, he wrote also his present Typikon (p. 717), granting to his brethren in ecclesiastical-liturgical practice to be guided by the Studite Synaxarion (*Στουδιτικὸν Συναξάριον*) adopted in many Byzantine monasteries (pp. 728, 736, 758, 760).

The Typikon of the Theotokos Monastery "of the Altars of the Sun" (*«τῶν ῾Ηλίου Βωμῶν»*), as we have already remarked above, is similar to the Typikon [^124] of the Monastery of St. Mamas in Constantinople. The similarity between both Typika is very great, but nevertheless not complete. The reckoning of the chapters from 1–32 inclusive and from 37–39 is identical in both Typika,...

A similarity to the Evergetis Typikon does indeed exist in both monuments, wherein the text of the Mamas Typikon stands closer to it than the text of the Typikon of the Monastery *«τῶν ῾Ηλίου Βωμῶν»*, yet the similarity can be seen only in certain chapters, wherein for collations it is not infrequently necessary to take into account two and even three chapters of the Evergetis Typikon situated at a great distance from one another, and moreover not in complete form, but with significant omissions. === LEAF p102 ===

*(Orig. p. XCIX)*

...and 41; 34 = 21 and 22; 35 = 9; 41 = 17; 44 = 14 and 17; 45 = 42 and to the supplementary article from which the prayer is borrowed; 46 = 3, 4, and 6. Upon a more detailed collation with other known ktetorik Typika, identical chapters can undoubtedly be found in those monuments as well. From this, consequently, it is clear that it is hardly possible to consider the Typikon of the Evergetis Monastery as the prototype of the present Typika. Rather, both of these Typika, like many other monuments of this kind, represent a compilation or collection of rules and instructions necessary in monastic practice, concerning all aspects of monastic life and selected by the founder of the monastery, according to personal taste, from other ktetorik Typika known to him.

The text of the Typikon of the Monastery *«τῶν ῾Ηλίου Βωμῶν»* we take from the original Patmos manuscript No. 265. The damaged chapters 17 and 18, owing to the absence of the upper halves of four folios of the manuscript, we supply according to the manuscript of the Chalki library No. 85 from the Typikon of the Monastery of St. Mamas, enclosing the additions made by us in square brackets.

XI. The last place in the series of Byzantine ktetorik Typika we assign in our edition to the Typikon of the Monastery of Michael the Archangel on the Mountain of Saint Auxentios, given by Emperor Michael VIII Palaiologos (1259–1282).

The monastery in honor of the commander of the bodiless hosts, the Archangel Michael, erected by Emperor Michael Palaiologos, as is supposed,[^125] around the year 1280 or 1281, was located in the diocese of Bithynia on the island of Oxeia near Chalcedon, opposite Constantinople. This monastery was situated on...

=== LEAF p103 ===

*(Orig. p. C)*

...the Mountain of Saint Auxentios, in a locality removed from worldly vanity and city turmoil, and which from ancient times enjoyed renown among all lovers of the desert ascetic life. According to the words of the Typikon, this locality was considered a "second Pelusium, or Nitria, or Tabennisi, or Skete" (p. 772). It is not surprising, therefore, that on this mountain, from the time of the settlement here (at the end of the 8th century) of the renowned hermit Saint Auxentios, who gave this mountain its name, there always lived no small number of illustrious and great ascetics (*περιφανεῖς βιαστάς* [illustrious ascetics]), seekers of the Kingdom of Heaven.

The noble and even related to the imperial house family of the Palaiologoi had from ancient times shown greater attention to this mountain. Already the grandfather[^126] of Emperor Michael Palaiologos, the Grand Duke Alexios, in monasticism Antonios (pp. 772, 789), established here a splendid monastery, which, however, thanks to various kinds of unfavorable circumstances, fell into desolation and complete ruin. To restore the given monastery and bring it into magnificent form fell already to the lot of his fortunate grandson, the Byzantine Emperor Michael, the son of the Grand Dome-...

=== LEAF p104 ===

*(Orig. p. CI)*

...-stic Andronikos, in monasticism Arsenios, and of Theodora Komnene, in monasticism Theodosia (p. 787). This fortunate conqueror of the Persians, Moesians, Scythians, Italians, and Latins, having returned to himself the throne of Constantine the Great, and to the Ecumenical Patriarch the church of Hagia Sophia with the cathedra of Chrysostom, upon which there presided for some time a patriarch who was an alien and an intruder (*προσεδρεύει πατριάρχης ἀλλογενὴς καὶ παρέγγραπτος*) (p. 771), resolved to renew the foundation of his grandfather. His motives were profound gratitude to God and to His commander of the heavenly hosts, the Archangel Michael, whom the emperor always honored as his patron, for those manifold benefactions that he received from Them in his life (pp. 769–772, 789–794), on the one hand; and on the other, the desire to have in his monastery ceaseless intercessors for himself and his entire lineage.

As concerns the material provision of the given monastery, it was in many respects inferior to other contemporary Constantinopolitan imperial monasteries, especially if we take into account that, by the will of its founder, the monastery was obliged to use the remainder of monetary funds from its annual upkeep for the ransom of captives, for aid to orphans, and for providing dowries in marriage for poor virgins (*εἰς παρθένων, ἀπορίᾳ συνεχομένων, ἔννομον γάμου κοινωνίαν καὶ σύζευξιν*) (p. 785). Concerning the revenues and material provision of the monastery, the founder directly declares in the Typikon that he did not en-...

=== LEAF p105 ===

*(Orig. p. CII)*

...-dow it with a large quantity of money (*οὐδὲ πλεῖστα χρήματα κατεκενώσαμεν*), densely populated villages (*οὐ πολυάνθρωπα χωρία*), extensive estates (*οὐ πολύπλευθρα γήδια*), large and fruitful fields (p. 790), etc., because he was occupied with the cares and needs of state, and therefore he laid the care for the monastery upon his imperial successors, asking them (pp. 792–793), as well as the Patriarchs of Constantinople (p. 794), to show it every protection and material support.

The Monastery of Michael the Archangel, according to the typikon of the emperor, was recognized as self-governing and independent regarding its internal administration by its hegumen, but upon the latter was laid, however, the duty to render due honor of respect to the hierarchs of the Metropolis of Chalcedon, whose name was pronounced at litanies and prayers, and to send them annually a monetary tribute ("let there be brought to such metropolitans each year also three baskets valued at one hyperpyron each and three litras of wax": *προσ[κ]αγέσθωσαν τοῖς τοιούτοις μητροπολίταις κατ᾽ ἔτος καὶ τρία κανίσκια ἀνὰ ἓν καθὲν ὑπέρπυρον ἀξιούμενα καὶ κηροῦ λίτρας τρεῖς*) and three litras of wax [^127] (pp. 773–774). In divine services the monastery was guided by the Palestinian [^128] typikon (p. 783), while regarding the most important aspects of internal monastic life there were regulations in the typikon [^129] of the founder-emperor, which in many respects resembled...

=== LEAF p106 ===

*(Orig. p. CIII)*

...similar rules in other known ktetorik Typika, as for example in the Irene, Evergetis, Michael Attaleiates, Pantokrator, and others. Examining, therefore, the Typikon published by us of the Monastery of Michael the Archangel on the Mountain of Saint Auxentios from this aspect, we must say that it introduces little that is new for Byzantine history in general and for our specifically liturgical aims in particular, but the publication of it at the present time, nevertheless, is not useless and to a certain degree even desirable.[^130]

As a historical document written by the fortunate conqueror of the Latins, under a strong and deep feeling of antipathy toward these sworn enemies of the Greek people and of the Orthodox Church, whom he calls "implacable enemies, savage beasts" (*ἀσπόνδων ἐχθρῶν, ἀγρίων θηρῶν*) etc. (p. 794), the Typikon of Michael Palaiologos is not devoid of significance, and historians of this difficult epoch in the life of the Byzantine state will not fail to make use of it for a vivid and living characterization of it. But this is only one aspect of the given monument. This Typikon acquires interest in scholarship from another side, as a supplement to two other Typika known at present that are in one way or another connected with the name of Emperor Michael Palaiologos. We have in mind, in the first place, an extract from the Typikon of the Constantinopolitan Monastery of St. Demetrios,[^131] written by Empe-...

The text of the Typikon of the Monastery of Michael the Archangel on the Mountain of Auxentios we borrowed from manuscript No. 85 of the year 1749 of the Chalki Library, which, as it now turns out, represents a verbatim copy of a paper manuscript of the end of the 15th century, No. 160, preserved in the library of the Metochion of the Holy Sepulchre in Constantinople (in the Phanar). The text of the present Typikon according to this latter manuscript was printed by Manuel Gedeon in a special brochure under the title: *«Μιχαὴλ τοῦ Παλαιολόγου Τυπικὸν τῆς ἐπὶ τοῦ βουνοῦ τοῦ Αὐξεντίου σεβασμίας μονῆς Μιχαὴλ τοῦ ἀρχαγγέλου»*, Constantinople 1895. Since the text of the original manuscript is significantly more correct than the copy, in the "additions, corrections, and errata" at the end of the present book we have made the necessary corrections to our edition.

The title of our book apparently excludes any thought of Western, so-called ktetorik or Studite Typika, but from what follows below it will become fully understandable why we could not pass over in silence this kind of liturgical monuments and were compelled by the very essence of the matter to assign to them in the present book no small place. The cause of this circumstance lies in the following. In surveying the testaments of St. Theodore the Studite and St. Athanasios the Athonite, as well as all Byzantine ktetorik Typika currently known in print, and moreover in no small quantity, we constantly encountered the fact of the existence in the liturgical practice of the ancient Byzantine Church of the Typikon or Synaxarion of the glorious Studite Monastery. Besides the *Hypotyposis* (*Ὑποτύπωσις*) already known to us, which connects its origin directly with this monastery, and the *Diatyposis* (*Διατύπωσις*) of St. Athanasios the Athonite, as created entirely on the foundation of the *Hypotyposis*, repeatedly and very...

=== LEAF p110 ===

*(Orig. p. CVII)*
