Such a very impressive number of authoritative witnesses and their rare unanimity speak eloquently for themselves and do not arouse in us even a shadow of doubt as to the authenticity of their historical testimony. And meanwhile, alongside this fact, we have to state another, in the highest degree...

<!-- START COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort12 -->

=== LEAF p111 ===

*(Orig. p. CVIII)*

...curious fact that not a single complete copy of a pure Studite Synaxarion or Typikon has been preserved down to our times or is known to scholarship to date. On the basis of careful and numerous personal researches, it is now known to us that no such Typika exist in the most famous and richest book repositories of the Orthodox East: on Sinai, on Athos, in Jerusalem, Cairo, on Patmos, in Constantinople, in the Meteora monasteries (likewise the National Library in Athens, whither the manuscripts of these monasteries are now gathered), and in other less important libraries. If one is to trust the catalogs existing in print or the scholarly descriptions of the most important European libraries of Rome, Vienna, Paris, London, Munich, and other cities, there is no hope of discovering such Synaxaria or Typika in these libraries either.

The repeated attempt of former Russian scholars, such as Gorsky and Nevostruev,[^137] church historians Archbishop Philaret of Chernihiv,[^138] Metropolitan Makarii of Moscow,[^139] Prof. Golubinsky,[^140] Archbishop Sergius of Vladimir,[^141] Prof. Mansvetov,[^142] and many others, to consider manuscript No. 330 (380) of the Moscow Synodal Library, and with it the cognate manuscripts No. 333 (381) and No. 905 (382) of the same library,[^143] Nos. 285 and 287 of the Synodal Typography Library,[^144] and No. 1136 of the Saint Sophia Library (St. Petersburg Theological Academy),[^145] as representatives or even directly as "Studite typika"...

=== LEAF p112 ===

*(Orig. p. CIX)*

...in Slavonic translation, must now be abandoned forever. In our review of the book of Prof. Mansvetov, on the basis of positive data, we established as an indubitable fact that manuscript No. 330 (380) of the Moscow Synodal Library must be referred "to the branch of ktetorik typika,"[^146] and is neither more nor less than the ktetorik Typikon of Patriarch Alexios of Constantinople (1025–1043), while the manuscripts cognate with it in other libraries are copies or transcripts from it with only the most insignificant variants. The complete copy of the Typikon of the Great Church published by us according to the Patmos manuscript, which now affords us the opportunity to draw comparisons of ecclesiastical liturgical peculiarities in both monuments, leaves in us not the slightest doubt as to the correctness of our conclusion regarding the said manuscript. It should be added here that Prof. Mansvetov, in his investigation *Concerning the Church Typikon*, inclined, albeit indecisively, toward the very same thought.[^147]

There has been expressed, among other things, in our literature the idea—first voiced long ago—that the Studite Typikon was seen on Sinai by Patriarch Nektarios of Jerusalem (17th cent.), and that "that typikon was preserved on Mount Sinai" allegedly down to the time of this patriarch;[^148] and a second time, in the posthumous work of Bishop Porphyrius Uspensky, *The Christian East: History of Athos*, part III, section II, St. Petersburg 1892, p. 388, that in the vestry (*skeuophylakion*) of the Athonite Vatopedi Monastery there is preserved a "typikon written on parchment with the inscription: 'Typikon of the Studites' (*τῶν Στουδιτῶν*)" (ibid., cf. p. 379). Both of these reports we carefully verified on site,[^149] and regarding the second in particular we even undertook an intentional journey to Athos, but our searches in both instances proved unsuccessful. A Typikon or...

=== LEAF p113 ===

*(Orig. p. CX)*

...Synaxarion of the Studite Monastery in both famous monasteries of the Orthodox East we did not find. Thanks, however, to these searches, with respect to the typikon of the Sinai Monastery we succeeded in establishing in the most positive manner that such in the monastery ever since the year 1214 had been its own distinctive Typikon, created on the foundation of the Typikon of the lavra monastery of our venerable father Sabbas the Sanctified (*Τυπικὸν κατὰ τὸν τύπον τῆς λαύρας τοῦ ὁσίου πατρὸς ἡμῶν Σάββα τῆς ἐν Ἰεροσολύμοις μονῆς*).[^150] As for the report of Rt. Rev. Porphyrius, having discovered on site no traces whatever of the existence of such a Typikon—although by our curiosity we stirred interest even among the monastics of this monastery, who zealously assisted us in our searches—we arrived at the indubitable conviction for ourselves that Bishop Porphyrius himself had not personally seen such a copy of the Typikon, by which must be explained the fact that in his history he reports neither a detailed title of the Typikon that interests us, nor any extracts from it, which would be exceedingly important for liturgics, but borrowed his information from the booklet of Nikodemos the Hagiorite: *Confession of Faith* (*῾Ομολογία τῆς πίστεως*),[^151] in which the given report is distinguished by neither clarity nor definiteness, and therefore awakens in us little confidence toward itself.

Thus, in the East, in its monasteries and rich book repositories, we did not succeed in discovering not only complete copies of the Studite Synaxarion or Typikon, but even any fragments or extracts of it. Willy-nilly, therefore, one had to place all one's hopes upon the West, upon the libraries of Rome and its environs, since in Western...

=== LEAF p114 ===

*(Orig. p. CXI)*

...literature, ever since the 16th century positive information has been transmitted that copies of Typika of the Studite branch exist there in several exemplars. Among the majority, however, of Western scholars, as for example Leo Allatius,[^152] Du Cange,[^153] Quirini-Veneta,[^154] and others, these copies are known rather under the name *Τυπικὰ κτητορικά*, which appellation we likewise retained for them in our edition, and only by Th. Toscani,[^155] Cardinal A. Mai,[^156] Cardinal Pitra,[^157] and Fr. Ant. Rocchi,[^158] and after them also by our Russian scholars—Archbishop Sergius,[^159] Prof. I. D. Mansvetov,[^160] and others—are they designated directly as "Studite." "Toscani in his investigation on typika says," writes Archbishop Sergius, "that he had at hand seven exemplars of the Studite typikon used in Italian and Sicilian monasteries."[^161] Consequently, in order to supply the deficiency indicated above, we now had to turn to these monuments of the "Studite typikon" of Italian libraries and, even if on the basis of them alone, elucidate for ourselves the chief peculiarities and distinctions of the Studite typika compared with the typika of the Jerusalem recension well known to us. This is, of course, the primary and principal requirement for a scholarly investigator of the historical fate of the Church Typikon. But in the scholarship of Orthodox divine services there ripened with ever greater persistence another no less important need: to study the indicated "seven exemplars of the Studite...

=== LEAF p115 ===

*(Orig. p. CXII)*

In view of such scholarly needs, which were exceedingly important in their consequences, we resolved to undertake a twofold journey to the West with the purpose of surveying not only the libraries of Rome and its environs, where the Western scholars listed by us had principally worked, but also throughout all of Italy where major book repositories exist, having visited with the same purpose, while passing through Vienna, also its Imperial Library. The results of this twofold journey to the West in terms of our principal task we now offer to the benevolent attention of our readers.

In reality, according to our personal researches, it turned out that the Western, so-called ktetorik or Studite copies of the Typikon are not seven, but nine in all. To the codices listed by Toscani we can now join an exceedingly important codex in scholarly terms from the 12th–13th century preserved in the Turin University Library under No. 216 (pp. 795–823), and a manuscript of the Vienna Imperial Library of the 13th century No. 127, or No. 326, or No. 144 (pp. 893–897). The Turin codex of the Typikon, described very superficially by G. Pasini,[^162] and with greater circumstantiality, comparatively speaking, of course, by Prof. Diehl [^163] (Ch. Diehl), was not subjected to special in-...

=== LEAF p117 ===

*(Orig. p. CXIV)*

=== LEAF p118 ===

*(Orig. p. CXV)*

...acquaintance with them on our part, divided spontaneously into two groups or recensions, whose chief characteristic features, both external and internal, emerged in very sharp relief.

XII. Into the composition of the first group enter: (a) the manuscript of the Turin Library of the 12th–13th century, representing a transcript from a still more ancient manuscript written in the year 1174 by the third hegumen of the Casole Monastery, the monk Nicholas (1153–1190) (p. 795); (b) the manuscript of the Barberini Library III No. 69, written at the instigation of the sixth hegumen (1201–1220) of the Casole Monastery, the Hieromonk Nikodemos, by the Hieromonk (*Ἱεροθέου μοναχοῦ καὶ ἀναξίου θύτου* [Hierotheos, monk and unworthy sacrificer]) Hierotheos in the year 1205 [^167] (p. 283); (c) the manuscript of the Roman Vallicelliana Library D No. 61 of the 13th century, which was in use among the clergy of the Diocese of Nardò (*Neretino*) (p. 833); and (d) the second manuscript of the Barberini Library II No. 102, written by the priest of the city of Soleto, Stephanos Ripa (*Ῥήπα*), in the year 1583 from an ancient codex that was in the possession of the Archbishop of Soleto in the Diocese of Hydruntum (Otranto), Antonios Arkoudios, and sent in parts to Rome in the months of July and October of the same year as necessary material for the printing there in 1598 by Antonios Arkoudios of the renowned *Anthologion* (pp. 833–836).

As the characteristic representative of this group of monuments must undoubtedly be recognized, in view of the noted date—the year 1174—the ancient Turin codex bearing the following title: **"Typikon, with God, that is, the diataxis [of the entire year] [^168] of the ecclesiastical constitution and office both of the dominical feasts and of the most holy Theotokos, and of the renowned saints, and of the entire year, according to the tradition of the holy and God-bearing fathers, both of Saint Sabbas and of the Studite, and for the most part of the Holy Mountain, not only so, but also a certain portion of the tradition of our most vene-"** (*«Τυπικὸν σὺν Θεῷ, ἤτοι διάταξις [τοῦ ὅλου χρόνου] τῆς ἐκκλησιαστικῆς καταστάσεως καὶ ἀκολουθίας τῶν τε δεσποτικῶν ἑορτῶν καὶ τῆς ὑπεραγίας Θεοτόκου, καὶ τῶν ἐπισήμων ἁγίων καὶ ὅλου τοῦ ἐνιαυτοῦ, κατὰ τὴν παράδοσιν τῶν ἁγίων καὶ θεοφόρων πατέρων τοῦ τε ἁγίου Σάββα, καὶ τοῦ Στουδίτου καὶ τὸ πλεῖστον τοῦ Ἁγίου Ὄρους, οὐ μὴν, ἀλλὰ καὶ μέρος τι τῆς παραδόσεως τοῦ ὁσιω-...»*)...

=== LEAF p119 ===

*(Orig. p. CXVI)*

...**"-rable father Joseph, [ktetor] of the [holy] monastery of Saint Nicholas of Casole, declaring [^169] and distinguishing both the obligatory and traditional fasts and prostrations, both how and when they ought to take place and when not; and first in certain chapters declaring these things in summary,[^170] and then also setting them forth more clearly in due order at the proper and suitable [place and] time."** (*«...τάτου πατρὸς ἡμῶν Ἰωσήφ, [τοῦ κτήτορος] τῆς [ἁγίας] μονῆς τοῦ ἁγίου Νικολάου τῶν Κασούλων, δηλοῦσα καὶ διακρίνουσα τάς τε ὀφειλομένας καὶ παραδεδομένας νηστείας καὶ γονυκλισίας, πῶς τε καὶ πότε ὀφείλουσι γίνεσθαι καὶ πότε οὔ. Καὶ πρῶτον μὲν ἐν κεφαλαίοις τισὶν ἐν ἐπιτομῇ ταῦτα προδηλοῦν, ἔπειτα δὲ καὶ τρανωτέρως κατὰ τάξιν ἐν τῷ ἰδίῳ καὶ προσφόρῳ [τόπῳ καὶ] καιρῷ διαλαμβάνειν»*). This title, with the exception of the most insignificant variants, was present, it must be supposed, also in all the other presently defective manuscripts of the first group. A confirmation of this can be found in the completely identical contents of these codices. As is evident from the title just cited, the copies of this recension, while pointing out their eclecticism from many of the most renowned Typika of the Orthodox East, at the same time persistently declare also that they have introduced onto their pages a certain portion (*μέρος τι παραδόσεως*) from the traditions or customs of the renowned Otrantan Monastery of Nicholas of Casole in Southern Italy.

The Monastery of Nicholas of Casole was located on the easternmost tip of the Calabrian peninsula, "by means of which, it seems, as Prof. Diehl observes, Italy as it were extends a hand toward Greece,"[^171] and was erected by Greek immigrants in the year 1099, with the assistance and aid of the renowned warrior Bohemond, Prince of Taranto and Antioch. The founder of the monastery, its first hegumen and in every respect solicitous builder, was Joseph († 1125), who for fully twenty-six years held the reins of governance in the monastery founded by him. To this most active and most enlightened hegumen the Casole Monastery owes that elevated role which it played in the history of humanism as a nursery of Greek enlightenment throughout all Italy, and that firm internal organization which it received in the form of a special *diataxis* (*διάταξις*).[^172] It is not surprising, therefore, that...

=== LEAF p120 ===

*(Orig. p. CXVII)*

...the Monastery of Nicholas of Casole always reverently honored the memory of its ktetor alongside the other chief benefactors of the monastery, such as Roger the Great († 1101), Roger II, King of the Two Sicilies (1130–1154), and others; annually celebrated his memory triumphantly on September 8 (*ἡ κοίμησις* [the dormition]); translated his incorrupt relics (*ἡ ἀνακομιδὴ τοῦ λειψάνου*) on October 8 in the year 1127 from the sepulcher into the monastic church and placed them in a specially prepared shrine (*εἰς τὸν λάρνακα*), which was subsequently repeated (*ἡ δευτέρα ἀνακομιδή* [the second translation]) also on July 16 in the year 1196.[^173] The successors of Hegumen Joseph—Victor (1125–1153) and especially Nicholas (1153–1190), who gained renown for his erudition and love of literary pursuits—proved to be active continuators of the labors of their famous predecessor and to a significant degree consolidated the fame of their monastery. From the latter we now possess in the Turin manuscript No. 216 a complete copy of the Typikon written by him in the year 1174, and the *Hypotyposis* (*Ὑποτύπωσις*) concerning the food and drink of the monastics in the Monastery of Nicholas of Casole composed by him in the year 1161 (pp. 795, 818–823). In the same spirit and direction, without doubt, proceeded activity in the monastery also under the subsequent hegumenoi: Kallinikos (1190–1195), Hilarion (1195–1201), and Nikodemos (1201–1220), although we possess no definite information concerning these hegumenoi. However, concerning the last hegumen, it is known to us that at his initiative the Typikon currently preserved in the Barberini Library III No. 69 was written in the year 1205 by the Hieromonk Hierotheos (p. 823). Under Hegumen Nektarios (1220–1235), not unknown also for his literary works, the Monastery of Nicholas of Casole, which had hitherto remained, thanks to the patronage of its powerful protectors, Prince Bohemond and his consort Constance, who temporarily governed the prin-...

---

<!-- START COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort13 -->

=== LEAF p121 ===

*(Orig. p. CXVIII)*

...cipality during the minority of her son Bohemond II († 1130), Prince of Taranto and Antioch; under Roger the Great, Roger II (1101–1154), King of the Two Sicilies, his son William (1154–1166) and his consort Elvira (more correctly—Margaret), their sons Duke Roger and King William II (1166–1189), completely autonomous and independent in all that concerned its internal organization, was subordinated to Archbishop Tancred of Otranto and began to pay the Roman Church a specified monetary tribute (*payait à l’eglise de Rome un cens déterminé*). During the reign of Charles of Anjou (1265–1285), a zealous defender of the Roman curia and highly ill-disposed toward Greek monks [^174] (p. 791), the subordination of the present monastery to Rome was expressed still more definitively. On November 19, 1267, the Roman Cardinal Rodolphe reconsecrated the church of the Nicholas of Casole Monastery and in place of Hegumen Basil (1259–1267) installed a new hegumen, James I (*Iakovos I*) (1267–1275), as a person, in all probability, more devoted to the Roman pontiff (p. 796). These facts, as well as the fact that the annual payment by the monastery to Rome had significantly increased by this time (*à cinq onces d’or et cinq taris par an*), clearly testify that the Nicholas of Casole Monastery at the end of the 13th century lost its independence and definitively submitted to the authority of the popes [^175].

The Greek emigrant monks who settled in the southern part of the Apennine peninsula followed the example of the homeland they had abandoned in everything concerning the internal organization of these monasteries and the liturgical orders within them. Following the custom of the most renowned monasteries of Byzantium, the Greek Italian monasteries created their own Typika or rules of church services, borrowing content for them primarily from the most renowned Typika of the Orthodox East. Both in the title cited above and in the contents of the Typika of the first recension, we find everywhere clear...

=== LEAF p122 ===

*(Orig. p. CXIX)*

...indications of their compilers' familiarity with the renowned typika of the Byzantine Church—of St. Sabbas the Sanctified, of St. Theodore the Studite, of the Holy Mountain of Athos (pp. 828, 829), of the Great Church of Constantinople (p. 828) or otherwise the patriarchal Typikon (*ἀκολουθία τοῦ πατριαρχικοῦ Τυπικοῦ*) (p. 832), of the Typikon unknown to us (*κεφάλαια τῶν παραλειπομένων* [chapters of omissions]) of John the Faster (p. 834), and others [^176]. The compilers of the Typika in the West were also well informed concerning the role that was granted in Byzantine monasteries to their ktetors or founders, who, as we know, could freely introduce into the liturgical practice of their monasteries special customs unknown to other monasteries. On this basis, in the Western Typika of the present recension a certain place is allotted to the practice of the Casole Monastery, formed under the influence of the *Diataxis* of the first hegumen of the monastery, Joseph, and known in the Typikon either under the name of his traditions (*παράδοσις*), or even the traditions of the church (*ἡ ἐκκλησία ἡμῶν* [our church] or *ἡ ἐκκλησία τῆς ἡμετέρας μονῆς* [the church of our monastery]) of the Casole Monastery (pp. 813, 814, 828, 833, and elsewhere). It is necessary in this connection to note that in all local Casole peculiarities of this kind there is nothing that would stand in contradiction to the practice of the Greek Byzantine Church or would recall any influences alien to the Orthodox Church.

Besides those noted—the title and the gravitation toward the practice of the Nicholas of Casole Monastery—in the Typika of the first recension it is not...

=== LEAF p123 ===

*(Orig. p. CXX)*

...superfluous to draw attention also to the constantly immutable composition of all copies of it. The contents of the Typikon are prefaced by special chapters to the number of 28, containing observations of a general character concerning the daily and annual cycles of divine services (a certain resemblance to the present "Mark's Chapters"). These chapters stand in the manuscripts themselves without headings, and in two codices (the Barberini No. 69 and the Vallicelliana No. 61), owing to the absence in them of the opening folios, are not present at all, although their existence here also can hardly be doubted. As a characteristic heading for them must be recognized that which is given by the Typikon of the Laurentian Library [^177] in Florence No. 15, written in the year 1336 by the Reader John Stavaleres (*τοῦ Σταβαλέρη*) from the town of Saragusa (*ἐκ χώρας Σαραγούσας*). This heading reads thus: **"Chapters declaring in summary the custom of the ecclesiastical constitution of the Holy Mountain"** (*«Κεφάλαια ἐν ἐπιτόμως δηλοῦντα τὴν συνήθειαν τῆς ἐκκλησιαστικῆς καταστάσεως τοῦ Ἁγίου Ὄρους»*) [^178]. We have noted nearly all the peculiarities of the exposition of the present chapters according to the Laurentian copy in comparison with their exposition in the examined codices of the Typikon of the first recension (pp. 797–809 in the notes). The next part of the Typika under review, following the noted chapters from the Typikon of the Holy Mountain, constitutes a very brief Menologion with commemorations and services only for the days of great feasts and of saints especially venerated in the Orthodox Church (pp. 809–817), wherein the services of the feast of the Annunciation are placed in all codices of this recension in the third part, among the services of the Triodion cycle (p. 828). The third part is always titled thus: **"Synaxarion, with God, beginning from the Publican and the Pharisee unto All Saints"** (*«Συναξάριον, σὺν Θεῷ ἀρχόμενον ἀπὸ τοῦ τελώνου καὶ τοῦ φαρισαίου μέχρι τῶν Ἁγίων Πάντων»*), and, in the composition of services and their textual peculiarities, is everywhere identical (pp. 817, 833, 834). To the named three principal parts is joined a fourth...

=== LEAF p124 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXI)*

...supplementary part, which, owing to incompleteness and defects in the manuscripts, is far from identical in composition across all codices. The most complete in this respect must be considered the Barberini codex III No. 102 (236) (pp. 834–835), and the briefest the Vallicelliana codex D. No. 61, in which this last part is entirely absent (p. 833).

XIII. Into the composition of the second recension of Western Typika enter: (a) the manuscript of the Vatican Library No. 1877, written in the year 1292 by Paul the Corinthian, a native of the Italian city of Troina (*Τραΐνα*), who lived first in the Monastery of the Holy Apostles in Agros (*ἐκ μονῆς Ἄγρους*), and then settled (*κάτοικος*) in the most ancient Mother of God Monastery of Mili (*τοῦ Μῆλι*), for which was prepared by him (*κατὰ τὴν ἡμετέραν ἐκκλησίαν τῆς αὐτῆς μονῆς τῆς ὑπεραγίας Θεοτόκου τοῦ Μέλι* [according to our church of the same monastery of the most holy Theotokos of Mili]) the present Typikon (p. 886); (b) the 13th-century manuscript No. 127, or No. 326, or No. 144 of the Vienna Library (p. 893); (c) the manuscript of the Barberini Library III No. 78, written in the year 1558 by a certain George from Constantinople for the cathedral church of the city of Bova (*Βοός*), through the zeal and at the expense of the clergy of this church, during the episcopacy of Aquila Pratzi (*᾿Ακύλα Πράτζι*) (p. 895); and (d) the second manuscript of the Vatican Library No. 1609, written in the 16th century also by George and presented to Pope Sixtus V for the Vatican Library by Emmanuel, chorepiscopus of Rhodes, in the year 1585 (p. 898).

Since the enumerated copies of the Typika of the second recension are for the most part without beginning, or lack titles, the latter, as most characteristic for the monuments of this group, we can point out in the 16th-century Vatican manuscript No. 1609, in which this title reads thus: **"Typikon, with God, containing the entire order of the ecclesiastical constitution of Theodore the Studite and the rest"** (*«Σὺν Θεῷ Τυπικόν, περιέχον τὴν ἅπασαν ἀκολουθίαν τῆς ἐκκλησιαστικῆς καταστάσεως Θεοδώρου τοῦ Στουδίτου καὶ λοιπῶν»*) (p. 898), while considering at the same time as the most prominent and more ancient representative of the Typika of this second recension the manuscript of the same Vatican Library No. 1877, written in the year 1292. The latter manuscript No. 1877, as is evident from the extensive scribal colophon appended to it (p. 836), connects its origin with the most ancient Sicilian Monastery of the Mother of God, "of Mili" (*τοῦ Μῆλι* or *Μέλι*).

=== LEAF p125 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXII)*

This monastery is located in the village of Flomaria (*in pago Flomariae*) on the river Mili (*in fluvio Mili*), seven miles south of the city of Messina. It was founded around the year 1089, in memory of victories over the Saracens, by Roger, Great Count of Calabria and Sicily († 1101), and was granted for settlement to Hegumen Michael and his fellow countrymen, Greek emigrants from Byzantium, whom this ruler always patronized [^179]. As a monument of this kind of benefactions can serve the donations and sigillia given by Roger the Great to this monastery [^180], and others. The Monastery of the Mother of God of Mili (*τοῦ Μῆλι*), having arisen, although somewhat earlier in time, under almost identical conditions to the renowned Nicholas of Casole Monastery, lived with it an entirely identical historical life and shared the same sorrowful fate, that is, in the end fell under the authority of the Roman pontiff. The difference in the fate of the two monasteries is only that the Mili Monastery continues its existence even to the present time [^181], whereas the Casole Monastery represents today only sorrowful ruins [^182].

=== LEAF p126 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXIII)*

However, so far as the Typika of the second recension described by us permit us to judge concerning the historical past of the Sicilian Mother of God Monastery of Mili (*τοῦ Μῆλι*) and its significance in the series of other South Italian Greek monasteries, we must in justice say that this past was far from being so glorious and brilliant as that of the Nicholas of Casole Monastery. Only by this, in our opinion, can be explained the fact that in the Vatican codex No. 1877 the local customs of the Mili Monastery, its ktetorik practice, found no place for themselves and were not reflected on its pages even in slight measure [^183], unless one takes into account certain names of saints of Sicilian and generally Western origin (pp. 844, 856, 858, 860, 861, 862) in its Synaxarion, which did not persist, however, even in the Synaxaria of other copies of the Typikon of the same recension (see variants *ibid.*). This is first. Secondly, herein lies also the reason why this codex of the Vatican Library, in the series of copies of the same recension, was far from having that depersonalizing and overwhelming significance which we see in the codex of the Turin Library No. 216 in relation to monuments homogeneous with it. Among the monuments of the second group nearly every codex has its own peculiar commemorations, its own liturgical distinctions, and sometimes quite significant differences in the exposition of one and the same services (see pp. 837–892 in the variants). Noting, in this manner, two characteristic peculiarities in the Typika of the second recension, we cannot fail to point out also a third, more essential and important feature of theirs. Copies of this category, having modestly declared in the title (MS of the Vatican Library No. 1609) that they contain within themselves the **"Typikon, containing the entire order of the ecclesiastical constitution of Theodore the Studite and the rest-"** (*«Τυπικόν, περιέχον τὴν ἅπασαν ἀκολουθίαν τῆς ἐκκλησιαστικῆς καταστάσεως Θεοδώρου τοῦ Στουδίτου καὶ λοι-...»*)...

=== LEAF p127 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXIV)*

...**"-rest,"** call not more than twice among the number of "the rest" the most renowned typika—of the Great Church, the Jerusalem, the Studite, the Holy Mountain of Athos (pp. 855, 863), the Olympian (p. 855 note), and the name of that same hierarch Antony (*δι’ ἐρωτήσεως τοῦ ἀρχιερατεύσαντος τοῦ ᾿Αντωνίου ὁσιωτάτου* [through inquiry of the most venerable Antony who served as hierarch]) (p. 853, p. 855 note 1), whose answers were utilized also by the compiler of the Evergetis Typikon (p. 442), with which the monuments of the recension described by us sometimes have great similarity (p. 853, p. 855 note 1, 866, 877) in the exposition of certain services. In a word, observing attentively the copies of this recension of Western Typika, one cannot fail to notice that they almost entirely gravitate toward the Eastern Byzantine Typika and reproduce on their pages their liturgical structure and order, without allotting a place to ktetorik practice even in that insignificant degree which is given to it in the Western Typika of the first recension. In this respect, concerning the Typika of this recension one cannot fail to pronounce that verdict which a certain anonymous hand inscribed on the endleaf of the 16th-century Vatican codex No. 1609, namely: "collected from the Eastern (Typika)" (*«συνάξαν ἐκ τῶν ἀνατολικῶν [Τυπικῶν]»*).

Besides all this, in the Typika of the second recension one can point also to other peculiarities, but pertaining already to their composition. Here too, as in the monuments of the first recension, the contents of the Typikon are also prefaced by a special article, which, although not present today across all copies of this recension, can hardly be doubted to have been present in them earlier, when the copies were in complete preservation (p. 872 note 3, 894). This article, titled thus: **"Chapters declaring in summary [the custom] of the ecclesiastical constitution"** (*«Κεφάλαια ἐν ἐπιτόμῳ δηλοῦντα τῆς ἐκκλησιαστικῆς καταστάσεως»*) (p. 836), in its exposition, however, possesses significant peculiarities [^184] in comparison with the similar...

=== LEAF p128 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXV)*

...article in the Typika of the first recension. The Synaxarion, which constitutes the second part of the Typikon, is distinguished by an abundance of commemorations, several being indicated for each day. The names of the saints, with very insignificant exceptions, as we have already observed above, are of Byzantine origin. Remarkable here is the circumstance that even at the latest period of the existence of Greek monasteries on the Apennine peninsula in the 16th century, when these monasteries were already in subordination to the Roman pontiff and when the influence of the liturgical practice of the Catholic Church upon the practice of these monasteries was indisputably strong, the Synaxaria of the Western Typika [^185] do not give place to the names and feasts of the Western Church alongside the names and feasts of the Orthodox Byzantine Church. A striking example of this we see in the manuscript of the Barberini Library of the year 1552 [1558] III No. 78, containing within itself the Typikon of the cathedral church of the city of Bova in Calabria. Here the Latin Menologion, or **"Office and order of the Menologion according to the Latins"** (*«᾿Ακολουθία καὶ τάξις τοῦ Μηνολογίου κατὰ Λατίνων»*) (pp. 896–897), necessary in the practice...

=== LEAF p129 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXVI)*

...of the named cathedral church, stands in the manuscript separately, at the end of it, as an appended article. The third part of the Typika of this recension—the Triodion part—has an inconsistent heading and is titled, at times **"Office of Holy Lent, beginning from the Publican and the Pharisee"** (*«᾿Ακολουθία τῆς ἁγίας τεσσαρακοστῆς, ἀρχομένη ἀπὸ τοῦ τελώνου καὶ τοῦ φαриσαίου»*) (pp. 863, 895), at times **"On the Sunday whereon is read the holy Gospel of the Publican and the Pharisee"** (*«Τῇ κυριακῇ, ἐν ᾗ ἀναγινώσκεται τὸ ἱερὸν εὐαγγέλιον τοῦ τελώνου καὶ τοῦ φαриσαίου»*) (p. 894), at times **"Synaxarion, with God, of Holy Lent, beginning from the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee unto the Sunday of All Saints"** (*«Συναξάриον σὺν Θεῷ τῆς ἁγίας τεσσαραкоστῆς, ἀρχόμενον ἀπὸ τῆς κυριακῆς τοῦ τελώνου καὶ τοῦ φαриσαίου ἕως τῆς κυριακῆς τῶν Ἁγίων Πάντων»*) (p. 898). In the supplementary part we find very few articles, which primarily concern food during the fasts (p. 894), prostrations in church and in cells, and the days when kolyvo is prepared (p. 898).

In order to present still more vividly the characteristic peculiarities of both recensions of the Typika described, let us enter into an examination of the most important distinctions in the services of their annual and daily cycles.

The services of the annual cycle in both recensions of the Typika are expounded for the most part similarly, but far from identically. In the Typika of the second recension, for example, the services of the Synaxarion are expounded significantly more simply and briefly than in the monuments...

=== LEAF p130 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXVII)*

...of the first recension. Examples of this can be seen in the services for the Nativity of the Most Holy Theotokos (p. 809 and notes 2–11, 810 and notes 1–7), for the feast of the Elevation of the Precious Cross of the Lord (p. 811, cf. p. 839), of the Nativity of Christ (Th. Toscani, *Ad Typica Graecorum*, pp. 44–51), and of the Annunciation (*ibid.*, pp. 56–65). A major peculiarity in the Synaxaria of the Typika of both recensions is constituted by the celebration of forefeasts and afterfeasts with dispensation for all foods not only for the Twelve Great Feasts, but also for the days of great and venerated saints, such as, for example, the Apostles John the Theologian and Andrew, St. Nicholas the Wonderworker, Righteous Anna the mother of the Most Holy Virgin, and others (pp. 813, 814, note 6; 843 and note 2; 845). In the Triodion services, similar in exposition can be considered in part the services of the preparatory weeks (Th. Toscani, *Ad Typica Graecorum*, pp. 52–55), and almost completely identical the services of Passion Week, beginning from Matins of Great Friday up to Pentecost (pp. 880–889, cf. 830–832; Toscani, *Ad Typica*, pp. 66–70). The differences here consist, according to the first recension, in special psalms for the Hours of Great Saturday and in Compline for the eve of Pascha (pp. 829–830), and according to the second, in a more detailed exposition of the rite of the Paschal kiss and generally of the conclusion of Matins on Pascha (pp. 886–887). But essential peculiarities of both recensions we see in the Lenten services, beginning with Monday of the first week. According to the first recension, all daily services in the first week of Great Lent are celebrated as customary, but with the reading of the Gospels throughout the entire week at all the Hours, not excluding even the First Hour; the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts was celebrated every day throughout the entire Great Fast, with the exception of Monday and Tuesday of the first week, when only hierarchs could officiate this service in cathedrals and great churches; at the Liturgy on the first Sunday of Lent, after the Trisagion, the Synodikon was read: **"That which is due unto God"** (*«᾿Εποφειλομένη πρὸς Θεόν»*); on Great Thursday were celebrated the Hours with special psalms and the order of the washing of feet [^186] at the end of the Liturgy (pp. 825–829). According to...

<!-- END COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort13 -->

---

<!-- START COHORT 1895_dmitrievsky_vol1_cohort14 -->

=== LEAF p131 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXVIII)*

...the second recension, all services of the first week are chanted [^187] (*ᾀσματικὴ ἀκολουθία*); the celebration of the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts each day is not obligatory; on Fridays throughout the entire year, with the exception of feasts of the Lord, there was performed the **"order of intercession"** (*«ἡ τάξις τῆς πρεσβείας»*) or paraklesis in honor of the Mother of God; at the Sixth Hour from Monday of the second week through the entire Great Fast, the brethren occupied in handiwork (*ὅσοι ἐργόχειρα ἐργάζονται*) assemble in the church to hear the catecheses of Theodore the Studite; whereas in the Typika of the first recension, instead of this, from Monday of the first week until Thursday of Palm Week, with the exception of Saturdays and Sundays, after the Third Hour the canonarch, having a koukoulion upon his head and a staff (*ῥάβδος*) in his hands, pronounced before the trapezarios in a loud voice (*βοᾷ μετὰ κραυγῆς ἰσχυρᾶς*) the following words: **"Lord, bless. Brethren and fathers, let us be attentive to ourselves, for we die; let us remember also eternal torment"** (p. 826); on the eve of Palm Sunday before the chanting of **"Lord, I have cried"**...

=== LEAF p132 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXIX)*

...the choirs exchanged places; the four Evangelists were read at the Hours on the first four days of Passion Week and were concluded on Great Thursday at the Ninth Hour; the Hours even in Passion Week on the first three days were celebrated in chanted form (*пѣсненнаго изложенія*), and on Thursday with three psalms and with the Great Litany and the Litany of Supplication; the washing of feet took place between Vespers and the Liturgy, after the chanting of the stichera at **"Lord, I have cried"**, before the Little Entrance with the Gospel; on the feast of Pentecost was appointed the rite of Chanted Vespers (pp. 866–880, 889–892), but without the reading of the Epistle and Gospel, of which some (p. 832, note 1) of the Typika of the first recension make mention.

As regards the peculiarities of the daily services, both recensions of the Typika exhibit in this respect a remarkable solidarity with each other almost down to the smallest details. In order to group these peculiarities, it is necessary to contrast them with the practice, well known to us, of the Jerusalem Typikon, the Typikon of the Great Church, and the ktetorik typika of ancient Byzantium.

Of the daily services, the Western Typika of both recensions are completely unacquainted with Little Vespers and the All-Night Vigil, which are practiced widely today under the influence of the Jerusalem Typikon; neither do they mention pannychides or agrypniai, which the Typikon of the Great Church and all Byzantine ktetorik Typika prescribe to be celebrated on the eve of Sundays, feasts of the Lord, of the Theotokos, in honor of especially venerated saints, and patronal feasts; but on the other hand, there are indicated in them several orders of Compline and Midnight Office with the so-called *ktetorik psalms* [^188] and all the remaining services known in present practice and from other most ancient typika. The highest number of stichera at **"Lord, I have cried"** for great...

=== LEAF p133 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXX)*

...feasts and Sundays is *eight*, and for ordinary days their number can even be *four*. At Matins on great feasts one kathisma is appointed for reading, but instead there are chanted three antiphons of psalms chosen appropriately for the celebrated event, and the Polyeleos. An unequal number of troparia at the canon is appointed in the morning services: there were read 8, and 10, and even 14 of them, as today. At the Liturgy antiphons are chanted not only on the days of the Twelve Great Feasts, but even on the commemoration days of great saints. In the latter case it is not infrequently indicated to chant the weekday antiphons: **"It is good [to give praise unto the Lord]"** (*«Благо есть»*), etc.

XIV. The last, ninth copy of the Western Greek Typikon has the following extensive inscription: **"Synaxarion, with God, that is, Typikon, Canonarion, containing the entire order of the church for the whole year: both of the psalmody and of the canons, and of the readings, and on which days we rest from customary toil, and on which commemorations of the saints we observe rest from the Hours and make metanias; and it contains also certain other matters contributing to the synaxis of the church, which was renewed from the ancient Typikon, which had been framed by our holy father Bartholomew the Younger of Rossano [Rousianos], by decree of the most reverend father Lord Blasius II, hegumen of the renowned Monastery of the Most Holy Theotokos, named of Cryptaferrata [Grottaferrata], in the year of the creation of the world 6808, and of the incarnation of our Lord Jesus Christ 1300, in the thirteenth indiction"** (*«Συναξάριον σὺν Θεῷ, ἤγουν Τυπικόν, Κανωνάριον, περιέχον τοῦ ὅλου χρόνου τὴν τῆς ἐκκλησίας ἀκολουθίαν· τῆς τε στιχολογίας καὶ τῶν κανόνων, καὶ ἀναγνωσμάτων, καὶ ποίας τῶν ἡμερῶν σχολάζομεν ἀπὸ τοῦ συνήθους καμάτου, καὶ εἰς ποίας τῶν ἁγίων μνήμας ἐπιτελοῦμεν ἀργίαν τῶν ὡρῶν καὶ μετανοίας ποιοῦμεν· περιέχει δὲ καὶ ἄлла τινὰ τὰ εἰς τὴν σύναξιν τῆς ἐκκλησίας συντελοῦντα, ὅπερ ἀνακαινίσθη ἀπὸ τοῦ παλαιοῦ Τυπικοῦ, τοῦ τυπωθέντος παρὰ τοῦ ὁσίου πατρὸς ἡμῶν Βαρθολομαίου τοῦ νέου τοῦ Ῥουσιανίτου, δι’ ὁρισμοῦ τοῦ εὐλαβεστάτου πατρὸς κυροῦ δευτέρου Βλασίου, καθηγουμένου τῆς περιβλέπτου μονῆς τῆς ὑπεραγίας Θεοτόκου, τῆς ἐπονομαζομένης τῆς Κρυπτοφέρρης, ἐν ἔτει τῆς τοῦ κόσμου κτίσεως ἑξακισχιλιοστῷ ὀκτακοσιοστῷ η΄, τῆς δὲ τοῦ Κυρίου ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ ἐνανθρωπήσεως χιλιοστῷ τριακοσιοστῷ ἰνδικτιῶνος δεκάτης τρίτης»*). The cited title speaks for itself and with sufficient clarity allows us to understand that in this codex we have an authentic *ktetorik Typikon* of the Theotokos Monastery of Grottaferrata, whose origin is connected with the hegumens of this monastery, Bartholomew of Rossano and Blasius II.

The Grottaferrata or Cryptaferrata Monastery of the Mother of God is located in Tusculanum near Frascati, at no great distance from Rome (not more than 30 versts). Its founder is considered to be the monk Nilus, descended from the noble and wealthy ancient Byzantine family of Melenas (*Malenarum seu Malai-...*)

=== LEAF p134 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXXI)*

...*norum*), which had migrated together with other emigrants from Byzantium to Rossano, the wealthiest city of Calabria. He was born around the year 910 and lived until the age of 30 in his native city, dedicating his time to learning, when he finally resolved to enter the Monastery of St. Mercurius not far from Rossano. Loving solitude, however, he departed this monastery and founded his own monastery in honor of the Archangel Michael in the neighboring mountains, wherein he lived for a full 17 years. Upon its destruction by the Saracens, St. Nilus wandered for a long time among other foreign monasteries, until he finally resolved, together with two of his disciples and future—as is supposed—successors in the hegumenate at the Grottaferrata Monastery, Paul and Bartholomew the Younger, to set out for Tusculanum. Here he first settled in the Greek Monastery of St. Agatha, but later, having requested as a gift from Gregory, the lord of Tusculanum, who had grown very fond of St. Nilus, a plot of land near the hamlet of Cryptaferrata (which gave its name also to the founded monastery), he founded his own monastery, which, however, he did not succeed in completing, as he reposed on September 25, 1004 (= 6513). This monastery was built up and endowed with property holdings providing means for the maintenance of the monks living in it by the disciples of St. Nilus, Hegumens Paul and Bartholomew the Younger. Especially much labor was expended in this regard by the latter, likewise a native of the city of Rossano, a member also of a noble Greek family. He constructed in the monastery in the year 1025 (p. 905) a splendid church in honor of the Mother of God, which exists with certain alterations even unto the present time [^189].

It is to this third hegumen after Nilus, Bartholomew, that the Grottaferrata Typikon of the year 1300 attributes the compilation and introduction into liturgical practice of a special ktetorik Typikon (*«ἐκ τοῦ παλαιοῦ Τυπικοῦ, τοῦ τυπωθέντος παρὰ τοῦ ὁσίου πατρὸς...»*)...

=== LEAF p135 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXXII)*

...**our holy father Bartholomew the Younger of Rossano [Rousianos]"** (*«...ἡμῶν Βαρθολομαίου τοῦ νέου τοῦ Ῥουσιανίτου»*). Although in the Life of this father, written by his disciples, there is, in truth, not even a faint hint of this fact, yet we have no sufficient grounds to doubt that the original Typikon of the Theotokos Monastery of Grottaferrata came from the hands of the third hegumen of this monastery. On the contrary, as it seems to us, it was entirely natural for a caring hegumen, who completed the work of his renowned predecessor, Hegumen Nilus, by the creation of an excellent church in the monastery in honor of the Mother of God, to take care also for a more solid internal organization of this monastery and the regulation of liturgical orders within it by compiling, according to the custom of ancient Byzantine ktetors, a special ktetorik Typikon. Th. Toscani inclines toward the very same thought and in his study on Western typika presents other considerations in its favor [^190]. Here only that fact noted in the inscription might seem somewhat incomprehensible and even strange: that the ktetorik Bartholomew Typikon, by decree of Blasius II, hegumen of the same monastery, in the year 1300 was *renewed* (*ἀνακαινίσθη*), and not *transcribed* (*ἀπεγράφη*) word for word, which would have been more natural; but even this fact, strange at first glance, becomes completely understandable upon closer acquaintance with the historical circumstances of the life of this monastery.

It is beyond all doubt that the first ktetor of the Grottaferrata Monastery, St. Nilus, as a native of Southern Italy and a pupil of the local Greek monasteries, introduced into the monastery founded by him, both in monastic life and in church liturgical practice, the very same orders to which he had been accustomed from childhood in his homeland, and strictly observed them in his monastery. In favor of this speaks the eloquent fact from the life of St. Nilus, that having settled in Tusculanum and categorically declared: **"This is my rest unto ages of ages: neither shall anyone lead me away hence"** (*«Haec requies mea est in saeculum saeculi: neque hinc futurum est, ut quisquam me deducat»*), notwithstanding the urgent persuasions of his friends and noble Roman patricians, who were very favorably disposed...

=== LEAF p136 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXXIII)*

...toward him, to visit the tombs of the holy Apostles Peter and Paul, *he was never in Rome in his entire life* [^191]. With such strength of will and character, as well as persistence, it is difficult to admit any outside influences whatever in the monastery founded by St. Nilus. More favorable conditions, apparently, for these influences could have existed during the reign of the third hegumen of the Grottaferrata Monastery, Bartholomew the Younger of Rossano, who entered upon the hegumenate after the brief rule over the monastery of his predecessor Paul. This hegumen, in his relations with the Roman see, upon which at that time sat John XIX (1024–1033) and Benedict IX (1033–1048), did not follow the cautious policy of his teacher, but availed himself of the friendship of the popes and in necessary cases even of their services for the monastery. He not only visited Rome, but participated in a Lateran Council in the year 1044 and signed second among the number of hegumens: *"Bartholomaeus abbas s. Mariae Grottaferrata"* (sic) [^192]. But these friendly relations with Rome under Hegumen Bartholomew, as it turns out, did not influence the alteration of the monastic and liturgical order in the Grottaferrata Monastery established by its founder St. Nilus, whom, as a disciple and his pupil, Bartholomew of Rossano [Rousianos] in his life wished not only to follow and imitate in everything, but even to emulate [^193]. Confirmation of this we find in the Typikon of the Grottaferrata Monastery itself and in other data. Orienting oneself in the contents of the Typikon, it is difficult not to notice that in the services common to all known Greek Typika of Western origin, as, for example, on the Twelve Great Feasts and the days of the most important saints, there exists great similarity. In particular, in the exposition of these services the Cryptaferrata Typikon stands closest of all to the Typika of the second recension. One can be visually convinced of this from the comparative table (*Specimen comparationis diversorum Typicorum orientalium atque Italo-Graecorum, quorum pleraque hactenus inedita*) appended by Th. Toscani to his work:...

=== LEAF p137 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXXIV)*

...*Ad Typica Graecorum* (pp. 44–77), if we keep in view the first, third, and fourth columns in this table [^194]. Of the very same thing we are persuaded also by the liturgical Menaia and other church books that were used in the practice of the 11th–12th centuries of the Grottaferrata Monastery and are preserved to this day in its library [^195], which, in their contents and liturgical peculiarities, diverge completely from the Typikon of Hegumen Blasius II, eloquently testifying at the same time to different orders in the monastery in the most ancient period [^196].

However, in the further historical life of the Grottaferrata Monastery circumstances changed greatly: it lost its independence and was subordinated to Rome. When, after the death of Hilary, Blasius II (1282–1303) entered upon the hegumenate, for confirmation in his office he was already obliged to turn to Pope Martin IV (1281–1285), who now titles the hegumen of the Grottaferrata Monastery no longer *"delectissimus in Christo filius"*, as Pope Benedict IX addressed St. Bartholomew in his letters [^197]—an address practiced by the popes, as we know, also in relation to other independent monks of the Greek monasteries of Patmos [^198], Si-...

=== LEAF p138 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXXV)*

...-nai [^199], etc., but already as his *vassal* (*vassallus*), *feudatory* (*feudalis*), etc. [^200]. For this submissive servant of the Roman pontiff, very naturally, nothing else remained to do than to reform the internal monastic life and liturgical structure in the monastery governed by him according to the customs of the Western Church.

Quite understandably, the Typikon written by Hegumen Bartholomew, which had been used until then in the practice of this monastery, could no longer fulfill its direct purpose. In accordance with the new regime of life and new liturgical orders established in the monastery, it was necessary for Hegumen Blasius II *not merely to transcribe* (*ἀπεγράφη*) mechanically this Typikon, as Th. Toscani quite rightly observes [^201], but to revise it from beginning to end and remake it anew (*ἀνακαινίσθη*). And in this manner, from under the pen of this hegumen in the year 1300 there appeared a *new* Typikon, in which, upon the ancient foundation brought into the monastery by its first hegumens-ktetors from Southern Italy, from the Calabrian monasteries, alongside services in no way differing from similar services in other Western Greek Typika, new services are created and recorded whose origin derives directly from Rome; there are introduced into practice hitherto unknown Latin litanies with the reading of Latin prayers, Epistles, and Gospels, etc. Thus, for example, on November 1, according to the Typikon of Blasius II, **"according to the most holy Church of Rome we celebrate the commemoration of All Saints and of the holy, glorious Great Martyr Caesarius"** (*«κατὰ τὴν ἁγιωτάτην ἐκκλησίαν τῆς Ῥώμης μνήμην ἐπιτελοῦμεν τῶν Ἁγίων Πάντων καὶ τοῦ ἁγίου ἐνδόξου μεγαλομάρτυρος Καισαρίου»*) (p. 904); on the 2nd day of the same month, **"we celebrate the commemoration of the departed, according to the custom of the most holy Church of Rome, because of our receiving alms"** (*«μνήμην ἐπιτελοῦμεν τῶν κεκοιμημένων, κατὰ τὴν συνήθειαν τῆς ἁγιωτάτης ἐκκλησίας Ῥώμης, διὰ τὸ λαμβάνειν ἡμᾶς ἐλεημοσύνας»*) (evidently, from Rome) (p. 905); on December 17 for the feast of the Dedication of the Grottaferrata Theotokos Church there is performed **"the litany of the Latins"** (*«τὴν τῶν Λατίνων λιτανείαν»*) with the reading of Latin prayers, and at the Liturgy on that same day the Epistle and Gospel are read in La-...

=== LEAF p139 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXXVI)*

...-tin (ibid.); on the feasts of the Nativity of Christ, Pascha, Pentecost, the Dormition, and whenever the hegumen wishes, **"at the Liturgy the office of the Greeks and Latins is joined together"** (*«ἐν τῇ λειτουργίᾳ τὴν ἀκολουθίαν Γραικῶν καὶ Λατίνων συννεμένην»*) (pp. 905–906); feasts of the Lord are counted to 14 (p. 908); at the end of the Synaxarion is recorded the **"Diataxis and constitution of the office, that is, of the order of the precious and spotless Body and Blood of our Lord Jesus Christ"** (*«Διάταξις καὶ διατύπωσις τοῦ ὀφφικίου, ἤγουν τῆς ἀκολουθίας τοῦ τιμίου καὶ ἀχράντου Σώματος καὶ Αἵματος τοῦ Κυρίου ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ»*) (ibid.); regarding the daily celebration throughout the entire Great Fast of the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts, supposedly non-existent according to ancient custom (*παλαιὰν συνήθειαν*), it is observed: **"but we, because the Church of Rome daily chants the complete Liturgy, chant the Presanctified daily, apart from Saturdays and Sundays and the feast of the Holy Forty [Martyrs] and the Annunciation"** (*«ἀλλ’ ἡμεῖς, διότι ἡ ἐκκλησία τῆς Ῥώμης καθ’ ἑκάστην ψάλλει τελείαν λειτουργίαν, προηγιασμένα ψάλλομεν καθ’ ἑκάστην, χωρὶς σαββατοκυριακῶν καὶ τῆς ἑορτῆς τῶν ἁγίων μ΄. καὶ τοῦ Εὐαγγελισμοῦ»*) (p. 908); mention is made of special prayers at each Lenten service (ibid.), of the reading of the Epistle and Gospel in Latin during the litiya on Palm Sunday (p. 909), of a special blessing of waters before the order of the washing of feet (ibid.), etc., etc.

Regarding the food of the monks, we find in this Typikon not a few directions that run completely contrary to the Eastern and the remaining Western Typika. Thus, for example, on the feast of the Elevation of the Cross of the Lord the table appointed is the same as for the feast of the Nativity of the Theotokos, and this means that the monks, besides two dishes of vegetables, ate *fresh cheese, eggs, and fish* (*τυρὸν χλωρὸν καὶ ὠὰ καὶ ἰχθύας*) and drank *good wine* (*ὑποκεраσίαν καλήν*) (p. 904); during the Fast of the Holy Apostle Philip in the morning the monks ate greens with cheese (*δύο λάχανα μετὰ τυρίου*) and well-mashed beans, and in the evening—eggs with pepper, fresh cheese, and fried eggs (*ὠὰ μετὰ πιπερίου καὶ τυρὸν χλωрὸν καὶ ὠὰ τυγανισμένα*) (p. 905); on Great Thursday they were dispensed for fish (p. 910), etc.

In the text of the exposition of the services appear words completely new, alien to the Greek Byzantine lexicon, and imported directly from Latin books and Western liturgical practice: *αἱ καμπάναι* [bells, *campanae*] (pp. 900, 902, et al.), *καπιτόλιον* [capitulum / chapter room] (pp. 902, 903, et al.), *καπίτελον* [capitellum] (p. 909), *πλούβιον* [pluviale / cope], as an outer vestment for the hegumen (p. 900) and an ordinary monk (p. 901), and others.

=== LEAF p140 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXXVII)*

Finally, at the litanies the Pope is commemorated in such a formula: **"Again we pray for our most honorable and most holy father, N., Pope of Rome"** (*«Ἔτι δεόμεθα ὑπὲρ τοῦ τιμιωτάτου καὶ ἁγιωτάτου ἡμῶν πατρὸς ὁ δεῖνα πάπα Ῥώμης»*), etc. (p. 903).

After all this, it is difficult now to agree with the conclusion of Th. Toscani that Blasius II made in the Cryptaferrata Typikon alterations and modifications that were very slight. **"From what has been said hitherto it is clear,"** he concludes after two or three examples, and moreover uncharacteristic ones, of the modifications admitted by him, **"that Blasius introduced certain changes into the Typikon, but that they are very slight and do not pertain to the substance of the office"** (*«Ex hactenus dictis patet... Blasium aliquas immutationes in Typicum induxisse, sed eas esse levissimas nec ad officii substantiam spectare»*) [^202]. On the contrary, we personally arrive at a conclusion completely the reverse, namely, that the Typikon of the Cryptaferrata Monastery that issued from under the pen of Blasius II completely altered its character (*substantiam*) and received a different form. The Blasian Typikon of the year 1300 is *Western* in the narrow, special sense of this word, or, more correctly, *Greek-Uniate*. The submissive "vassal" of the Roman pontiff knows no other model and no other traditions than the Roman Church, and only on the basis of its practice and customs strives to reorganize both the liturgical order and the monastic constitution of life in the monastery governed by him. Of the traditions that guided the predecessors of Blasius II and the Greek monks of other Italian monasteries—traditions whose origin was connected with the most renowned Byzantine typika: of the Great Church, the Studite, the Jerusalem, etc.—he seems not even to suspect, passing them over in complete silence in his Typikon. If he leaves in the Typikon certain services without substantial alterations, as, for example, for the Twelve Great Feasts and the Triodion cycle, this he does out of necessity, since in the Latin Church different services had been composed for these days, completely distinct from the Greek ones, and to introduce them into the practice of the Grottaferrata Monastery would have meant breaking definitively the connection with the orders that had been practiced in the monastery for almost two hundred years. But even in services of this kind Blasius II permitted, nevertheless, all possible alterations: he introduced Latin terminology, prescribed Latin litanies with readings of the Epistle and Gospel in La-...

=== LEAF p141 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXXVIII)*

...-tin and special Latin prayers, and permitted in the monastic refectory departures [from rule] in conformity with the practice of the Roman Church. It remains for us, consequently, to regret—if, of course, all this was not done with some premeditated aim—that Fr. Th. Toscani in his study resolved to examine the Typikon of the Grottaferrata Monastery in parallel and in connection not only with other known Greek Typika of Western provenance, with which it has at least some connection, but even with Typika purely Byzantine—the Jerusalem, the Holy Mountain, and the contemporary Typikon of the Great Church, with which it has almost nothing in common. And, to tell the truth, the comparative table (*Specimen comparationis*) of Typika in Th. Toscani is drawn up so skillfully and so seductively that it was difficult even for such serious scholars of ours as Archbishop Sergius and the late professor of the Moscow Theological Academy I. D. Mansvetov to resist the temptation to place, under its influence, the Grottaferrata Typikon on a par with the other Typika of Western provenance and to make use of it for characterizing liturgical orders that allegedly possess a connection with the monastery of St. Theodore the Studite. In our view, formed now under the influence of an all-round study of the Grottaferrata Typikon, an investigator of the historical fate of the Orthodox Church Typikon may with a calm conscience, without any detriment to the cause, completely ignore this Greek-Uniate Typikon.

Undoubtedly of the same character and of the same scholarly value for the Orthodox liturgist are also those Typika, existing in the West in whole or even in the form of fragments, whose provenance, according to Fr. Antonio Rocchi, is connected with the Typikon of Hegumen Blasius II (*quaedam exemplaria plus minus absoluta ex illo conficeretur*). Of such Typika Fr. Rocchi names: (a) the Typikon of the year 1583, transcribed by the Grottaferrata monk Paul Bevilacqua and, five years later, sent by Cardinal Santoro to the Messina Monastery; (b) the abridged Typikon (*compendium*) of the year 1595, transcribed by a monk of the same monastery, Luke (*Lucas de Felicibus*), for the Montesano (*Montissani*) Monastery and preserved at present in Rome in the Casanatense...

=== LEAF p142 ===

*(Orig. p. CXXXIX)*

...Library (*in bibliotheca Casanatensi*); (c) the Typikon of the year 1605, transcribed by order of the prior of the monastery Cyriacus Marcellinus by the hieromonk of the Messina Monastery Arrighius (*Arrighius*) for use among the Trygonian monks (*in usum monachorum Trygonensium*), representing virtually a copy of the Typikon of 1583 sent to Messina; (d) a Typikon of the 17th century preserved in the Sorrento Seminary; and, finally, (e) the Typikon of the year 1861, transcribed from the Blasian Typikon by the hands of many monks of the Grottaferrata Monastery for the well-known learned Cardinal Jean-Baptiste Pitra, who occupied himself, among other things, with Greek Typika and wished to publish this Typikon in its entirety with his own commentaries [^203].

In the interests of completeness, we consider it not superfluous for specialists to note also that the Cryptaferrata Typikon of Hegumen Blasius II did not remain in obscurity. Aside from Th. Toscani, who devoted to this Typikon more space and attention than others, the Typikon examined by us entered, in no small part of its content, into the following book of Roman edition of 1772 [^204] under the title: **"Horologion, with the holy God, according to the ancient order of the monastery of Cryptaferrata, containing indeed together with the Psalter the Typikon and the entire office outside the choir, according to the final concession of the apostolic church, for the need of the monks in Italy of Basil the Great, who revealed heavenly things and is our father. The Six-Month [Horologion]"** (*«῾Ωρολόγιον σὺν Θεῷ ἁγίῳ, κατὰ τὴν ἔκπαλαι τάξιν τῆς μονῆς τῆς Κρυπτοφέρρης, περιέχον μὲν δὴ σὺν τῷ Ψαλτηρίῳ Τυπικὸν καὶ πᾶσαν τὴν ἔξω τοῦ χοροῦ ἀκολουθίαν, κατὰ τὴν ἐσχάτην συγχώρησιν τῆς ἀποστολικῆς ἐκκλησίας, εἰς χρείαν τῶν τῆς Ἰταλίας μοναζόντων τοῦ οὐρανοφάντορος καὶ πατρὸς ἡμῶν Βασιλείου τοῦ Μεγάλου. ῾Εξάμηνον»*), and to a considerably lesser degree into another book, likewise of Roman edition of 1683, under the title: **"Liturgikon, with the holy God, according to the order of the Typikon of the all-venerable monastery of Cryptaferrata, and indeed also the custom of the Italo-Greek monks of our great father Basil"** (*«Λειτουργικὸν σὺν Θεῷ ἁγίῳ κατὰ τὴν τάξιν τοῦ Τυπικοῦ τῆς πανσέпτου μονῆς τῆς Κρυπτοφέρρης, καὶ μὴν καὶ ἔθος τῶν Ἰтаλογραικῶν μοναζόντων τοῦ μεγάλου πατρὸς ἡμῶν Βασιλείου»*).

After all that has been set forth above, excluding entirely the Greek-Uniate Grottaferrata Typikon, regarding the eight copies of the Typikon of Western Italian provenance we must state that they cannot be assigned either to the category of com-...

=== LEAF p143 ===

*(Orig. p. CXL)*

...-plete and pure copies of the Studite Typikon, nor even to the category of genuine ktetorik typika, as the latter present themselves to us in the numerous copies of Byzantine provenance known to scholarship. Better characterization than that given to them in the superscription of these Typika of both recensions, in our opinion, is difficult to formulate. **"Typikon, that is, the constitution of the ecclesiastical status and office,"** reads the most characteristic superscription, **"according to the tradition of the holy and God-bearing fathers: both of Saint Sabas and of the Studite, and for the most part of the Holy Mountain... and a certain portion of the tradition of our most venerable father Joseph, (ktetor) of the monastery of Saint Nicholas of Kasoulon"** (*«Τυπικόν, ἤτοι διάταξις τῆς ἐκκλησιαστικῆς καταστάσεως καὶ ἀκολουθίας... κατὰ τὴν παράδοσιν τῶν ἁγίων καὶ θεοφόρων πατέρων· τοῦ τε ἁγίου Σάββα καὶ τοῦ Στουδίτου καὶ τὸ πλεῖστον τοῦ ῾Αγίου Ὄρους... καὶ μέρος τι τῆς παραδόσεως τοῦ ὁσιωτάτου πατρὸς ἡμῶν Ἰωσήφ, (κτήτορος) τῆς μονῆς τοῦ ἁγίου Νικολάου τῶν Κασούλων»*), or more briefly: **"Typikon of Theodore the Studite and the rest"** (*«Τυπικὸν Θεοδώρου τοῦ Στουδίτου καὶ λοιπῶν»*). That is, these are Typika not of some single author or father of the Church, but of many or, better to say, of all the most renowned persons in the Orthodox Church who distinguished themselves by their labors on the Church Typikon; these are consolidated, compilatory Typika, or, as Antonio Arcudio expresses it, having made use of the Typika of the first recension for his *Anthologion* [^205] of the year 1598: **"Typikon composed and collected according to the order of the ancient and catholic Typikon"** (*«Τυπικὸν συντεθὲν καὶ συλλεχθὲν κατὰ τὴν τάξιν τοῦ παλαιοῦ καὶ καθολικοῦ Τυπικοῦ»*). Here, and especially in the codices of the second recension, in the extracts from various Typika (concerning some of which, as for example concerning the Typikon of the Great Church, of Mount Olympus, etc., the superscription remains completely silent, expressing itself vaguely: *«καὶ λοιπῶν»*), indeed, we see on the part of their compiler a great familiarity with the practice of the Studite Monastery, in-...

=== LEAF p144 ===

*(Orig. p. CXLI)*

...-sofar as we can judge of it on the basis of the sources accessible to us—unacknowledged extracts from the *Hypotyposis* of Theodore the Studite, the *Diatyposis* of St. Athanasius of Athos [^206], and perhaps even from the Studite Synaxaria themselves directly; but to say that all the peculiarities of liturgical practice contained in the Western Typika are either identical or,

=== LEAF p145 ===

*(Orig. p. CXLII)*

at least, were created and formed upon a Studite foundation, we have no right to assert, since this practice of the Studite Monastery in its full extent remains as yet unknown. Furthermore, with the exception, of course, of the Cryptaferrata Blasian codex, one cannot consider as *ktetorik* in the strict sense of this word not only the Western Typika of the second recension, but even those of the first. True, in these latter we find *a certain small portion* (*μέρος τι*) of customs entered upon the pages of the Typika on the basis of traditions stemming from Venerable Joseph, ktetor of the Monastery of St. Nicholas of Kasoulon; but they, in the first place, constitute in actual fact an insignificant and as it were even accidental part in the Typikon, and in the second place, are always precisely noted in such expressions as: **"our church"** (*«ἡ ἐκκλησία ἡμῶν»*, p. 814), or **"the church of our monastery"** (*«ἡ ἐκκλησία τῆς ἡμετέρας μονῆς»*, p. 813), or **"according to the constitution of our most holy father Joseph"** (*«κατὰ τοῦ ἁγιωτάτου πατρὸς ἡμῶν Ἰωσὴφ διάταξιν»*, p. 830). The ktetor of the Kasoulon monastery or whoever among his successors adopted the Typikon into liturgical practice did not venture, as was done in Byzantine monasteries, to link it with a specific, definite authoritative person, such as the ktetor himself might be, or some prominent benefactor of the monastery, but granted it the right to call itself by such a name as belongs to it by origin—that is, a Typikon compiled on the basis of the traditions of the holy fathers: St. Sabas the Sanctified, St. Theodore the Studite, and other fathers (*«καὶ λοιπῶν»*), among whose number was also St. Joseph of Kasoulon, but *for the most part* (*«καὶ τὸ πλεῖστον»*) on the basis of the practice of the Holy Mountain Athonite rule. The Western Typika even of the first recension are, consequently, neither more nor less than compilatory collections presenting a digest of rules exceedingly diverse in origin concerning liturgical practice and monastic discipline.

Thus, our hopes of seeing complete copies of the Typikon or Synaxarion of the Studite Monastery in the West were not fulfilled. From direct personal examination of all the Typika known to us in the libraries of Italy and Vienna, we came away with the indubitable conviction: first, that such Synaxaria do not exist there either; and second, that in these Typika we possess now new and highly persuasive proofs...

=== LEAF p146 ===

*(Orig. p. CXLIII)*

...of the existence in the ancient liturgical practice of the Byzantine Church of Studite Synaxaria. On the basis of the described Typika of Western provenance, it can be positively asserted that the Studite Synaxarion was not only widely practiced in ancient Byzantium and in the numerous monasteries of the Orthodox East, but even far beyond the borders of Byzantium in the West, among the Greeks living in so-called Magna Graecia, in southern Italy, in whose liturgical monuments traces of this practice have not disappeared completely even to the present day. The results of our searches in this regard, it must be confessed, are not brilliant, especially if one keeps in view that an entire decade and more of years and much persistent, painstaking labor were expended on these searches. Wishing from the heart greater success, and above all good fortune in their searches, to those investigators following after us into this same question of primary importance—since without a definite and clear resolution of what the Studite Synaxarion is and what its distinguishing peculiarities are, further work on the historical fate of our Typikon is impossible—we comfort ourselves with the consciousness that on this arduous path toward resolving the question of the Studite Typikon, encumbered both with incorrect information, and with preconceived opinions, and with superficial judgments of non-specialists who readily trusted authorities, we have not labored in vain and have done something to facilitate their further labors...

Having obtained as the result of our searches almost exclusively negative conclusions, we should not wish to leave in a helpless position those to whom the question of the Studite Typikon and its peculiarities in comparison with the Jerusalem Typikon "ought to be known" (we mean our instructors of liturgics in the seminaries, who are obliged to give an answer to this question by the existing syllabus approved by the Educational Committee under the Most Holy Synod). To them we recommend, in resolving this question, to be guided by that authoritative opinion which the highly knowledgeable investigator of Eastern divine services, the famous writer of the 11th century, Nikon of the Black Mountain, expressed on this account in his consolidated First Discourse of the *Taktikon*. It goes without saying that in doing so it is necessary to ignore those critical remarks which Prof. I. D. Mansvetov made concerning the opinion of Nikon of the Black...

=== LEAF p147 ===

*(Orig. p. CXLIV)*

...Mountain in his dissertation [^207], since these remarks for the most part are erroneous in themselves, and we have no right whatever to distrust Nikon of the Black Mountain until genuine copies of the Studite Synaxarion are discovered. Likewise to be left without attention are the opinions on the Studite Typikon of His Grace Philaret, Archbishop of Chernihiv, and Th. Toscani [^208], as erroneous, and in certain instances representing the fruit of simple misunderstanding on the part of these authors. For persons wishing independently to orient themselves in so interesting and important a question, we propose, although a complex and difficult, yet for now the only possible and reliable path for studying the most important peculiarities of the practice of the Studite Monastery—namely: for a more detailed acquaintance with the peculiarities of the services of the Fast and throughout the entire Pentecostarion according to the Studite rule, to the monuments of undeniably Studite origin—the *Hypotyposis* of Theodore the Studite and the *Diatyposis* of St. Athanasius of Athos—one must join Triodia likewise undeniably of *Studite origin*. Such Triodia we know: *Greek* in the Sinai Library of the 10th century No. 735, of the year 1099 No. 741 [^209] and No. 742, and in the Athos Vatopedi Library of the 11th century No. 316 (927); *in the Slavonic-Russian language* in the Moscow Synodal Typography Library of the 11th–12th centuries No. 255 and No. 254 [^210] and the Moscow Synodal Library of the 12th century No. 319 (423); and, finally, *in Bulgarian* in the Imperial St. Petersburg Public Library from the collection of Verković recently acquired by it, of the 12th–13th centuries No. 102, the so-called Orbel Triodion [^211]. For the study of the Studite Synaxarion or Menologion...

=== LEAF p148 ===

*(Orig. p. CXLV)*

...indubitable service will be rendered to them by the catechetical instructions of St. Theodore the Studite and the so-called Studite Menaia, preserved to our time in no small number, which, as Archbishop Sergius says, "differed little from the Menaia of the Great Church" [^212]. Thanks to our edition of the complete Typikon of the Great Church, acquaintance with the Studite Synaxarion, consequently, is now facilitated to the utmost degree. As regards the peculiarities of the synaxarion services themselves of this Typikon, for those interested in them there lies ahead here a work exceedingly complex and painstaking. For this purpose, from the presently known ktetorik Byzantine typika closely acquainted with the liturgical practice of the Studite Monastery, such as, for example, the 11th-century Typika—the Evergetis and that of Patriarch Alexius of Constantinople—it is necessary to select in the most careful manner everything that is marked in them by the following qualifications: **"Thus the Studite ordered to do"** (*«Οὕτως διετάξατο ὁ Στουδίτης ποιεῖν»*, p. 265), or **"we do not go to the refectory, according to the model of the Studite"** (*«οὐκ ἀπερχόμεθα ἐν τῇ τраπέζῃ, κατὰ τὸν τύπον τοῦ Στουδίτου»*, p. 355), or **"the remaining monasteries, as many as hold the Typikon of the renowned monastery of the Studion, do not chant, etc."** (*«τὰ λοιπὰ μοναστήρια, ὅσα τὸ Τυπικὸν κρατοῦσι τῆς περιωνύμου μονῆς τῶν Στουδίου οὐ ψάλλουσι κ. τ. λ.»*, p. 605), or **"monastic custom according to the Studite manner is for the monks to walk"** (*«монастырски студиискоумоу обычаи єсть ходити мнихомъ»*) [^213], etc., or **"thus, then, the Studite monastery celebrateth"** (*«тако оубо стоудникыи монастырь праꙁдноуѥть»*) [^214], or **"the Studite church alone chanteth not otherwise at any time"** (*«ѥдина же стоудничьскаа цр҃квы инако николиже поющи»*) [^215], etc. Remarks of this kind are scattered in great number in the Typikon of Patriarch Alexius. For the same purpose there may be taken into account also those data, accessible now, which have been extracted by us from the Western Typika of both recensions, especially if, for greater safety in conclusions, there are...

=== LEAF p149 ===

*(Orig. p. CXLVI)*

...made beforehand comparisons of these data with other homogeneous ones in monuments undeniably of Studite origin [^216]. After such arduous labor, as a reward the investigator of the peculiarities of liturgical practice of the Studite Synaxarion will obtain quite sufficient material with which even a demanding specialist can be satisfied until genuine, complete copies of such a Synaxarion or Typikon are made public.

In concluding our preliminary guiding explanations, we consider it for ourselves:

1) A pleasant duty to express deep gratitude to the highly esteemed professors of St. Petersburg University I. V. Pomyalovsky and of Kazan University D. Th. Belyaev, who by their kind word and counsels, precious to us in many respects, rendered us assistance and support in our present work;

2) An urgent necessity to apologize for the absence in such a book as the present one of indices, even if only of personal names. The need for such an index we well recognize, and we intend, if, however, material resources permit, to make good this deficiency *in the second part* of the present volume. To relegate the index thither we are prompted, on the one hand, by the enormous size of the first part, and on the other hand, by considerations of a scholarly and economic nature. The second part will be devoted to materials pertaining to the historical fate of the Typikon of the Jerusalem recension (in all its branches) and of the Holy Mountain Athonite, consequently, to materials homogeneous with those being published now, especially in the Menologion or Synaxarion. To make a separate index later also for the second part of the present volume would be uneconomical in many respects. Desiring to unite both parts into a single whole, we are releasing them for sale together;

3) A sorrowful necessity to make confession of fault especially before those of our readers who possess, as the saying goes, an "unfortunate" eye that cannot endure typographical errors in any book, and in the publication of monuments especially. Much...

=== LEAF p150 ===

*(Orig. p. CXLVII)*

...attention was turned to this aspect of the present edition, and all possible means were applied for this purpose [^217], but to avoid typographical errors, to our deep regret, was not granted to us. The "mournful" errata list of the present book is in evidence. We comfort ourselves only with the consciousness that these typographical errors [^218] are for the most part insignificant and easily corrigible, on the one hand; and on the other hand, in certain and not few cases, these are not "errata", but in the proper sense of the term "addenda et corrigenda", evoked by various circumstances inevitable in the printing of an extensive work over the course of several years. In any case, not flattering ourselves in the least regarding the faultlessness of our edition, we permit ourselves to address our readers with the following words of an ancient colophon: **"Fathers and brethren, wheresoever we have miswritten, or rewritten, or omitted in writing, read correcting for God's sake, and curse not, because the books are decrepit, and youthful mind hath not attained."**

Kyiv. The year 1895,  
September 25.

[^2]: In the inventory of 1201 of the Patmos Library, recently published in *Byzantinische Zeitschrift* of Prof. Krumbacher by the French scholar Diehl (Vol. I, Issue 3–4, p. 521), this manuscript is recorded thus: *"Ἕτερον βιβλιδόπουλον Τυπικὸν τῆς Μεγάλης Ἐκκλησίας"* ["Another small book: Typikon of the Great Church"].

[^3]: I. D. Mansvetov, *The Church Ordo* (*Церков. Уставъ*), pp. 142–243; *Ivanov*, *Description of Manuscript Greek Gospels and Apostolos Formerly Belonging to the Taurida Theological Seminary, and Now Preserved in the Church-Archaeological Museum at the Kyiv Theological Academy* (*Опис. рукописныхъ греческихъ Евангелій и Апостоловъ, принадлежащихъ когда-то Таврической дух. семинаріи, а нынѣ хранящихся въ Церковн.-археол. музеѣ при кіев. дух. Академіи*), *Proceedings of the Imperial St. Petersburg Archaeological Society*, Vol. I (new series), and others.
