[^20]: *Constitut. Apostol.*, lib. V, caput 15 (J. B. Cotelerius, *Patres apostolici*, Lutetiae Parisiorum 1672, p. 255): "He (that is, the Lord) commanded to break the fast on the seventh day when the cock crows, but on the Saturday itself He commanded to fast, not because one ought to fast on the Sabbath when creation was finished, but because one ought to fast on that Saturday alone on which the Creator was yet beneath the earth." ... "On every Saturday, except one (that is, the pre-Paschal one), and on every Lord's day, assemble together and rejoice."

[^21]: The late professor of the Moscow Theological Academy, I. D. Mansvetov, drew attention to this article on the relaxation of the fast on Great Saturday, but gave it a somewhat peculiar interpretation: "The exceedingly indulgent rule of the Studite Typikon (according to Mai's *Hypotyposis*), utterly incomprehensible from the standpoint of present practice, concerning the complete (?) relaxation of the fast on Great Saturday found a place for itself also in the complete Studite Typika (?!), e.g., in Synodal manuscript No. 330/380. But one of the persons who used this copy," remarks Mr. Mansvetov, "found such an instruction impossible to observe and erased the scandalous words, whence resulted the following unintelligible reading: 'And on the Great Sabbath let there be pure breads, that is, great loaves, and let them be anointed with oil, and let each of the monks take a half; and let them eat also... (here some word has been erased, but it can be deciphered: *and fish*) and both dishes: cooked grain and herbs with oil (then again certain words are scraped away, but they can be deciphered and it is visible that it was written: *cheese and eggs*); for indeed the fathers on that day did not forbid them. But we, because the Liturgy and meal take place toward evening, in order to come to the readiness, do not issue a command, but submit as an opinion that one should refrain from such things; yet if it seems fitting to the hegumen to serve and eat them, we do not forbid it' [fol. 211]. Thus, according to the ordo, it was permissible on Great Saturday after Vespers to eat cheese, eggs, and fish, but in view of the late hour it proved inconvenient and was left to the discretion of the superior. An echo of this custom, which by that time was already abandoned, is heard in the following remark of Synodal Typikon No. 382/905 concerning food on Great Saturday: 'Non-lenten food and fish we have not been accustomed to eat on that Saturday, for the sake of our new Slavic enlightenment' [fol. 315]. This remark belongs to the Slavic redactor (one must bear in mind: a 15th-century manuscript) of the Studite Typikon and was inserted by him in place of the inconvenient rule on food that was read in the original. To retain this strange article seemed scandalous to him, and he struck it out in the name of the religious feeling of newly enlightened Slavic society." *Mitropolit Kiprian v ego liturgicheskoi deiatelnosti* [Metropolitan Cyprian in His Liturgical Activity], Moscow 1882, pp. 163–164.

## Cohort 4 Footnotes

[^45]: Variant readings according to the manuscript of the Moscow Synodal Typography Library No. 142 (285): *into the church* [*въ црвныꙗ*].

[^56]: The *Diatyposis* of the Venerable Athanasius of Athos constitutes part of the Typikon of this father under the title: *Typikon, that is, Kanonikon, of our venerable and God-bearing father Athanasius of Mount Athos* (*«Τυπικὸν ἤτοι Κανονικὸν τοῦ ὁσίου καὶ θεοφόρου πατρὸς ἡμῶν Ἀθανασίου, τοῦ ἐν τῷ Ἄθῳ»*). Its first part with the cited title treats of the building of the lavra on Athos and of the initial organization of monastic life there, while the second with the title: *The Diatyposis of our venerable and blessed father Athanasius* (*«Ἡ Διατύπωσις τοῦ ὁσίου καὶ μακαρίου πατρὸς ἡμῶν Ἀθανασίου»*) constitutes properly already the testament of this father, in which he speaks of the appointment and election of the hegumen, of the epitropos of the lavra, of mutual brotherly love among the monks, of the administration of the lavra, of its charitable work, of the orders of divine services, and of the arrangement of monastic life within it. [^59]: The *Diatyposis* of the Venerable Athanasius of Athos was published by Mr. Ph. Meyer according to two manuscripts—the Iveron No. 754 (228) of the 16th century and the Lavra MS of 1814. The latter manuscript he terms an "official copy" (*officielle Abschrift des kanonikon*), evidently because in an inscription to it by the hand of the didaskalos Procopius it is noted that this copy is "An exact copy of the sacred testament of our God-bearing father St. Athanasius and ktetor of the sacred monastery and Greatest Lavra, etc." (*«Ἴσον ἀπαράλλακτον τῆς ἱερᾶς διαθήκης τοῦ θεοφόρου πατρὸς ἡμῶν ἁγίου Ἀθανασίου καὶ κτήτορος τῆς ἱερᾶς μονῆς καὶ μεγίστης λαύρας κ. τ. λ.»*) (*Haupturkunden*, p. 271). To this copy the editor gives preference over the Iveron copy, since, according to his supposition, there served as its original Cod. No. 2 of the very beginning of the 11th century, preserved in the Lavra skeuophylakion and, it may be, written even by the hand of the Venerable Athanasius himself (*ibid.*, p. 273), whereas the text of the Iveron manuscript was borrowed from another complete 11th-century codex, No. 1, of the same skeuophylakion (pp. 272–273). In particular, the second, liturgical part of the *Diatyposis* (*περὶ ἱερῶν ἀκολουθιῶν*), which is of the greatest interest to us, Meyer published in his book under No. 3 with the additional title: *Hypotyposis katastaseos tes lauras tou hosiou Athanasiou* (*«Ὑποτύπωσις καταστάσεως τῆς λαύρας τοῦ ὁσίου Ἀθανασίου»*) (pp. 130–140) according to the Iveron manuscript No. 754, but after verification, however, with the aid of "his friend" (*durch meinen Freund*), the hieromonk of the lavra and first secretary of the Kinot in Karyes, Fr. Chrysostom, according to the original preserved in the Lavra skeuophylakion in codex No. 1 (p. 271). Undoubtedly Fr. Chrysostom, to whom we too personally had recourse with a similar request repeatedly, but every time without result, rendered his German "friend" not only the aforementioned favor, but also acquainted him with two curious codices of the Lavra skeuophylakion, which, by Mr. Meyer's frank admission, "are not shown at all" (p. 272) and which nevertheless turned out to be now described in detail in his book (pp. 272–273).

## Cohort 6 Footnotes

[^76]: A most curious fact is the following. Sava of Serbia built on Athos his Hilandar Monastery in honor of the Mother of God, whom in the Typikon he almost everywhere terms "Directress" [*nastavnitsa*] (pp. 1, 25, 27, 28, etc.), but in Chapter 12 this title is unexpectedly changed, and the Mother of God is named—"Benefactress" [*blagodatel'nitsa*] (According to the remark of the Life of Symeon and Sava, written by Domentijan, — "Evergetis is in Slavic Benefactress" [*Evergetitsa slavenski blagodetel'nitsa*], fol. 11v). The one who would violate the independence of the Hilandar Monastery, according to the remark of the Typikon, "shall be guilty not only before the divine Body and Blood of the Lord God and our Savior Jesus Christ, and of the Mother of God our Lady the Benefactress" (*οὐ μόνον ἔνοχος ἔσται τῷ θείῳ Σώματι καὶ Αἵματι τοῦ Κυρίου καὶ Θεοῦ καὶ Σωτῆρος ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ καὶ τῇ Θεομήτορι καὶ Δεσποίνῃ ἡμῶν τῇ Εὐεργέτιδι*), etc. (*Typikon of Sava*, p. 26, cf. p. 631). Evidently, this appellation entered the Hilandar Typikon through an oversight of the translator or redactor from its prototype—the Evergetis Typikon. But the Typikon of St. Sava of Serbia in the edition of Mr. Daničić (*Glasnik* XX), which we use, has been considerably renovated in language, and therefore stands further from the Greek original than, for example, the "Typikon of St. Sava for the Studenica Monastery", published by J. Jireček (*Glasnik* Book XL, 1874, pp. 132–181). The latter was, of course, a copy of the Hilandar Typikon, and in the majority of chapters a verbatim one (*ibid.*, p. 137); yet meanwhile it turns out that it betrays its origin more clearly and frankly than its original—the Hilandar Typikon. In the Studenica Typikon of the Venerable Sava of Serbia, Graecisms of the kind of the following: *po anafore* [according to the anaphora], *metanie* [metania], *felagedenu* (*φαγέδαιναν* [gangrenous ulcer]), *skupyne* (*σκνιφότης* [stinginess]), *krasovolem* [with the krasobolion], etc. (*Glasnik* XL, p. 147ff.) are encountered constantly; the translation is closer to the Greek original of the Evergetis Typikon (cf. the eleventh chapters of both monuments); and the epithet of the Mother of God—"Benefactress" [*blagodetelnitse* or *blagodet'nitse*] can already be found repeatedly (pp. 153, 154, 155, 157).

Printed in Vienna, in the Slavo-Serbian Printing House, 1794. There is no pagination in the book, and therefore we note folios 11v, 28.

[^86]: Although the Typikon of the Pantokrator Monastery published by us speaks insistently of Emperor John Komnenos as its ktetor, and acknowledges no role in this matter for his pious spouse Empress Irene, we are nonetheless not disinclined to consider the latter as the founder and even initiator in the matter of building the present monastery. Grounds for this we find first of all in the touching narrative of the Synaxarion, which appointed the commemoration of Empress Irene as founder of the Pantokrator Monastery to be celebrated on August 13 (*Μνήμη τῆς ἀοιδίμου καὶ παμμακαρίστου βασιλίσσης καὶ κτητορίσσης τῆς σεβασμίας μονῆς τοῦ Παντοκράτορος Σωτῆρος Χριστοῦ Εἰρήνης, τῆς διὰ τοῦ ἁγίου καὶ ἀγγελικοῦ σχήματος μετονομασθείσης Ξένης μοναχῆς* [Commemoration of the renowned and all-blessed empress and ktetorissa of the venerable monastery of Christ the Savior Pantokrator, Irene, who through the holy and angelic schema was renamed the nun Xene]), and then also in the Pantokrator Typikon of 1136 itself. Describing the rare soul qualities of this fortunate mother-empress, who gave her spouse eight children—four boys and four girls—the Synaxarion notes in the empress a deep, sincere piety and boundless love for charitable works, the fruit of which was her ardent desire to establish a magnificent monastery, which by its churches and charitable institutions attached to it—xenons [*strannopriimnitsami*] and gerokomeions [*bogadel'niami*]—was to surpass all other Byzantine monasteries (*τὴν βασιλικὴν μονήν, τὴν ἐπονομαζομένην τοῦ Παντοκράτορος, αὐτὴ ἔκτισεν ἡ ἀοίδιμος ἐκ θεмеλίων· ὁμοίως καὶ τοὺς νῦν ὁρωμένους περικαλλεῖς ναούς, καὶ τὰ ξενοδοχεῖα καὶ γηροκομεῖα, τὰ ὁποῖα ὑπερέβησαν τούς τε ἀρχαίους ναοὺς καὶ τὰ ἀρχαῖα ξενοδοχεῖα καὶ γηροκομεῖα, καὶ τὰ νέα, κατὰ τὴν τοпоθεσίαν καὶ ὡραιότητα* [the imperial monastery called of the Pantokrator, the renowned lady built from the foundations; likewise both the exceedingly beautiful temples now seen, and the xenodocheia and gerokomeia, which surpassed both the ancient temples and the ancient xenodocheia and gerokomeia, and the new, in location and beauty]). The chief architect under whose supervision these structures were executed was the architect Nikephoros, personally known to the empress, called in the Synaxarion a "new Bezalel" [*novym Veseleilom*]. After the rough completion of the monastery's buildings, when difficult financial circumstances ensued, the empress resolved to draw into the work of completing the planned structures of this monastery the emperor himself. Once, according to the narrative of the Synaxarion, Empress Irene, taking the emperor by the arm, led him inside the church of the Pantokrator, and falling before him to the ground, bowed her head to the very floor and with tears uttered: "Accept, Sovereign, this church erected by God, under thy protection" (*διὰ τὴν χάριν σου* [for thy favor]). The empress would not rise from the floor until she heard from the lips of the emperor consent to her request. Touched by the tears and fervent entreaty of his spouse, the emperor gave a promise, which he also fulfilled brilliantly, placing in all respects the present monastery among the foremost (*ἐπρώτευε μεταξὺ ὅλων τῶν μοναστηρίων τῆς Κωνσταντινουπόλεως*) in Constantinople (*Synaxaristes ton dodeka menon tou eniautou* by Nicodemus the Hagiorite, Zakynthos 1868, Vol. III, pp. 240–242). The Synaxarion adds that the empress, having died in Bithynia, was buried in the monastery founded by her (*ibid.*, p. 242), or, as our Typikon indicates more precisely, in the burial church in honor of the Archangel Michael (pp. 658, 664, 677, 680). — The tomb of Empress Irene in the Pantokrator Monastery, the repeated and insistent admonitions of Emperor John Komnenos to the monks of this monastery to celebrate the memory of his deceased spouse (p. 681, etc.), and the various possessions—*διάφορα κτήματα*—left to the monastery (p. 698), as our Typikon states, by the late empress—all these are facts eloquently testifying, in our opinion, that she was connected with the monastery not by name alone as the wife of the ktetor, but also by more intimate and vivid personal relations, in the capacity of originator of the being of the given monastery and of that flourishing condition into which it was brought, according to her will, by Emperor John Komnenos.

[^87]: Nicodemus the Hagiorite, *Synaxaristes ton dodeka menon tou eniautou*, Vol. III, p. 241.

[^88]: Archbishop Antony of Novgorod speaks of the "front" and "rear gates" of the present monastery (P. Savvaitov, *Puteshestvie v Tsar'grad XII stol.* [Journey to Tsargrad in the 12th Century], pp. 146–147).

[^89]: A more circumstantial description of the Pantokrator Monastery and a clearer indication of its two gates we find in our 14th-century pilgrim Stephen of Novgorod. "We went," he writes, "to the great monastery of the Savior, that is to say the Almighty. Enter into the first gates, and above the gates there is the Savior fashioned in mosaic, for He is great in form and lofty; likewise also enter into the second gates, also into the monastery, to see a marvelous and beautiful building, and the church is adorned with mosaic from without, so that it shines" (N. Sakharov, *Skazaniia russkogo naroda* [Tales of the Russian People], St. Petersburg 1849, Vol. II, Book VIII, p. 54). Prof. N. P. Kondakov erroneously interprets the "second gates" in the sense of doors "from the 2nd narthex inside the church" (*Vizantiiskie tserkvi i pamiatniki Konstantinopolia*, p. 213), since Stephen of Novgorod categorically speaks of "the second gates also into the monastery", and not into the church.

[^90]: The decoration of ancient Byzantine churches with numerous kandilas was in the spirit of their time: "Of kandilas there is hung a great multitude beyond number in St. Sophia," writes the pilgrim Stephen of Novgorod, "some in side-chapels and chambers, and others on walls and between walls and in church passages, where great icons stand, and there kandilas with olive oil burn" (Sakharov, *Skazaniia russkogo naroda*, Vol. II, Book 8, p. 52).

[^91]: The dedication [enkainia] of the church in honor of the Mother of God the Merciful [*Eleousa*] is appointed to be celebrated on the 11th day of the month of August. In the Synaxarion the feast is recorded thus: *«Τῇ αὐτῇ ἡμέρᾳ τελοῦνται τὰ ἐγκαίνια τοῦ σεβασμίου καὶ περικαλλοῦς οἴκου καὶ θείου ναοῦ τῆς ὑπεραγίας Δεσποίνης ἡμῶν Θεοτόκου τῆς Ἐлеούσης»* [On the same day is celebrated the dedication of the venerable and most beautiful house and divine church of our most holy Lady the Theotokos the Merciful] (*Synaxaristes*, Vol. III, p. 235). Why the Church of the Theotokos Eleousa in Prof. N. P. Kondakov is turned into a "monastery", and why he attributes its construction to the 11th century (if not earlier) (*Vizantiiskie tserkvi i pamiatniki Konstantinopolia*, p. 72)—all this is incomprehensible to us.


## Cohort 7 Footnotes

[^92]: On the municipal porticos of Constantinople and on the remains of the porticos of the Church of the Mother of God Eleousa, see Prof. Kondakov, *Vizantiiskie tserkvi i pamiatniki Konstantinopolia* [Byzantine Churches and Monuments of Constantinople], pp. 135–137, 211.

[^93]: If the remains of the buildings of the Pantokrator Monastery in the mosque of "Zeyrek Camii" or "Kilisse Camii" are correctly identified by archaeologists, then they relate closest of all to the Church of the Mother of God the Merciful [*Eleousa*], built by Emperor John Komnenos at the Pantokrator Monastery and adorned with rich mosaics. "The magnificent facade of the church," writes Prof. N. P. Kondakov describing the present mosque, "presents a high wall with 7 blind arches. The door in the central arch, which is somewhat larger in size, leads into the first or outer narthex, now extremely dark; five doors leading from this narthex into the second still preserve their doorframes of variegated marble with delicate and complex molding in the form of colonnettes. The second narthex, even darker, is illuminated through the doors and special lunettes made above the doors. It is evident that the illumination of the first narthex was once much stronger, consequently that the arches of the facade were originally open, and walled up only subsequently, perhaps already under the Turks. Meanwhile, both narthexes were richly adorned, and besides the marble floor, there can still be seen in them portions of marble revetment protruding from under the stucco... The colossal half-length mosaic image of the Savior (in Stephen of Novgorod—tall) in the lunette above the door was destroyed under the Turks, when two semicircular windows were punched through here... Three doors of the inner narthex lead into the present mosque. It occupies one right or southern section—likewise a church—of the former temple. The entire temple consists of three similar sections or churches... An extremely original plan of the temple resulted, covered with four domes, namely: the right and left wings—properly churches—covered each with one dome, and a central smaller church covered with two... The small dimensions of the central church and its very shape indicate that this church played the role of the so-called choir in Western churches. In the right church the dome (without a drum) rests by means of four pendentives upon columns, which in their present plastered form represent Romanesque pillars broken into colonnettes... their capitals have the form of trapezoids, on which it is easy to distinguish chalices with branches in the form of Ionic volutes and surrounded by palmettes... Three arches on the western side open a view onto the gynecaeum or galleries situated above the inner narthex; the arches rest upon slender marble colonnettes. Here too, as in the gynecaeum of SS. Sergius and Bacchus, mosaics are hidden beneath the plaster. But of all the decorations there remain only the mosaic floor and the revetment of the apse with variegated marbles according to the design of Hagia Sophia. Three arches resting upon pillars open widely into the middle church. Having a square plan, it is covered with two domes: one oval dome located along the length of the church, and another oval one covering the apse and located transversely. In the sanctuary two open arches leading into the sanctuary of the prothesis and the vestry [diakonikon], covered with half-domes, are curious. The third church or left wing, in its proportions (it is more spacious than the first) and in the arrangement of the dome upon four broad pillars, is curious in its proximity to ancient Russian structures, which evidently also adopted the type of monastic churches of Byzantium. This church has only one entrance (at the present time?) from the northern side, and the dome, as in the first, illuminates and covers its front part; the sanctuary is formed by two large arches and a semi-domed vault of the apse. Beneath the exposed plaster here frescoes are visible (the church suffered recently from water standing in it) and entablatures of red marble with carved palmettes. The eastern walls of the church are quite characteristic in their masonry and architectural decoration: they recall medieval Roman basilicas just as much as Georgian structures: slender colonnettes here too frame arcades along with variegated molding of cornices, string-courses, etc." (*Vizantiiskie tserkvi i pamiatniki Konstantinopolia*, pp. 212–215). Comparing this description of the mosque of "Zeyrek Camii", made by an eyewitness and learned archaeologist, with the brief description of the Church of the Mother of God Eleousa according to our monument, we see a clear resemblance in the facade of both churches, in the arrangement of the narthexes, in the number of entrance doors (we think that the seventh door on the southern side is now walled up), in the division into three naves, and in their decoration with mosaics, concerning which our Typikon, in describing the Church of the Pantokrator, for some reason remains completely silent. Other architectural and decorative details of the present mosque, described with such circumstantiality by N. P. Kondakov, we do not touch upon, since in our Typikon we possess no data for their comparison. We note only that the existing gynecaeum in the mosque of "Zeyrek Camii", arranged on its western side above the inner narthex, likewise speaks in favor of our supposition. In the Church of the Mother of God the Merciful, where every week people gathered in multitude without distinction of sex for agrypnies [vigils], the need for a gynecaeum was natural; whereas in the Church of the Pantokrator, according to the rule of the monastery, women, even noble ones, were not admitted by any means, and in a gynecaeum, consequently, there was no necessity whatever. The monks stood in the church in stasidia.

[^94]: "Emperor Manuel," the pilgrim writes to us, "inquired throughout the entire Greek domain and commanded to summon all priests and to give them a hyperpyron (*ὑπέρπυρον*)... And at Holy Sophia there are 3,000 priests: five hundred receive a stipend [*ruga*], and two and a half thousand do not; and whenever a priest of the five hundred dies, then from the two and a half thousand a priest enters into his place" (P. Savvaitov, *Puteshestvie arkhiep. Antoniia* [Journey of Archbishop Antony], pp. 173–174). In this report there is perhaps an exaggeration, but the similarity in the organization of the clergy of Sophia and of the Church of the Mother of God the Merciful, according to our monument, is complete.

[^95]: By completely the same name (*τὰ ὀρφανά* [the orphans]) are called the attendants or kandilaptai in the Typikon of Hagia Sophia of Constantinople (pp. 32, 34, etc.) and in other monuments of patriarchal hierarchical services (p. 157). One must suppose that the kandilaptai of Hagia Sophia and of other churches of Byzantium retained for themselves the appellation *«τὰ ὀρφανά»* or *«οἱ ὀρφανοί»* because they were taken from the public orphanage (*ὀρφανοτροφεῖον*), which undoubtedly stood under the patronage of the Great Church (pp. 18, 84).

[^96]: See in more detail concerning this in our book: *Bogosluzhenie strastnoi i paskhal'noi sedmits vo sv. Ierusalime v IX–X v.* [The Divine Services of Passion and Paschal Weeks in Holy Jerusalem in the 9th–10th Centuries], Kazan 1894, note 105, pp. 403–406.

[^97]: "And from that same altar (that is, over the tomb of Anna) not far away myrrh-bearers sing," writes Archbishop Antony, "and there stands before them a great icon of the most pure Mother of God holding Christ; and tears ran from her eyes onto the eyes of Christ our God. And they give the water that is in the altar for anointing unto all people" (P. Savvaitov, *Puteshestvie*, pp. 62–63).

[^98]: Empress Irene in the year 1114 decreed in her typikon that two hieromonk-eunuchs performing divine services in her monastery should receive maintenance (*τὸ αἰδέσιμον ἀπὸ τῆς πολιτείας* [venerable support from the state]) from the imperial treasury. Miklosich et Müller, *Acta et diplomata graeca medii aevi sacra et profana*, Vol. II: *Acta et diplomata monasteriorum et ecclesiarum orientis*, Vol. II, p. 346.

[^99]: For the determination of the value of the given coin and its manifold types, see P. Savvaitov, *Puteshestvie arkhiep. Antoniia v Tsar'grad*, p. 173, note 295. In our money [circa 1895] each hyperpyron was worth about 4 rubles.

[^100]: A. Quirinus-Venetus, *Diatribae ad prior. part. veteris officii quadragesimalis Graeciae orthodoxae*, Rome 1721, pp. 173–176, and Prof. I. D. Mansvetov, *Tserkovnyi Ustav*, p. 130, know of the "Order of the Hagiopolite," but do not elucidate the true meaning and significance of this order in the divine services of the Byzantine Church.

[^101]: To the Church of the Mother of God the Merciful from the monastic bakery (*ἀπὸ τοῦ τῆς μονῆς ἀρτοκοπείου*) for the celebration of the Liturgy there was issued daily three prosphoras: an imperial/master's one of two litras weight of semidalis [fine flour], and two commemorative ones of pure wheat flour (*ἀπὸ ἀлеύρου καθαρωτάτου*) of the same weight. From the monastery also came the wine (*νᾶма*) for the Liturgy (p. 680). In the Church of the Archistrategos Michael the Liturgy was celebrated thrice a week: on Tuesdays, Thursdays, and Saturdays, and each time upon three prosphoras of two litras of the purest wheat (p. 681).


## Cohort 8 Footnotes

[^102]: This carrying of icons and gonfalons in the processions of the cross of ancient Byzantium was by no means an easy matter. A curious picture of such a procession with the icon of the Mother of God, painted by the hand of the Evangelist Luke, taken from life, although not without embellishments and exaggerations characteristic of a "seasoned" traveler, we find in our pilgrim Stephen of Novgorod. "We went on Tuesday to the Holy Mother of God to venerate the *processional icon*," writes our pilgrim, "for that icon the Evangelist Luke painted, having depicted the Lady Virgin Mother of God herself while she was still alive; *for that icon they carry out every Tuesday*. Astounding indeed to behold how the people and folk assemble from other cities! And that icon is very great, richly encased, and chanters sing before it beautifully, and all the people cry: *Kyrie eleison*! with weeping. *Upon one man's shoulders they set a frame, and he spreads out his arms as if crucified, and likewise they roll back his eyes(?); it is terrible to behold, across the pathway it tosses him hither and thither, very violently it wheels him around, and he remembers not whither the icon carries him*. Then another approaches, and he likewise, then a third and a fourth catch hold, and they all do likewise. And they sing great chanting with the clerks, and the people call out: 'Lord, have mercy!' with weeping. Two deacons hold rhipidia, and others a shrine before the icon. A marvelous sight! Three men mount it upon the shoulders of a single man, and he walks as if unburdened by the will of God" (Sakharov, *Skazaniia russkogo naroda* [Tales of the Russian People], Vol. II, Book VIII, pp. 52–53). Evidently, the ordinary physical exhaustion of the icon-bearers was taken by our pious pilgrim for a manifestation of a special miracle from the icon. Conscious of the heavy labor of the icon-bearers and valuing it, Emperor John Komnenos did not forget them in his Typikon. For each above-mentioned procession of the cross, it was appointed to distribute from the monastery 50 hyperpyra, of which *two hyperpyra each* were given to each of the bearers (*βασταγάριοι* [*bastagarioi*]) and ministers (*λοιποὶ δουλευταὶ τῆς ἁγίας εἰκόνος* [remaining servitors of the holy icon]) of the holy icon (pp. 681–682).

[^103]: Describing the sacred relics preserved at the end of the 12th century "in the imperial golden chambers," Archbishop Antony says, among other things, the following: "And many other holy relics *in the golden chambers* did we kiss, *and the icon of the most pure Mother of God Hodegetria, which the holy Apostle Luke painted, which goes into the city*" (P. Savvaitov, *Puteshestvie arkhiep. Antoniia* [Journey of Archbishop Antony], pp. 33, 94–95). The last emphasized words give us reason to think that the icon of the Hodegetria was constantly located in the palace, and from there was solemnly carried out each time litanies with this icon were celebrated throughout the city to this or that monastery, to this or that church. Archbishop Antony knows of the monastery in honor of the Mother of God Hodegetria, but indicates there only the relics of St. Spyridon, which in that "monastery lie beneath the altar" (*ibid.*, p. 112), and mentions not a word about the presence there of the icon that occupies us. The location of this icon in the palace fully corresponds to the historical information that we possess concerning this icon, both in the period described by us and in subsequent times. Emperor John Komnenos directly calls the icon of the Hodegetria his own possession (*τὴν θείαν εἰκόνα τῆς ὑπεράγνου Δεσποίνης καὶ Θεοτόκου μου Ὁδηγητρίας* [the divine icon of my all-pure Lady and Theotokos, the Hodegetria]) (p. 681), and, according to the testimony of Niketas Choniates, harbored toward it a special reverence and respect throughout his entire life. With this icon he went on campaign against the Scythians, and when "the Roman phalanxes were failing, he, standing before the icon of the Mother of God (*τὴν τῆς Θεομήτορος εἰκόνα*) and gazing upon it with an imploring look, shed tears hotter than the sweat of the warriors" (Nicetas Choniates, *Ioannes Comnenus*, Geneva 1593, p. 17), and received help from it. After the victory over the Perso-Armenians, the emperor ordered a magnificent chariot to be constructed for the triumph, "to adorn it in places even with stones, though not the most expensive," and to harness four beautiful horses, with hair whiter than snow. The tsar [emperor] himself did not mount the chariot, but placed upon it the icon of the Mother of God, since she was the cause of his joy and ecstasy, from which he was beside himself, and to her, as the invincible commander (*ὡς συστρατηγέδι ἀμάχῳ* [as invincible fellow-general]), who together with him commanded the army, he attributed the victories. Appointing the foremost grandees in his presence to lead the horses by the bridle and entrusting to close kin the care of the chariot, he himself walked on foot ahead, carrying the cross (*ibid.*, p. 21). But in our later pilgrims of the 14th–15th centuries, the icon of the Mother of God Hodegetria is indicated already in the monastery of her name (N. Sakharov, *Skazaniia russkogo naroda*, Vol. II, Book VIII, pp. 52, 61, 72, 101). We deem it not superfluous to note here that in these pilgrims the monastery of the Hodegetria is either indicated in proximity to the Pantokrator Monastery, or they record some obscure tradition about the presence in the Pantokrator Monastery of even the icon itself. The monk Ignatius of Smolensk, for example, writes the following concerning the icon of the Hodegetria: "On the 6th [day] we went to the most holy Mother of God Hodegetria, and having bowed down we kissed it, and took *pomirizmo* [perfumed oil] and anointed ourselves with rose-water (*ῥοδοστάγματα* [*rhostagmata* / rose-scented drops]). And we went *to the great honorable Monastery of the Pantokrator*" (*Pravoslavnyi Palestinskii Sbornik*, Vol. IV, Issue III, p. 8). "In the Hodegetria," writes a contemporary of Ignatius of Smolensk, the deacon Alexander, "there is an icon of the holy Mother of God; they carry it out every Tuesday, and it works miracles for whoever comes with faith and receives health. And Luke the Evangelist painted this icon. Once there were iconoclasts(?), and they preserved *this icon in the Pantokrator Monastery in a wall, and having lit a lamp before it, for 60 years it was not extinguished before it*" (N. Sakharov, *Skazaniia russkogo naroda*, Vol. II, Book VIII, p. 72). Evidently, in popular tradition even in later times there were preserved echoes of that spiritual bond which existed between the Monastery of the Pantokrator, the icon of the Mother of God Hodegetria, and the monastery of her name, and which Emperor John Komnenos established in the Typikon analyzed by us.

[^104]: On the feast of the Transfiguration Byzantine emperors made solemn processions to Vespers and Liturgy in the Pantokrator Monastery. "On the feast of the divine Transfiguration," says the Kuropalates, "the emperor likewise departs into the venerable Monastery of Christ the Savior, the Pantokrator" (*«Κατὰ τὴν τῆς θείας Μεταμορφώσεως ἑορτήν, ἀπέρχεται (ὁ βασιλεὺς) ὡσαύτως εἰς τὴν σεβασμίαν μονὴν τοῦ Σωτῆρος Χριστοῦ τοῦ Παντοκράτορος»*) (B. Niebuhr, *Corpus Scriptorum Historiae Byzantinae: Pseudo-Codinus Curopalates, De officiis*, Bonn 1839, p. 81). According to the Synaxarion, on the 4th day of the month of August in this same monastery they celebrated the consecration of the exceedingly beautiful and divine church in Constantinople of the imperial monastery of our Lord and God and Savior Jesus Christ, the Pantokrator (*τὰ ἐγκαίνια τοῦ ἐν Κωνσταντινουπόλει περικαλλοῦς καὶ θείου ναοῦ τῆς βασιλικῆς μονῆς τοῦ Κυρίου καὶ Θεοῦ καὶ Σωτῆρος ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ τοῦ Παντοκράτορος*). (Nikodemos the Hagiorite, *Synaxaristes*, Vol. III, p. 222, ed. by S. Raphtanes, Zakynthos 1868).

[^105]: The use of aloeswood (*aloë syriana* or *asphalta*), as a resinous substance with a very pleasant fragrance, during divine services of the Byzantine Church was a fairly widespread custom. Archbishop Antony of Novgorod gives us a curious picture of its burning in the Church of Hagia Sophia. "The church is paved with red marble," he describes this temple, "and beneath it is a vault [crypt], and men go below, and vents are made through the marble. And whenever the emperor enters into that church, then they carry underneath much aloeswood and incense and place it on coals, and the fragrance passes through those vents into the church into the air, and the entire church is filled with sweet savor" (P. Savvaitov, *Puteshestvie Antoniia v Tsar'grad* [Journey of Antony to Constantinople], pp. 93–94).

[^106]: In the Monastery of Michael Attaleiates, in this case, an innocent child (*παιδίον ἁγνὸν καὶ ἄκακον* [a pure and guileless child]) was sent into the sanctuary. Miklosich et Müller, *Acta et diplomata graeca medii aevi sacra et profana*, Vol. II: *Acta et diplomata monasteriorum et ecclesiarum orientis*, Vol. II, p. 310.

[^108]: Healthy monks, according to the Typikon of John Komnenos, washed in the bath twice a month, and during the Nativity and St. Philip fasts once a month (p. 670).

## Cohort 11 Footnotes

[^125]: M. Gedeon, *Μιχαὴλ τοῦ Παλαιολόγου Τυπικὸν τῆς ἐπὶ τοῦ βουνοῦ τοῦ Αὐξεντίου σεβασμίας μονῆς Μιχαὴλ τοῦ ἀρχαγγέλου* [Typikon of Michael Palaiologos of the Venerable Monastery of Michael the Archangel on the Mountain of Auxentios], Constantinople 1895, pp. 14–15.

[^126]: Concerning his ancestors and the nobility of his lineage, Emperor Michael Palaiologos speaks at length in the prologue to the typikon of the Constantinopolitan Monastery of St. Demetrios, written by this emperor for the monastery founded by him (I. E. Troitsky, *Avtobiografiya Mikhaila Paleologa i otryvok iz ustava, dannago im monastyryu sv. Dimitriya* [Autobiography of Michael Palaiologos and an Extract from the Typikon Given by Him to the Monastery of St. Demetrios], St. Petersburg 1885, pp. 22–23): "My father," wrote this emperor, "traces the lineage of his ancestors to empresses and imperial sons-in-law, and [my mother] to the emperors themselves, so that God from afar prepared for me both renown of lineage (family) and the foundation of my present power. Concerning the lineage of the Palaiologoi, my paternal lineage (leaving aside the maternal, clearly imperial one), if anyone took it into his mind to trace its nobility that began anciently, a nobility that with the course of time grew more and more, since [each] father transmitted it to his children considerably augmented compared to what he himself had received [from his father]; as well as how the representatives of this lineage, together with earthly prosperity, applied care also to how to live for God"... In chronicle books "is recorded not only what ranks and honors they received, how they shared with the sovereigns the labors of governance, how they were showered with great riches, what warriors and commanders and heroes in war they were, but there are also included instructive references to the erection of divine houses (churches), sacred monasteries and institutions, to the donations of estates, to care for the poor, to the provision for the infirm, to the support of the needy of every kind, as well as to what fruit they brought by their pious deeds unto God, on the one hand, proclaiming by them the Giver, and on the other, acquiring through fleeting and corruptible goods heavenly blessings. Thus, of this nobility that advanced greatly forward, as has been said, thanks to the augmentations that each made to what was received by inheritance, as well as of piety, my grandfather, the Grand Duke, and my father, the Grand Domestic, became the heirs."

[^127]: Into almost the same subordination in relation to the Patriarch of Constantinople, but without monetary and other material contributions to his benefit, did Emperor Michael Palaiologos place also the Monastery of St. Demetrios (Troitsky, *Avtobiografiya* [Autobiography], p. 36).

[^128]: The Palestinian typikon was used in liturgical practice also by the monks of the Monastery of St. Demetrios or of the Kellibara (*Byzant. Zeitschr.*, B. IV, H. I, s. 45).

[^129]: The time of the compilation or writing of the given Typikon by the founder can be dated to the last year of the life of Emperor Michael Palaiologos. In his address to his successors he says: "Since therefore, contending thus on behalf of the Romans, I also arrived, as one clothed with human weakness, at burdening my soul with the load of many sins," etc. (*«᾿Επεὶ δὲ οὖν οὕτως ὑπὲρ τῶν ῾Ρωμαίων ἀγωνιζόμενος, ἔφθασα καὶ αὐτός, ὡς τὴν ἀνθρωπίνην ἀσθένειαν περικείμενος, πολλῶν ἁμαρτιῶν ἐπιφορτίσαι τῇ ἐμῇ ψυχῇ φορτίον κ. τ. λ.»*, p. 792). In these words the last will of a dying man is felt... The historical facts that the emperor recounts in his Typikon do not contradict the indicated time (cf. I. E. Troitsky, *Avtobiografiya Mikh. Paleologa* [Autobiography of Mich. Palaiologos], p. 45).

[^130]: It must, however, be confessed that now, after the appearance of the brochure of M. Gedeon under the title: *«Μιχαὴλ τοῦ Παλαιολόγου Τυπικὸν τῆς ἐπὶ τοῦ βουνοῦ τοῦ Αὐξεντίου σεβασμίας μονῆς Μιχαὴλ τοῦ ἀρχαγγέλου, ἐκδίδοντος τὸ πρῶτον»* [Typikon of Michael Palaiologos of the Venerable Monastery of Michael the Archangel on the Mountain of Auxentios, edited for the first time], Constantinople 1895, which preceded the appearance of our book by only a few months, the necessity indicated by us above, it goes without saying, has already passed; but to exclude from the present *Description* the Typikon of Michael the Archangel, which has lain in our galley proofs for more than two years, is now no longer in our power.

[^131]: The Monastery of St. Demetrios was founded by the ancestor of Emperor Michael VIII, George Palaiologos, "a man illustrious and burning with zeal according to his piety and divine love, illustrious by his intellect, and courage, and military experience." During the "Latin tyranny" it was "turned into dust and ashes, so that hardly could one notice only faint traces of what had been there"; but under Emperor Michael VIII "all this fallen and excavated [ruin] was restored by a generous hand and expenditure, and brought into its original magnificence" (I. E. Troitsky, *Avtobiograf. im. Mikhaila Paleologa* [Autobiography of Emperor Michael Palaiologos], pp. 31–32).

[^132]: The grounds for this date are indicated by I. E. Troitsky in *Avtobiografiya imp. Mikhaila Paleologa* [Autobiography of Emperor Michael Palaiologos], p. 42. Ph. Meyer, citing I. E. I, s. 52), contrary to the precise indication of the esteemed professor that Andronikos "ascended the throne upon the death of his father in December 1282, being 24 years of age" (*Avtobiogr.*, p. 45).

[^133]: The origin of the designation "of the Kellibara," applied to the Monastery of St. Demetrios, is explained in the Typikon of this monastery by Michael Palaiologos: "I consider it necessary," says this emperor, "in order that it may be known and firmly established to all, to explain that with the monastery of Demetrios, renowned among the martyrs, is united by me and made one also the greatest and venerable Monastery of the Kellibara situated in Asia, which from ancient times was built and honored in the name of the most pure Mother of God (the so-called) Not-Made-by-Hands... In consequence of this, the Monastery of the Kellibara and the Monastery of the Great-Martyr Demetrios situated in Constantinople from henceforth and unto all future times shall not be two different monasteries, but shall constitute one and the same... Both monasteries must not be separated or divided. By virtue of this my imperial majesty declares and indicates beforehand that my present rule must be considered a rule for both monasteries, as if in reality they constituted one monastery, and under its operation fall equally both the Saint Demetrians and the Kellibarians, so that my imperial majesty desires and decrees that henceforth neither the first without the second, nor the second without the first shall be conceived, and that there shall be no distinction between them even in appellations" (I. [^151]: "This very same diataxis (that is, the order of commemoration)," says N. the Hagiorite, "is transmitted without variation also in the parchment Typikon of the sacred, imperial, and magnificent Monastery of Vatopedi, found in the library of the vestry (*skeuophylakion*), which is inscribed that it is of the Studites" (*«Αὐτὴ ἡ ἰδία διάταξις... ἀπαραλλάκτως φέρεται καὶ ἐν τῷ μεμβραΐνῳ Τυπικῷ τῆς ἱερᾶς βασιλικῆς καὶ μεγαλοπρεποῦς μονῆς τοῦ Βατοπεδίου, τῷ εὑρισκομένῳ ἐν τῇ βιβλιοθήκῃ τῇ τοῦ σκευοφυλακίου, ὅπεр ἐπιγράφεται, ὅτι εἶναι τῶν Στουδιτῶν»*): Venice 1819, p. 24. It is very much to be regretted that Nikodemos the Hagiorite did not cite the *diataxis* itself in full; then we might have been able to say with what we are dealing in the given case.

[^162]: *Codices mss. bibliothecae regii Taurinensis Athenaei*, Turin 1749, part I, pp. 308–309.

[^163]: *Mélanges d'archéologie et d'histoire*, 1886, fasc. III–IV, pp. 176–178, 183–186. The description of the Typikon of the Turin Library No. 216 made by Prof. Diehl can be called circumstantial only in a relative sense. Strictly speaking, Prof. Diehl does not describe the copy of the Turin Typikon itself, since, in his opinion, it represents a reproduction (*«qu'un reproduction des livres liturgiques grecs déjà connus»*) of Greek liturgical books already known, and would of itself deserve no attention (*«il ne mériterait aucune attention»*), if the monastics of St. Nicholas of Casole had not made in it certain marginal notes relating specifically to the history of the monastery (*Mélanges*, p. 177). In view of this, Prof. Diehl turns his attention entirely to that aspect of the Typikon which principally elucidates the historical fate of the Monastery of Nicholas of Casole, situated on the southern coast of Italy, on the Plain of Hydruntum (Otranto), near the small town of Otranto. With these aims, of course, he occupied himself in his description with a very circumstantial analysis of the *Hypotyposis* (*Ὑποτύπωσις*) given to the said monastery by its third hegumen (1153–1190), the monk Nicholas, in the year 1161 (*Mélanges*, pp. 183–186). Worthy of note, however, is the following circumstance in the aforementioned analysis of Prof. Diehl. Turin manuscript No. 216 has the *Hypotyposis* of Nicholas of Casole unfinished. Owing to the absence of several folios in the manuscript, it breaks off at chapter 23, so that we, in order to supply the missing portion (pp. 822–823), had to apply through the esteemed professor of Novorossiysk University A. A. Pavlovsky to the Barberini manuscript III No. 69. Whereas Prof. Diehl not only fails to note the said defect in the manuscript, but in the exposition of its contents goes even further and indicates such articles as apparently ought not to be in the *Hypotyposis* or *Kanon on Food and Drink* (*«Κανὼν περὶ βρώσεως καὶ πόσεως»*). "One more thing was prescribed (?) to the monastics," we read in Prof. Diehl: "when one goes out from the monastery, he must in all things maintain fitting modesty—not speak, not raise his eyes, 'at the appearance of women and at other useless objects,' but always walk with prayer and with downcast eyes. If he does not observe these rules, he shall be removed from the community for fourteen days. Upon the hegumen lay the duty to encourage the observance of these severe rules and strictly to exact penalty for the slightest deviations. The most minute details of daily life must not escape him: the cook who burns firewood un-economically must receive a reprimand from him. But the compiler (evidently, of the testament?) did not limit himself to these simple prescriptions; for the authoritative hegumen of a rich monastery he traced out also higher duties. The hegumen lives simply, like his monastics; he does not wear luxurious clothing, does not gather gold, does not acquire personal property; when he goes out from the monastery, he does not ride mules or horses without necessity, but, after the example of Christ, walks on foot; he has servants at his disposal neither for himself nor for the monastery, remembering that man, created in the image of God, must not be enslaved. Especially must he care for the dignity of his monastery. 'Thou shalt not honor, beyond what is proper for the community, persons of noble rank and holders of power, and thou shalt not fear to sacrifice life unto the shedding of blood, in order to preserve the laws and commandments of God. With this magnificent and forceful rule conclude the rules of conduct (?) prescribed for the hegumen'" (pp. 185–186). Whence these "rules of conduct of the hegumen" were taken by Prof. Diehl, we do not know. One might assume that the Turin manuscript was complete when it was used by the said professor, but of this we are doubtful, because the following article, for which the title: *Diataxis of the Proskomide*, etc. (*«Διάταξις τῆς προσκομιδῆς» κ. τ. λ.*, p. 823) we borrowed from the above-indicated Barberini manuscript No. 69, has no beginning in the Turin codex and is not indicated by G. Pasini in his description of the manuscripts of the Turin Library (*Codices ms. biblioth. Taurinens.*, part I, p. 309), which has only the one following after it: *Concerning the Presanctified* (*«Περὶ τῶν προηγιασμένων»*). The omission noted in Pasini is possible only under the condition of the absence of the beginning of the article with the indicated title. One must therefore think that these "magnificent and forceful rules of conduct of the hegumen" were borrowed by Prof. Diehl from another codex that was more complete and in good preservation. Thus, for example, similar prescriptions concerning the conduct of monastics within the monastery and outside it can be seen in the MS of the Barberini Library III No. 102, fol. 146.

[^184]: As a specimen we provide a collation of the texts of one and the same article in both recensions. MS of the Turin Library No. 216 (p. 806): "Concerning Compline (*Περὶ τῶν ἀποδείπνων*). We perform Compline thus: beginning from Bright Saturday we chant the customary Six Psalms, and **'God is with us'** (*Μεθ’ ἡμῶν ὁ Θεός*), and the 50th [Psalm], and the triodion of the 50th day. At Compline, the triodia are chanted daily until All Saints, without making prostrations; but from the beginning of the Fast (*τεσσαρακοστή*) of All Saints [i.e., Apostles' Fast] we chant the Blameless [Psalm 118, *Ἄμωμος*] and **'Glory to God in the highest'** (*Δόξα ἐν ὑψίστοις Θεῷ*), and **'Lord Almighty'** (*Κύριε Παντοκράτορ*), and at the end of the first stasis 12 prostrations, if we do not have a feast during this Fast. We observe the same pattern also during the Fast of Saint Philip; and after the completion of the Fast of the Holy Apostles, [we chant] as far as **'Glory to God in the highest'**, **'Lord Almighty'**. On the 12 days [of the Nativity season] we resume again the order that we perform after Pascha until All Saints, saying after the canon, **'Glory to God in the highest'**, and the Trisagion. And we do the same also on all Sundays, that is, on Saturday evening, and Cheesefare Week, and all the Octaves [eight-day periods]. Then again after the Twelve Days and the Octaves of Theophany we resume the order [observed] from the Holy Apostles until the Holy Apostle Philip until Friday of Meatfare." — MS of the Vatican Library No. 1877, fols. 4v–5: "It is needful to know that we perform Compline thus: beginning from the second day (?) of Bright Week we chant the 3 psalms, namely **'The Lord is my light'** [Ps. 26], **'O God, [attend] unto my help'** [Ps. 69], then **'Have mercy on me, O God'** [Ps. 50], the canon, **'O God of compassions'**, the Trisagion, **'Our Father'**, the priest: **'For Thine is...'**, the apolytikion, and he dismisses. These things we chant thus until All Saints... But from the beginning of the Fast of the Holy Apostles we chant the customary 6 psalms first, as **'When I called'** [Ps. 4] and the rest, **'God is with us'**, the customary troparia of the Fasts, the canon, the Trisagion, then the 6 psalms from the twelve of Great Compline of Great Lent, **'Glory to God in the highest'**, the Trisagion, **'Our Father'**, **'O God of our fathers'**, **'We shall never be silent'**, **'Lord Almighty'**, and he dismisses. We chant the same Compline also during the Fast of Saint Philip, and at all other times likewise thus we chant these things, except only when we omit the 6 psalms and those after the canon and **'Lord Almighty'**."

[^185]: Prof. Diehl, on the basis of the Typikon of the Turin Library and other data, made the following conclusion concerning the monastery of St. Nicholas of Casole: *«jusqu’aux derniers jours de l’abbaye ils célébrèrent les fêtes de l’Église grecque»* [unto the last days of the abbey they celebrated the feasts of the Greek Church] (*Mélanges d’archéologie et d’histoire*, 1886, fasc. III–IV, p. 183).

[^186]: The order of the washing of feet was performed after the exclamation of the priest: **"For Thou art our sanctification..."** (*«Яко ты еси освященіе»*). During the chanting of the troparion: **"When the glorious disciples"** (*«Егда славніи»*), the priests with lamps and censer went out into the narthex of the church, where the order of washing was performed. The stichera of the washing were chanted on 6 with the refrains: **"Thou shalt sprinkle me with hyssop"** (*«Окропиши мя иссопомъ»* [Ps. 50:9]), **"Create in me a clean heart"** (*«Сердце чисто»* [Ps. 50:12]), and **"Cast me not away"** (*«Не отвержи мене»* [Ps. 50:13]), which were followed by the litany, the prayers: **"O Lord most good, Who art unapproachable"** (*«Κύριε ὑπεράγαθε, ὁ ἀπρόσιτος»*), and two at the bowing of heads: **"Master, Lord of glory, Who didst show us the measures of humility"** (*«Δέσποτα Κύριε τῆς δόξης, ὁ ὑποδείξας ἡμῖν μέτρα ταπεινώσεως»*) and **"O Lord our God, Who according to Thy abundant mercy didst empty Thyself"** (*«Κύριε ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν, ὁ κατὰ τὸ πολὺ ἔλεός σου κενώσας»*), and the Gospel from John for the washing of feet was read. The persons whose feet were washed were the following: *ἐν πρώτοις τὸν πυλωρόν, εἶτα ἀναγνώστην ἕνα καὶ διάκονον ἕνα, καὶ πρεσβυτέρους δ'. καὶ μοναχοὺς ἰδιώτας δ'. καὶ τὸν οἰκονόμον, καὶ ἔσχατον πάντων τὸν ἐκκλησιάρχην, ὁμοῦ ιβ'. κατὰ τὸν ἀριθμὸν τῶν ιβ'. ἀποστόλων* [first the gatekeeper, then one reader and one deacon, and 4 presbyters, and 4 simple monks, and the oikonomos, and last of all the ekklesiarches, in total 12, according to the number of the 12 apostles]. *᾿Απὸ τῶν ἐσχάτων ἕως τῶν πρώτων νίπτει οὖν τοὺς πόδας τῶν μαθητῶν καὶ ἐκμάσσει τῷ λεντίῳ καὶ σφραγίζει τῇ χειρὶ καὶ ἀσπάζεται αὐτούς* [From the last unto the first, therefore, he washes the feet of the disciples, and wipes them with the towel, and seals them with his hand, and kisses them]. The hegumen then read the second Gospel; the troparia were chanted: **"Ye call Me Lord, O disciples"** (*«Κύριον φωνεῖτε, ὦ μαθηταί»*), **"Having had your feet washed"** (*«Νιφθέντες πόδας»*), and **"Of great gifts"** (*«Μεγάλων χαρισμάτων»*), with the refrains: **"Thou shalt sprinkle me"**, **"Create in me a clean heart"**, and **"Glory... Both now..."**, whereupon all entered into the church, and the prayer behind the ambo was read. MS of the Turin Library No. 216, fols. 140–143; Barberini Library No. 69, fols. 58v–63; No. 102, fols. 121v–123.

[^205]: The full title of this book, in the highest degree curious and rare at present, is as follows: *«Νέον ᾿Ανθολόγιον πληρέστατόν τε καὶ ἀκριβέστατον εἰς τὸ τὰς νυχθημέρους κανονικὰς ὥρας τε καὶ δεήσεις ἀναγινώσκειν, συντεθὲν καὶ συλλεχθέν, κατὰ τὴν τάξιν τοῦ παλαιοῦ καὶ καθολικοῦ Τυπικοῦ τῆς παραδόσεως τῶν ἁγίων πατέρων· τοῦ τε ἁγίου Σάββα καὶ τοῦ Στουδίτου, καὶ τὸ πλεῖστον τοῦ ῾Αγίου Ὄρους, οὐ μὴν δ’ ἀλλὰ καὶ μέρος τῆς παραδόσεως τοῦ ὁσιωτάτου πατρὸς ἡμῶν Ἰωσὴφ τῆς μονῆς τοῦ ἁγίου Νικολάου τῶν Κασούλων. In Rome, 1598]. For our purposes (pp. 835–836) we made use of a copy belonging to the Barberini Library in Rome.

[^206]: In order to confirm our thought, we shall endeavor to indicate several examples of this kind of extracts by juxtaposing identical passages both in monuments that are undeniably Studite and in copies of the Western Typika of both recensions. Thus, for example, the absence of All-Night Vigils, agrypniai, and pannychides and the indication of several types of Compline, besides those known at present—the Small and Great—in both recensions of the Western Typika lead us to the thought that in this there was undoubtedly reflected upon the compilers of these Typika the influence of the practice of the Studite Monastery. In the *Hypotyposis* of Theodore the Studite and the *Diatyposis* of St. Athanasius of Athos we see the very same thing (p. 229, note). Eight and 10 troparia on the canon are appointed at Matins in the *Diatyposis* of St. Athanasius of Athos (p. 249). The chanted Hours on the first and Passion weeks of the Great Fast, according to the Typika of the second recension (pp. 870–872, 879), may be placed in parallel with the following remark of the *Hypotyposis*: **"After the brethren rest (that is, after Matins), before daybreak the ecclesiarch strikes the semantron, and when all gather into the church, we chant the First Hour with a kathisma, but without readings at it; and at the Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours we read. At each antiphon or 'Glory' prayer is offered by the priest and deacon"** (p. 231, note 7). The direction concerning the gathering of all the monks engaged in manual work to listen to the catechetical instructions of St. Theodore the Studite (p. 877) is similar to a certain degree to the rule of Theodore the Studite to read these instructions on certain days (p. 231, note 7; p. 237). In the Typika of the second recension, the remark concerning the changing of choirs at Vespers on the eve of Palm Sunday before the chanting of **"Lord, I have cried"** (p. 878), and in the Typika of the first recension concerning the reading by the ecclesiarch at the Third Hour of the proclamation: **"Brethren and fathers, let us be attentive to ourselves,"** etc., are almost verbatim identical with similar remarks in the *Hypotyposis* and *Diatyposis* (p. 237, p. 233 and note 1, p. 250). Other examples can likewise be indicated.

[^207]: *Tserkovnyi ustav (Tipik)* [The Church Typikon], pp. 76–79; cf. *Khristianskoe Chtenie* 1888, Nos. 9–10, pp. 510–522.

[^208]: *Ibid.*

[^209]: The superscription of this Triodion is as follows: **"Beginning, with God, of the Triodion of Holy Lent unto the Great Sunday; a composition of Joseph and Theodore"** (*«᾿Αρχὴ σὺν Θεῷ τοῦ Τριῳδίου τῆς ἁγίας τεσσαρακοστῆς μέχρι τῆς κυριακῆς μεγάλης, ποίημα Ἰωσὴφ καὶ Θεοδώρου»*).

[^210]: The title of the manuscript is as follows: **"Triodion, that is to say the Tripesnets, we begin with God; having the office completed from Palm Sunday unto All Saints; the composition of the two, Joseph and Theodore"** (*«Трїодь сирѣчь Трыпѣсньца съ Бг҃мь починнаемь. ѿ недѣ. цвѣтноє до всѣх ст҃ыхъ имѣюща слоужьбоу исполненꙋ. творенїе двою. Иосифа и Ѳеѡдора»*).

[^211]: The manuscript is superscribed thus: **"Here we begin the Triodion of the soul-saving Fast; Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee; the composition of Theodore and of Joseph. For the Sunday in which is read the Gospel of the publican** (*τοῦ τελώνου*) **and the Pharisee"** (*«Сѣмь починнаемь Трїѡдь дш҃еспнаго поста. н҃ед ѡ мытари и о фарисеи. творенїе Ѳеѡдора. и Иосифово. Къ недѣлѣ, внаже чтетсѧ е҃ѵ ѡ делоноу [τοῦ τελώνου] и фарисею»*). The Orbel Triodion in philological respect is described in *Otchet Imperatorskoi publichnoi biblioteki za 1891 god* [Report of the Imperial Public Library for the Year 1891], St. Petersburg 1894, pp. 25–27; but this manuscript deserves a very detailed description and study on the part of liturgists. This is for now the only monument known to us of the practice of the Studite rule among the Bulgarians as well.

[^218]: We beg pardon of our readers for the following typographical errors frequently repeated in the book, which are difficult now even to correct, namely: *ἐκτενῆν* instead of *ἐκτενὴν*, *ἐκτενοῦς* instead of *ἐκτενῆς*, *γηρωκομεῖον* instead of *γηροκομεῖον*, *῾Υιὸς* instead of *Υἱὸς*, *Ἄσατε* instead of *ᾌσατε*, *κᾀκεῖ* and *κᾀκεῖσε* instead of *κἀκεῖ*, *κἀκεῖσε*, *ἐξαγίου,* instead of *ἐξαγίου*, *ἦχος* instead of *ἧχος*, *εἶτα* instead of *εἶτα*, *οἷα* (feminine gender) instead of *οἵα*, etc., and for punctuation not fully maintained, especially in the first eight gatherings [sheets] of the book.
